  [G1.8] Data mismatches and unsupported figures within the report itself
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report contains four identified internal inconsistencies: (1) Quilter platform charges stated as 0.27% in the existing arrangements table and as 0.27% in the replacement analysis narrative (both values match); (2) Nest charges shown as Plan Charges 0.30% and Contribution charge 1.8% in the existing arrangements table, but only 0.30% used in the replacement analysis (£21,272.64 × 0.30% = £63.82), with the 1.8% contribution charge not integrated into the switching analysis; (3) True Potential charges listed as Platform 0.40%, Ongoing Advice 0.50%, and Fund Management 0.58% totalling 1.48% in existing arrangements, but shown as 0.98% current annual recurring charge in replacement analysis (0.40% + 0.58% = 0.98%, excluding the 0.50% ongoing advice charge); (4) Client objectives state desire to retire at 60 with £25,000–£30,000 annual income and preference for flexible income, paired with a Growth-oriented risk profile of 7/10, which appears misaligned with the modest income target, though cashflow analysis shows assets sufficient even under stress scenarios.
    File:     No external file comparison applies to this requirement; the assessment is internal to the suitability report itself. The extraction report's own analysis identifies these internal inconsistencies within the canonical model.
    Discrepancies:
      - Nest contribution charge treatment: existing arrangements table lists 0.30% Plan Charges and 1.8% Contribution charge as separate line items, totalling 2.1% recurring charges; replacement analysis uses only 0.30% as 'Current Annual Recurring Charge' in the pension switch comparison, excluding the 1.8% contribution charge from the switching cost assessment.
      - True Potential charge reconciliation: existing arrangements table shows 0.40% (Platform) + 0.50% (Ongoing Advice) + 0.58% (Fund Management) = 1.48% total; replacement analysis shows 0.98% as 'Current Annual Recurring Charge', which equals 0.40% + 0.58%, omitting the 0.50% ongoing advice charge from the 'recurring' classification.
      - Objectives and risk profile alignment: report states objectives of retiring at age 60 with annual income requirement of £25,000–£30,000 and explicit preference for 'flexible income over guaranteed income'; risk assessment assigns Growth profile (7/10); the tension between a growth-oriented profile and modest income objectives is not explicitly reconciled in the narrative, though cashflow modelling shows surplus under stress scenarios.
      - Quilter charge phrasing: existing arrangements states 0.27% as a factual charge; replacement analysis uses conditional phrasing 'If you were to retain the pension with Quilter the platform charges would be 0.27%' — this is a difference in tone and certainty rather than a numerical contradiction, as both figures are 0.27%.
    Three of the four identified inconsistencies represent material charge treatment differences. (1) On Quilter: both figures state 0.27%; the difference is phrasing (conditional vs. factual), not a numerical discrepancy. This does not constitute a data mismatch on arithmetic grounds. (2) On Nest: the existing arrangements table lists two separate charge components (Plan Charges 0.30% and Contribution charge 1.8%), totalling 2.1% of recurring charges. The replacement analysis isolates only the 0.30% Plan Charge figure and calculates £63.82 annual cost (£21,272.64 × 0.30% = £63.82), which arithmetically reconciles. However, the 1.8% contribution charge is not explained as excluded from the recurring charge assessment; whether this is appropriate depends on whether the contribution charge is one-time or ongoing, and this basis is not stated in the report. This is a data inconsistency because two different charge totals (2.1% vs. 0.30%) are used in different parts of the report without explaining which basis applies to which analysis. (3) On True Potential: existing arrangements lists 0.40% + 0.50% + 0.58% = 1.48%. Replacement analysis shows 0.98%, which equals 0.40% + 0.58%, omitting the 0.50% ongoing advice charge. The arithmetic is correct for the 0.98% figure if the ongoing advice charge is excluded, but the report does not explain why it is excluded (e.g., whether it will be replaced, is not recurring, or is an error). This is a data inconsistency because two different totals (1.48% vs. 0.98%) are presented without an explicit basis for the difference. (4) On objectives and risk: the report articulates a Growth risk profile (7/10) alongside modest income objectives (£25,000–£30,000 annual) and a stated preference for flexible income. Cashflow modelling demonstrates sufficiency under stress scenarios. The apparent tension is not explained in narrative. However, this is not an arithmetic inconsistency — it is a potential conceptual misalignment between stated profile and stated objectives, which falls within the scope of CHK-003 (risk alignment) and CHK-004 (capacity for loss) rather than CHK-001 (data completeness and consistency). The extracted report itself flags this as a possible finding for CHK-003, so it is acknowledged but outside this requirement's scope. On charge discrepancies: the derived figures computed from the canonical model confirm that Nest's £63.82 annual charge (at 0.30%) and True Potential's implied 0.98% figure are arithmetically sound in isolation. The inconsistency is not one of calculation error but of incomplete disclosure of the basis for excluding components (Nest's 1.8% contribution charge, True Potential's 0.50% ongoing advice charge) from the switching analysis.

  [G1.10] Values expressed in different frequencies or formats reconcile (weekly/monthly/annual, gross/net, percentages and totals)
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts three income figures at different frequencies: (1) Annual gross earnings of £45,000 (Annually); (2) Full State Pension of £230.21 per week (Weekly); (3) Tony's net contribution of £600 per month (Monthly).       - State Pension weekly amount of £230.21 (annualises to £11,970.92) has no direct corroboration in case file; nearest figure in file is Council Tax of £221.00 in [P1], which is expenditure not income
      - Monthly household expenditure stated as £2,986 in report; this matches a derived sum from expenditure categories in [P1] but the relationship to the £2,237 modelling figure is unexplained

    The partner's contribution discrepancy (£600 vs £800 monthly) is a material inconsistency between the report's assertion and the Fact Find evidence. The report states £600 net monthly in the 'Your current situation' section [I passage] but [P1] records £800.00. This is not a basis or frequency conversion: both are stated monthly, both are the same liability account row, yet the amounts differ by £200 (33% variance). The guard on gross/net basis is applied but does not account for the £200 discrepancy on partner's contribution, which has the same stated basis (net monthly) on both sides. No calculation bridge is provided in the report to reconcile the two expenditure figures in the same modelling document.

  [G1.11] Facts used in calculations, cashflow modelling, projections and assumptions match the facts stated in the report narrative
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts cashflow modelling was performed with the following key assumptions: Annual earnings £45,000 gross until retirement at age 60; Monthly personal expenditure £2,237 until retirement and £2,000 net in retirement; One-off sofa expenditure £2,500; Mortgage payments £750 per month for 7 years; Inheritance receipt of £350,000 next year; Investment growth 6.00% per annum before charges; State Pension at age 67; General expenditure inflation 2%; Savings inflation 2%; State pensions inflation 2.5%. The baseline scenario projects to age 100 with no shortfall. Stress tests include high inflation (6%) showing asset depletion at age 81, and market crash (2007-2008 level) showing no shortfall. Life expectancy benchmark (ONS age 87) shows no shortfall.
    File:     Supporting documents corroborate: £45,000 earnings [P19], retirement age 60 [P16], £350,000 inheritance [P19], State Pension age 67 [P19], general inflation 2% [P15], and retirement age 87 (ONS life expectancy) [P20]. However, the actual cashflow modelling output documents [P1-P5], [P12-P13] show projected fund values at age 87 under different growth scenarios (0%, 2.94%, 5.88%) but do not disclose the assumed monthly expenditure figures (£2,237 or £2,000), mortgage payment (£750), sofa expense (£2,500), or the 6.00% growth assumption used in narrative. The narrative states monthly expenditure is £2,237 [P19 states total household £2,986 with Tony contributing £600, implying client's share approximately £2,386, not £2,237]. The narrative also states household expenditure totals £2,986 with partner contributing £600 [P19], creating ambiguity about which figure (£2,986, £2,386, or £2,237) represents the client's actual personal expenditure used in cashflow modelling.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states personal monthly expenditure £2,237 for cashflow modelling [Suitability report section 15] but also states total household expenditure £2,986 with partner contribution £600 [P19], implying client share £2,386, a difference of £149 (6.3%)
      - Report narrative states 6.00% investment growth assumption [page 15, Cashflow Analysis] but modelling documents show all illustrations use growth rates of 0%, 2.94%, and 5.88% — 6.00% does not appear in any modelling output
      - Report does not disclose in the financial position narrative the assumed 6% inflation stress test rate or provide basis for age 81 asset depletion outcome
      - Report assumes mortgage payment £750 per month for 7 years but this figure does not appear in supporting evidence and conflicts with stated monthly expenditure of £2,237 if client is making both payments
      - Report states one-off sofa expense £2,500 [page 15, Cashflow Analysis quote] but this figure does not appear in any supporting evidence or financial position document
    The report asserts that cashflow modelling was performed (wasCashflowModelled = true) and relies on this modelling to support the recommendation. However, a material ambiguity exists about the client's personal monthly expenditure figure used in the model. The report states in the Cashflow Analysis section that 'Your expenditure is £2,237 per month until retirement' but separately states 'Your monthly expenditure totals £2,986 and Tony contributes £600 per month towards the household bills' [P19]. This creates three possible interpretations: (1) client's personal share is £2,386 (£2,986 - £600), approximating to £2,237; (2) £2,237 is an adjusted forecast used for modelling; (3) one is gross household and one is net. The extraction ambiguity document flags this unresolved: the relationship between these figures is not clarified in the report. The 6.00% growth assumption stated in narrative does not match any modelling output, which uses only 0%, 2.94%, and 5.88% inflation-adjusted rates [P1, P4, P12-P13]. This is consistent with FCA prescribed illustration rates and the guard that illustration growth rates differ from cashflow assumptions, but the report does not explain the basis for the 6.00% figure or confirm it was used in a separate non-illustrated cashflow model. The stress test scenarios (age 81 for 6% inflation, no shortfall for 2007-2008 crash, age 87 for ONS benchmark) are asserted but the underlying model data is not in the file. Specific assumptions like £2,500 sofa purchase and £750 monthly mortgage payments are disclosed in narrative but have no supporting documentation. Where the report quotes modelled figures (scenarios with specific outcomes) without providing the modelling output, the claim is unsupported by evidence in the file. The facts stated in the narrative (earnings £45,000, inheritance £350,000, retirement age 60, State Pension age 67) are corroborated [P19]. General inflation 2% is corroborated [P15]. However, the personal expenditure figure used in cashflow, the growth rate assumption, and specific expense items cannot be fully reconciled.

CHK-002 — Objectives, Needs and Time-Horizon Alignment
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G2.1, G2.2, G2.3, G2.4, G2.5, G2.6, G2.7, G2.8). Report states inheritance of £350,000 from mother's property sale; meeting evidence [P6] states approximately £150,000 from mother's estate with legal complications. Report asserts client will retire at age 60; meeting notes indicate client at 55 (as of 21/07/2026) contemplating transitional phase and planning return to part-time work in January, with no specific retirement age of 60 documented in evidence. Report does not disclose that inheritance receipt is contingent on legal proceedings outcome and potential £20,000 contribution by client to resolve ex-partner dispute; meeting notes [P6] indicate this uncertainty. 7 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G2.1] Stated goals and their priority order
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report states 8 client objectives in priority order: (1) Receive and manage Pension Sharing Order from ex-husband's Quilter pension (~£260,000); (1) Retire or reduce work at age 60 with annual income £25,000–£30,000; (2) Consolidate existing dormant pensions (Nest and True Potential); (2) Preference for flexible income over guaranteed income; (3) Ensure pension funds perform well to build retirement savings; (3) Navigate inheritance of £350,000 from mother's property sale; (4) Reduce Inheritance Tax liability; (5) Appoint adult daughters as Power of Attorney. The report details that the client is aged 55, has investment horizon of 5–10 years, will retire at 60, seeks flexible drawdown not annuities, and expects to receive £350,000 inheritance. Cashflow modelling confirms assets sufficient to meet £25k–£30k income target through retirement to age 100, even under stress scenarios (6% inflation, 2007/8 market crash). Risk profile assessed as 7/10 (Growth).
    File:     Meeting summaries from 16/01/2026, 20/02/2026, 18/03/2026, and 21/07/2026 document stated client objectives. Evidence shows: client managing pension transfer post-divorce from ex-husband Paul Annis [P2]; objective to consolidate Nest and True Potential pensions for better management [P8]; retirement objective with flexible income strategy [P8]; interest in flexi-access drawdown and annuities [P8]. Meeting on 21/07/2026 [P5], [P6] records client at age 55, recently left employment (earning £45,000 annually, £2,900 net/month), contemplating pension access to support transitional phase, planning return to part-time work January 2027 at £20,000 salary, intending to remain in part-time work until retirement. Inheritance discussed as approximately £150,000 from mother's estate (not £350,000) currently complicated by legal challenges; Melanie and sister may need to contribute £20,000 each to resolve legal battle [P6]. Client prefers financial buffer covering 12 months expenses [P6]. Estate planning mentioned with daughters appointed as Power of Attorney [P8]. No evidence of stated retirement age 60 in meeting notes; 21/07/2026 meeting discusses her being at 55 and transitional phase but does not lock retirement at 60. No explicit mention of £25,000–£30,000 annual income target in meeting summaries. Investment horizon stated as 5–10 years [P4].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states inheritance of £350,000 from mother's property sale; meeting evidence [P6] states approximately £150,000 from mother's estate with legal complications
      - Report asserts client will retire at age 60; meeting notes indicate client at 55 (as of 21/07/2026) contemplating transitional phase and planning return to part-time work in January, with no specific retirement age of 60 documented in evidence
      - Report does not disclose that inheritance receipt is contingent on legal proceedings outcome and potential £20,000 contribution by client to resolve ex-partner dispute; meeting notes [P6] indicate this uncertainty
      - Report states annual income target of £25,000–£30,000 in retirement; no meeting summary explicitly corroborates this specific income figure
      - Report presents retirement income objective as priority 1 alongside pension sharing; meeting evidence suggests focus is currently on transitional phase and maintaining part-time work until retirement rather than single retirement target at 60
    The report asserts 8 stated objectives with priority ranks. Evidence from meeting summaries [P2], [P7], [P8], [P5], [P6] corroborates most objectives at substance level: (1) pension sharing order management and consolidation of pensions are documented [P2], [P7], [P8]; (2) preference for flexible income over annuities is documented [P8]; (3) fund performance objective is implied in consolidation rationale; (5) Power of Attorney with daughters is documented [P8]. However, three material discrepancies exist: (i) Inheritance quantum: the report asserts £350,000 from mother's property sale; meeting evidence [P6] states 'approximately £150,000' from mother's estate, a difference of £200,000 (57% overstatement). The 21/07/2026 meeting also documents that the legal status is uncertain: 'currently complicated by legal challenges involving her mother's ex-partner' and 'Melanie and her sister may need to contribute £20,000 each to resolve the situation'. The report presents the £350,000 as a planned receipt without disclosing the legal contingency, represented instead as a certain future input to cashflow modelling [P17]. (ii) Retirement age: The report prioritises 'retire or reduce work hours at age 60' as a priority-1 objective. Meeting evidence [P5], [P6] records that as of 21/07/2026, Melanie is 55, recently departed employment, and is 'contemplating accessing her pension benefits to support herself financially during a transitional phase, with plans to return to work potentially in January'. [P6] documents her intention to 'return to part-time work in January, with an estimated salary of £20,000' and 'plans to remain in part-time work until retirement'. No meeting summary locks retirement at age 60; the evidence shows a transitional phase and future part-time work scenario, not a fixed age-60 retirement objective. The report's priority ranking treats age-60 retirement and the £25,000–£30,000 income target as equally priority-ranked objectives, but the meeting evidence suggests the immediate concern is managing the transitional phase (now, at 55) and re-entering part-time work. (iii) Income target: The £25,000–£30,000 annual retirement income figure is stated in the report objectives and underpins the cashflow modelling [P17], but no meeting summary explicitly records the client confirming this specific income target. The evidence documents modelled income analysis but not client statement of this as a stated objective. The extraction metadata notes an ambiguity: the report states two different monthly expenditure figures (£2,986 household total with partner's £600 contribution, and £2,237 personal expenditure in modelling), and it is unclear whether these reconcile to a consistent baseline for the income target. The priority ordering in the report also contains an inconsistency: objectives are ranked 1, 2, 3, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, which means pension-sharing and retirement income both carry priority rank 1, but the evidence does not establish that client explicitly prioritised these as equal. The guard that 'advisers paraphrase' applies: the substance of consolidation, flexibility, and estate planning are evidenced. However, the retirement age of 60 and the £350,000 inheritance figure are material inputs to the suitability recommendation (they determine the time horizon for growth and the quantum of assets available), and these are either unsupported or contradicted by the evidence. The inheritance in particular is used in the cashflow model as a certain future input [P17: 'green is the inheritance which we assumed you receive in two years' time'], but the meeting evidence discloses material uncertainty about both the amount and receipt. This is a disclosure shortfall: the client objectives as stated do not acknowledge the contingency. The cashflow stress tests [P17] refer to the inheritance being modelled as received, supporting asset sufficiency, but the meeting notes indicate this is far from certain. Vulnerability overlay: The client is navigating post-divorce financial transition at 55, with recent employment departure, and is managing a complex family inheritance involving legal proceedings. The meeting evidence [P5], [P6] documents that Morgan Seddon explicitly recognised the need for a financial buffer (3–6 months minimum, client preferred 12 months) and factored inheritance uncertainty into the plan. However, the report does not disclose the contingency or the client's heightened support needs during this transition in its statement of objectives. The objectives are stated as definite, not conditional.

  [G2.2] Retirement target date and investment time horizon details
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts a client retirement target age of 60 with an investment term of 5 years. The report states multiple time horizons across objectives: LongTerm (6 instances), MediumTerm (1 instance), and ShortTerm (1 instance). Life expectancy is stated as age 87 based on Office of National Statistics data for a 55-year-old female. The report projects funds to last throughout the client's lifetime based on this benchmark.
    File:     Evidence corroborates several key figures. The meeting summary [P9] confirms the client is 55 years old and is 'considering accessing her pension benefits' and 'with plans to return to work potentially in January.' The meeting notes [P9][P10] indicate she plans to work part-time until retirement, balancing responsibilities as a grandmother. The Office of National Statistics data [P11] confirms life expectancy for a 55-year-old female is 87 years. The Fact Find [P17] lists a potential inheritance of £150,000 estimated 'in 2-3 years time, maybe sooner.' The meeting summary [P9] notes she departed employment recently with a prior annual income of approximately £45,000. Fund documentation [P1][P2][P3][P4] and investment literature [P5][P6] state minimum recommended holding periods of 5 years. The risk profile report [P15] documents the process of reaching an agreed risk level through discussion based on 'natural risk level, investment amount, target and desired time horizon.'
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts a single retirement target age of 60, but the meeting summary [P9] indicates the client is currently 55 and considering 'returning to work potentially in January' (of what year is unclear from context), with plans to 'remain in part-time work until retirement' [P10]. This suggests a more complex timeline than a single target age of 60 would capture.
      - The report states multiple disparate time horizons (LongTerm, MediumTerm, ShortTerm) across objectives without explicit hierarchy or reconciliation to the stated 5-year term. The classification of 8 objectives across these three categories lacks clear justification.
      - The 5-year term appears in fund documentation [P1][P2][P3][P4] and [P5][P6] as a minimum recommended holding period, but the report does not clarify whether this 5-year term is the client's investment horizon, the fund's minimum holding period, or both, or how it reconciles with a retirement age of 60 when the client is currently 55.
      - No explicit evidence links the stated objectives to specific time horizons in client-authored documentation. The meeting summary [P9][P10] does not contain a prioritised, client-endorsed list of objectives with stated time horizons to cross-check against the report's assertion of multiple time horizons.
    The comparison can be performed because key dates and figures are present in the file. The retirement target age of 60 is corroborated by the report's own statement [P12] and matches the client's stated retirement goal. The life expectancy of 87 is corroborated by the Office of National Statistics data [P11]. However, material tensions exist that the report does not resolve. First, the report asserts a 5-year investment term, but the meeting evidence [P9][P10] reveals the client is in a transitional phase—recently departed from employment at age 55, considering pension access now, planning to return to part-time work in January, and intending to remain in part-time work until retirement. This timeline is substantially more complex and nearer-term than a simple 5-year horizon to age 60 would suggest. The meeting notes do not show the client endorsing a 5-year term; they show discussion of pension options 'to support herself financially during a transitional phase.' Second, the report classifies 8 objectives across three time horizons (LongTerm ×6, MediumTerm ×1, ShortTerm ×1) without citing a source document where the client stated these objectives or their time horizons. The meeting summary [P9][P10] discusses financial planning, pension access, debt repayment (credit card £7,000, personal loan £13,000), a £20,000 cash buffer, potential inheritance, and future part-time work—but does not present these as a ranked list of 8 distinct objectives with assigned horizons. The report does not cite evidence for how it derived this structure. Third, the extraction notes an internal inconsistency within the report itself: a risk profile of 7 (Growth) appears misaligned with 'modest income target (£25k–£30k on projected assets)' given that 'the high risk profile appears suited to capital accumulation rather than the stated income objectives' [relatedCheckIds CHK-001, CHK-003]. This suggests the report's own objectives and time horizons may not have been fully reconciled with the risk profile agreed through the process documented in [P15]. The fund documentation [P1][P2][P3][P4][P5][P6] states a 5-year minimum recommended holding period, but these are generic fund descriptors, not evidence that the client's own investment horizon is 5 years. The 5-year term appears in the report's /objectives/termYears field but is not supported by explicit evidence of the client endorsing this as her investment horizon, distinct from or in addition to her retirement target age of 60.

  [G2.3] Access requirements — emergency, planned and ad-hoc
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts two access requirements: (1) Planned retirement access at age 60 (required from 2031-03-24) with an annual income target of £25,000–£30,000 net. (2) Regular income drawdown in retirement via Flexi-Access Drawdown on the Aviva Platform. The report also states an emergency fund of £17,000 in cash holdings, confirmed as sufficient because the client 'have liquid cash should you require instance access funds'. The report states the client 'understand the pension freedoms and prefer the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown when you access your pension'.
    File:     The case file confirms multiple aspects of these access requirements through meeting summaries and fact find documents. [P3] and [P4] record that Melanie is in a transitional phase at age 55, considering pension access, and discussing flexible drawdown and lump sum withdrawal options. [P4] states Morgan 'emphasised the importance of having a buffer of three to six months of expenses, though Melanie preferred a larger buffer, ideally covering 12 months.' [P5] records approximately £17,000 in savings with planned use of £2,500 to pay off credit card debt, leaving approximately £14,500 available as emergency buffer. [P6] confirms Melanie's 'keen interest in the flexibility offered by the drawdown option' and notes she 'remain[s] open to exploring other options, such as annuities'. [P11] records 'Your investment horizon is five to ten years'. [P17] from the Fact Find documents: 'You plan to retire at age 60', 'You intend to draw an annual pension of £25,000 to £30,000 until your state pension starts at age 67', and 'You are exploring flexible pension options for your retirement as this was your preference over guaranteed income'. No document records a formal assessment that £17,000 (or £14,500 after planned debt repayment) meets the stated preference for a 12-month buffer, nor does the evidence confirm the adequacy of this amount against the stated monthly expenditure figures.
    Discrepancies:
      - Emergency fund basis discrepancy: Report infers £17,000 held in cash as emergency fund (from category B). Evidence shows £17,000 in savings with £2,500 earmarked for credit card debt repayment [P5], leaving net available liquidity of approximately £14,500, not the full £17,000.
      - Emergency fund adequacy standard mismatch: Report states client 'comfortable' with £17,000 as emergency fund based on having 'liquid cash'. [P4] records that Morgan 'emphasised the importance of having a buffer of three to six months of expenses' while 'Melanie preferred a larger buffer, ideally covering 12 months'. No evidence reconciles the stated monthly expenditure (£2,836 per fact find [P13], or £2,237 per report modelling as noted in extraction ambiguity) against the held emergency fund to confirm whether £17,000 meets even the three to six month standard, much less Melanie's stated 12-month preference.
      - Investment horizon ambiguity: Report does not explicitly state a time horizon for the recommended Aviva pension investment. [P11] records 'Your investment horizon is five to ten years', but this appears to relate to a different context (possibly the overall strategy before retirement at 60). The term for the Aviva pension recommendation itself—from age 55 now (2026) to age 60 (2031)—is five years until retirement access begins, not five to ten years.
    The report's treatment of access requirements is substantially supported by the evidence, but with two material gaps. First, the emergency fund assessment lacks rigour. The report asserts £17,000 is adequate and that the client is comfortable with it because she 'have liquid cash', citing [P2]. However, [P5] shows £2,500 is committed to debt repayment, reducing net available emergency liquidity to £14,500. More importantly, [P4] records that the adviser Morgan discussed and Melanie actively preferred a 12-month expense buffer, yet no document on file quantifies whether £14,500–£17,000 achieves this. The stated monthly expenditure in the Fact Find [P13] is £2,836.23, which would require £34,034–£28,448 for 12 months; the report's referenced figure of £2,237 (per extraction note) would require £26,844–£13,422 for 12–6 months. Either way, the held emergency fund falls short of Melanie's stated 12-month preference and potentially falls short of even a conservative three-month standard if the higher expenditure figure applies. The report asserts adequacy without this comparison. Second, the time horizon for the Aviva pension investment is not explicitly stated in the report recommendation section. [P11] records a five to ten year horizon, but this may be a general planning horizon rather than the specific term applicable to the pension vehicle being recommended. The actual investment term to retirement at age 60 is five years from now (2026–2031), aligning with the lower end of the range noted, but this is not confirmed in the report itself. Third, [P6] records that Melanie remains open to annuities, yet the report presents drawdown as the sole recommended access structure without documenting consideration of why guaranteed income was rejected despite the client's openness to it. This is internal consistency (objectives vs. recommendation) rather than a comparison failure, as the report's preference statement and the drawdown recommendation align; however, the evidence of client openness to other options is not addressed in the report's justification. The planned retirement date (age 60, 2031), the annual income target (£25,000–£30,000), and the preference for flexible drawdown are all corroborated by evidence. The emergency fund holding and its adequacy are only partially supported.

  [G2.4] Foreseeable life changes
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts six foreseeable life changes: (1) Employment status change from employed to self-employed on 2026-03-24 with £0 financial impact stated; (2) Loss of employee pension contributions and Nest plan becomes paid up on 2026-03-24; (3) Planned retirement on 2031-03-24 with financial impact of -£45,000 gross; (4) Expected receipt of inheritance from sale of late mother's property on 2027-03-24 of £350,000; (5) State Pension eligibility on 2034-03-24 of £11,970.92 annually (£230.21 per week × 52); (6) Inheritance Tax implications from 6 April 2027 when unused defined contribution pensions become part of taxable estate. The report states the client is aged 55, recently divorced, expects annual earnings around £45,000 gross going forward as self-employed, hopes to retire at age 60 maintaining income of £25,000–£30,000 per annum, and is expecting inheritance of £350,000 from late mother's property pending legal proceedings.
    File:     The Fact Find [P10] and [P13] support employment status change to self-employed with expected annual earnings of around £45,000 gross. [P13] confirms the client is aged 55, recently divorced, and states 'You will no longer be entitled to employee benefits such as pension contributions and your Nest plan will become paid up.' [P13] confirms retirement goal: 'You are hoping to retire at age 60' and income target 'maintaining an income of between £25,000 and £30,000 per annum.' [P13] confirms inheritance: 'You are also expecting to receive an inheritance of £350,000 from the sale of your late mothers property which is currently pending legal proceedings.' [P18] states 'Your investment horizon is five to ten years.' [P7] addresses IHT change: 'From 6 April 2027, most unused defined contribution pensions and death benefits (such as drawdown funds and uncrystallised pots) will be included in your taxable estate for IHT.' State pension information is referenced in [P3] action item 'You will send the state pension forecast screenshot' and [P4] shows 'State pension 99 years' (age reference unclear but contextually likely age 67 based on [P20] which states 'Once her state pension kicks in at age 67'). [P20] confirms 'Once her state pension kicks in at age 67, half of her retirement expenditure will be covered by this.' The Certified Final Order [P6] shows divorce completion date '1S/04/2026'.
    Discrepancies:
      - Financial impact of employment status change stated as £0 in report but no supporting evidence or calculation provided for this figure
      - Retirement date calculated as 2031-03-24 (age 60 from DOB 15/12/1970) but investment horizon stated in [P18] as 'five to ten years' which from 2026 would extend to 2031-2036, creating ambiguity about the actual retirement date
      - State Pension eligibility date stated as 2034-03-24 (age 67) appears correct but the £11,970.92 annual amount (£230.21 per week × 52) is not independently verified in the evidence; [P20] refers to state pension at age 67 but does not quote the specific weekly amount
      - Inheritance date stated as 2027-03-24 but [P13] states it is 'currently pending legal proceedings' with no definitive date confirmed
    The report asserts six foreseeable life changes. Five of these are supported by evidence in the file: (1) Employment status change to self-employed is confirmed in [P13] and supported by [P10]; (2) Loss of employee benefits and Nest plan becoming paid up is explicitly stated in [P13]; (3) Retirement at age 60 is confirmed in [P13] with target income of £25,000–£30,000; (4) Inheritance of £350,000 is confirmed in [P13] though with caveat of pending legal proceedings; (5) IHT rule change from 6 April 2027 is stated in [P7] and is legislative, not client-specific, so per the guard ('Legislative changes affecting everyone are not client-specific foreseeable changes') this should not be counted as a foreseeable personal life change. The State Pension eligibility is placed at age 67 in [P20] which aligns with the 2034-03-24 date calculated from DOB 15/12/1970, but the specific weekly rate of £230.21 is not evidenced in any passage; it is used only in the report's extraction note as a derivation. The employment status change lists £0 financial impact with basis 'Unspecified' — the report does not explain why changing from employed to self-employed with loss of £600/month employer contribution (per [P10] partner contribution context) has zero impact, nor does evidence support this quantification. The retirement date of 2031-03-24 (age 60) is internally consistent with DOB and stated retirement age, but [P18] states 'Your investment horizon is five to ten years' which, read from 2026, would extend to 2031–2036; no explanation reconciles the five-year minimum horizon with a five-year plan to retirement (2026 to 2031). The inheritance is stated as pending legal proceedings in [P13], making the specific 2027-03-24 date uncertain. The IHT change is legislative and affects all pension holders, not a personal foreseeable change specific to this client's circumstances. Overall, the majority of foreseeable changes are supported, but quantifications for employment financial impact lack support, the State Pension figure lacks evidence of the specific amount, and the inheritance date is contingent on legal resolution.

  [G2.5] Conflicting goals
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts eight objectives with priority ranks 1–5 distributed across them. OBJ1 (priority 1): consolidate pension pots from Quilter divorce settlement (£260,000). OBJ2 (priority 2): transfer True Potential and Nest pensions (£67,945.44 combined). OBJ3 (priority 3): receive inheritance of £350,000 from mother's property sale, pending legal proceedings. OBJ4 (priority 1, shared): retire at age 60. OBJ5 (priority 2, shared): achieve annual retirement income of £25,000–£30,000 with preference for 'flexible income over guaranteed income'. OBJ6 (priority 3): optimise tax efficiency. OBJ7 (priority 4): clear mortgage and consumer debts (£20,000 cash buffer). OBJ8 (priority 5): maintain investment flexibility and de-risk as retirement approaches. The report states risk profile 7/10 (Growth), capacity for loss 'moderate'. Cashflow analysis confirms assets sufficient to cover retirement expenditure even under stress scenarios (6% inflation, 2007/8-style market crash). Client is aged 55, currently self-employed at £45,000 gross annual earnings, expects to work part-time to age 60. Monthly expenditure £2,986 with partner Tony contributing £600.
    File:     Meeting evidence [P4] confirms client is seasoned professional with accounting and currency trading background, currently employed at Bridgeport Interiors Limited earning £42,000 annually (later noted self-employed, £45,000 gross [P13]). [P5] confirms objectives include consolidating pension pots, exploring tax-efficient strategies, understanding flexi-access drawdown and annuities, and developing comprehensive retirement strategy aligned with risk profile. [P12] confirms flexibility interest and willingness to adjust risk levels approaching retirement. [P9] records inheritance as approximately £150,000 (not £350,000), complicated by legal challenges; Melanie and sister may need to contribute £20,000 each to resolve, with buffer needed for 12 months expenses; discusses tax efficiency using personal allowance (£12,570, with ~£8,500 available after £4,000 employment income). [P9] confirms intention to return to part-time work in January at estimated £20,000 salary, remain part-time until retirement, balance grandmother responsibilities. [P13] confirms divorced, lives with partner Tony, separate financial arrangements, two adult daughters integral to estate planning, intended as Power of Attorney. [P6] records Morgan Seddon actions to investigate best route for financial strategy, document advice, process disinvestment within four to six weeks. No evidence found of explicit discussion or documentation of eight prioritised objectives, risk profile discussion outcome, or structured cashflow analysis in meeting summaries.
    Discrepancies:
      - Inheritance amount: report states £350,000; evidence states £150,000 ([P9], [P13] references 'sale of your late mothers property').
      - Inheritance certainty: report treats £350,000 as objective target amount 'Actual' modality; evidence describes £150,000 as 'anticipating' with legal complications, potential £20,000 contribution required by Melanie ([P9]).
      - Retirement income target: report specifies £25,000–£30,000 annual income with flexible drawdown preference; no evidence in file confirms client articulated this specific income range or drawdown preference in structured form.
      - Objectives structure: report lists eight objectives with specific priority ranks (1–5 distributed); evidence discusses consolidation, tax efficiency, flexibility, and retirement timing but does not confirm eight separate ranked objectives or explicit priority ordering.
      - Employment income: report states client 'recently altered employment status, going from employed to self-employed with the same company. You expect annual earnings to be around £45,000 gross going forward' ([P13]); [P4] states 'career as an accountant at Bridgeport Interiors Limited, where you earn an annual salary of £42,000'; [P9] mentions 'return to part-time work in January with estimated salary of £20,000' — three different salary figures.
      - Risk profile: report states risk profile 7/10 agreed and discussed; [P7] confirms questionnaire completed and agreed as 7/10 but does not show structured risk profiling discussion output or capacity for loss assessment detail in meeting evidence.
      - Cashflow analysis: report asserts 'Cashflow analysis to assess whether you will have sufficient funds for retirement' and 'Based on your circumstances alone, your pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement, even following a fall in the market' ([P7]); no standalone cashflow analysis document retrieved in evidence pack; [P9] mentions Morgan 'factor[ing] this potential inheritance into Melanie's cash flow plan' but no detailed modelling output found.
    The comparison can be performed on core facts but reveals material discrepancies on amounts and clarity. The £350,000 inheritance is the most significant numerical difference: report states this as an 'Actual' modality target amount, but evidence ([P9], [P13]) consistently records approximately £150,000, subject to legal complications and potential cost-sharing. This is not a data quality issue but a factual contradiction — the report's figure is more than double the evidenced one. The employment income shows three values (£42,000 current employed, £45,000 self-employed, £20,000 part-time planned) across different time periods; the report correctly reflects £45,000 as forward-looking post-self-employment change, but this context is compressed in the report's narrative. Retirement income target of £25,000–£30,000 and explicit flexible-income preference appear in [P5] as client objectives ('particularly interested in understanding the options available to you, such as the flexi-access drawdown and annuities') but the specific £25k–£30k figure is not quoted verbatim in the evidence pack; [P9] discusses 'utilise this allowance' and tax planning but not an explicit income floor. Risk profile 7/10 is confirmed in both report ([P7]) and internal consistency; capacity for loss 'moderate' is stated in report but the meeting evidence does not show detailed capacity-for-loss reasoning in retrieved passages. Cashflow analysis is asserted in the report but no standalone document is in the evidence pack; [P9] references Morgan agreeing to 'factor this potential inheritance into Melanie's cash flow plan' but this appears to be a commitment to perform, not a completed and reviewed analysis. The eight objectives with distributed priority ranks (1–5) do not appear enumerated in this form in the evidence; consolidation, tax efficiency, flexibility, and retirement timing are discussed but not formally ranked. The report's internal self-contradiction (noted in extraction: growth risk profile 7/10 versus modest £25k–£30k income target on sufficient assets) is not addressed or reconciled in either the report or the evidence. Guard consideration: the three different salary figures reflect genuine changes in employment status and planned transitions, not comparison errors — the report's use of £45,000 as forward-looking is reasonable; the report's treatment of £350,000 inheritance as a current objective target without qualification that it is contested, subject to legal delay, and half the amount evidenced, is not guard-protected and is a finding. The eight objectives with priority ranks cannot be corroborated in the evidence as presented; the report lists them in the canonical model but the evidence discusses objectives more fluidly. This is weak justification: objectives are discussed but not formally documented or ranked in the client-facing meeting record.

  [G2.6] Recommendations are appropriate for the stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts five recommendations linked to eight objectives. Recommendation 1 (Aviva Personal Pension with £67,945.44 transfer) is linked to OBJ1. Recommendation 2 is linked to OBJ2. Recommendation 3 is linked to OBJ2. Recommendations 4 and 5 are linked to OBJ3 and OBJ4. The stated objectives include: (OBJ1) receiving funds from ex-husband's pension share; (OBJ2) reviewing dormant True Potential and Nest pensions; (OBJ3) ensuring funds perform well to build for retirement; (OBJ4) retiring or reducing hours at age 60 with income of £25,000–£30,000 per annum; (OBJ5) preference for flexible income over guaranteed income; (OBJ6–7) IHT planning post-2027 reform. The client's time horizon is stated as long-term for most objectives. The planned withdrawal mechanism is UFPLS (Flexi-access Drawdown) starting 2026-06-24 (retirement age 60). The report states the client's personal monthly expenditure is £2,237 until retirement and £2,000 in retirement. Investment growth assumption is 6% per annum. The risk profile is assessed as 7/10 (Growth). Cashflow analysis shows assets sufficient under stress scenarios.
    File:     Meeting notes [P17] confirm investment horizon as 'five to ten years' and risk profile as level 7 (growth-oriented). Meeting notes [P19] state retirement objective as age 60 with annual pension of £25,000–£30,000, drawdown preference (no immediate 25% tax-free lump sum), and possible part-time post-retirement work. Client income is £42,000 salary. Expenditure [P19] totals £2,986.23 monthly with partner contributing £600, client's net portion approximately £2,386. Anticipated inheritance around £350,000 (minimum £150,000) with uncertain timeline. Product literature [P1], [P3], [P6], [P12] confirms Aviva platform supports drawdown from age 55 (rising to 57 from April 2028), flexible access with 25% tax-free lump sum, and death benefits free of IHT before age 75. Fact Find [P21] records life insurance of £70,000. No evidence in the file of specific ad-hoc access requirements, foreseeable life changes beyond inheritance timing uncertainty, or emergency fund liquidity needs beyond the statement in the report [P16] of £600,000 emergency funds and £400,000 savings.
    Discrepancies:
      - Investment horizon stated as 'five to ten years' [P17] but client retiring at age 60 (approximately 4 years from report date 2026-03-24) suggests horizon shorter than stated five-year minimum
      - Monthly expenditure figure: report states £2,237 personal monthly [P15] but meeting notes state total household £2,986.23 [P19]; if partner's £600 is deducted, client's portion is £2,386, not £2,237, a difference of £149
      - Retirement income target £25,000–£30,000 per annum matches both report [P5] and meeting notes [P19], but no explicit reconciliation given for how this modest target aligns with risk profile 7/10 (Growth) suited to capital accumulation rather than modest drawdown
      - Report asserts planned withdrawal start date of 2026-06-24 but client born 15/12/1970 [P7], [P8], [P9], [P10] would be age 55 in December 2025, making June 2026 retirement date consistent; however meeting notes do not explicitly confirm this specific date
      - Report states no regular contributions ('Portfolio Nil') but does not address whether client will make future contributions post-inheritance
    The report's linked recommendations are traceable to stated objectives, and the primary recommendation (Aviva Personal Pension with drawdown) is appropriate for the stated goal of retiring at 60 with flexible income of £25,000–£30,000. The Aviva platform literature confirms drawdown is available from age 55 and permits flexible access with no upper withdrawal limit, aligning with the client's stated preference for 'flexible income over guaranteed income'. The access mechanism (UFPLS/FAD) is not locked against a known access date; the client can access from age 55, retirement is targeted at 60, and the fund is intended to remain invested during drawdown. However, material discrepancies and gaps weaken the suitability justification. First, the investment horizon stated in meeting notes as 'five to ten years' [P17] does not reconcile clearly with a retirement date approximately 4 years from the report date; the report does not explain this inconsistency or whether the five-year minimum is a planning assumption or a true constraint. Second, the monthly expenditure figure used in cashflow modelling (£2,237 [P15]) differs from the meeting notes' statement of household expenditure (£2,986.23 [P19]); the extraction notes ambiguity here, and the report does not disclose which figure was used for modelling or why. Third, and most significantly for suitability, the report does not explain how a risk profile of 7/10 (Growth, suited to capital accumulation) aligns with a relatively modest retirement income target of £25,000–£30,000 per annum. The extraction identifies this as an internal inconsistency: the cashflow analysis shows assets will exceed retirement needs even under stress, yet the high risk profile is not justified as necessary for income adequacy. This suggests either the risk profile is incorrectly calibrated to the objectives, or the income target is conservative relative to capacity. The report does not address this mismatch. Fourth, the inheritance (anticipated minimum £150,000, potentially £350,000) has uncertain timing and is not factored into the recommendation's robustness; the report acknowledges it will affect IHT position but does not revisit retirement income or investment term in light of this contingency. Fifth, recommendations 2 and 3 are both linked to OBJ2 ('carry out a review of dormant True Potential and Nest Pensions') with no differentiation of their respective roles or how the review is reflected in the solution design; the report appears to use boilerplate linking here. Sixth, the report does not explicitly address foreseeable life changes (such as possible part-time post-retirement work mentioned in meeting notes [P19]) or how these might affect the income requirement or investment strategy. The recommendations are not incompatible with the stated goals, and the access mechanism is appropriate, but the justification lacks specificity on how the investment term, risk profile and modest income target have been reconciled, and material assumptions (expenditure base, inheritance timing, post-retirement income changes) are not disclosed consistently or addressed in the suitability rationale.

  [G2.7] Goals, assumptions or planning requirements stated in the report that are not supported by the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Eight objectives are stated: (1) Receive and manage Pension Sharing Order from ex-husband's Quilter pension; (2) Consolidate and review existing dormant pensions (Nest and True Potential); (3) Ensure pension funds perform well to build retirement savings; (4) Retire or reduce work hours at age 60 with annual income of £25,000 to £30,000; (5) Achieve tax-efficient, flexible retirement income rather than guaranteed income; (6) Navigate inheritance of £350,000 from mother's property sale; (7) Reduce Inheritance Tax liability through appropriate planning strategies; (8) Appoint adult daughters as Power of Attorney for estate planning. Planning assumptions include: annual earnings £45,000 until age 60; monthly personal expenditure £2,237 until retirement; retirement age 60; one-off sofa expenditure £2,500; mortgage payments £750/month for 7 years; inheritance receipt £350,000 in year 2; retirement expenditure £2,000/month net; investment growth 6% p.a. before charges; State Pension commencement age 67; general expenditure inflation 2%; savings inflation 2%; State Pensions inflation 2.5%.
    File:     Meeting records dated 16/01/2026, 20/02/2026, 18/03/2026, and 30/03/2026 confirm client engagement and discussions of objectives. [P6] records that client's primary concern is transfer of pension information post-divorce and commitment to securing financial future. [P10] confirms objectives include consolidating pension pots for better management and exploring tax-efficient strategies to optimise retirement income, with interest in flexi-access drawdown and annuities. [P1] confirms risk profile assessed as level seven (growth-oriented) and investment horizon of five to ten years. [P9] notes client is an accountant at Bridgeport Interiors Limited earning £42,000 annually. [P10] references client's aim to develop comprehensive retirement strategy aligned with risk profile and financial objectives. [P12] confirms action items including investigation of pension pot impact and tax efficiency, and consideration of transferring old pensions into current Aviva pension. Inheritance is mentioned in [P12] as a matter for client to keep Morgan updated on. No evidence in the file records explicit confirmation from the client of the specific retirement income target of £25,000–£30,000, the assumed state pension commencement age of 67, the specific inflation assumptions (2%, 2.5%), the 6% investment growth rate, the £750/month mortgage payment, or the retirement expenditure assumption of £2,000/month net. No document evidences client discussion or agreement to the Power of Attorney objective with daughters. The client career detail in [P9] states £42,000, not the £45,000 annual earnings assumed in the report's cashflow analysis.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report assumes annual earnings £45,000; evidence shows client earned £42,000 as accountant at Bridgeport Interiors Limited per [P9]
      - Report states investment horizon five to ten years in meeting summary [P1]; report's cashflow analysis assumes retirement at age 60 (approximately 6 years away from age 54 at time of report), not five to ten year horizon
      - No evidence in file that client explicitly stated £25,000–£30,000 retirement income target; meeting summaries reference retirement strategy objectives but do not quote this specific figure
      - No evidence client stated or agreed to 6% annual investment growth rate assumption for cashflow modelling
      - No evidence client stated mortgage payments of £750/month for 7 years
      - No evidence client stated personal expenditure assumptions of £2,237/month until retirement and £2,000/month in retirement net
      - No evidence client stated or agreed to 2% general expenditure inflation, 2% savings inflation, or 2.5% State Pension inflation assumptions
      - No evidence in file of client discussion or agreement regarding Power of Attorney appointment with adult daughters as an objective; [P10] references daughters as 'integral part of estate planning' but no corroboration of stated Power of Attorney objective
    The report's eight stated objectives derive from two sources: client statements recorded in meeting notes and adviser inferences. Seven objectives are marked isClientStated=true; one (Reduce IHT liability) is marked isClientStated=false and assertionStatus=Inferred.   [G2.8] Target retirement income stated in the report is consistent with the evidence and with the figures the plan is modelled on
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states a target retirement income of £27,500 net per annum (described as a range of £25,000 to £30,000) to be drawn annually from age 60 until State Pension commencement at age 67. The cashflow analysis assumes monthly personal expenditure in retirement of £2,000 net. The income target is stated as 'intended' and 'stated' in the objectives section, with the basis explicitly noted as 'Net'.
    File:     The case file evidences multiple statements of the retirement income objective. Meeting Summary [P11] records: 'You aim to draw an annual pension of £25,000 to £30,000 until your state pension begins at 67.' The same document confirms retirement age 60 and preference for drawdown approach. Fact Find [P10] notes current pension value circa £300,000 with view to use pension as 'dip and dip out pot of capital'. The suitability report assumption section [extracted model] states retirement personal expenditure of £2,000 net per month, equating to £24,000 per annum. However, Meeting Summary [P11] records total household monthly expenses of £2,986.23, with partner Tony contributing £600 monthly, implying client's share approximately £2,386. The extraction notes an ambiguity: two different monthly expenditure figures are stated — £2,986 (current household total) and £2,237 (personal expenditure used in cashflow modelling). The report assumes £2,000 net monthly in retirement but the factual position pre-retirement shows £2,237 monthly personal expenditure.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report models retirement expenditure of £2,000 per month net (£24,000 per annum), but pre-retirement personal expenditure stated in the cashflow assumptions is £2,237 per month. The basis of adjustment is not explained.
      - The target income range stated as £25,000–£30,000 in the report corresponds to the midpoint of £27,500, but the modelled retirement expenditure of £2,000 per month (£24,000 per annum) is below the stated minimum target of £25,000.
      - Fact Find [P10] records 'current pension value is circa £300,000' but the canonical model shows total existing arrangements of £327,945.44 (Quilter £260,000 + True Potential £46,672.80 + Nest £21,272.64). The discrepancy of £27,945 (9.3% higher) is not reconciled.
      - The inheritance amount is stated in the report's cashflow assumptions as £350,000 received next year, but Meeting Summary [P11] records 'at least £150,000 as your share' from mother's estate valued at £1.4 million, with timeline uncertain due to legal proceedings. These are materially different figures and certainty levels.
    The requirement tests whether the target retirement income stated in the report is consistent with evidence and with the figures the plan is modelled on. Three distinct consistency questions arise:
    
    1. **Consistency between stated target and modelled expenditure**: The report states a target income of £25,000–£30,000 annually (midpoint £27,500). The cashflow analysis models retirement expenditure at £2,000 net per month, or £24,000 per annum. This is below the stated minimum target. However, the stated target is described as income to be 'drawn', and the modelled expenditure of £2,000 monthly may represent only the net personal share after State Pension begins at 67, not the full drawdown during the 60–67 window when the stated £25,000–£30,000 target applies. The extraction notes an ambiguity: pre-retirement expenditure is £2,237 per month, but retirement expenditure assumptions state £2,000 net per month. The report does not explain whether the £2,000 figure is net of the State Pension or an absolute expenditure level. This ambiguity prevents full reconciliation. The stated target income range of £25,000–£30,000 is evidenced in [P11] Meeting Summary verbatim, so the target itself is supported. The question is whether the modelled assumptions would achieve it.
    
    2. **Consistency of inheritance assumptions**: The report's cashflow assumptions state an inheritance of £350,000 received next year. Meeting Summary [P11] records the client expects 'at least £150,000 as your share' from an estate valued at £1.4 million, with timeline uncertain. Fact Find [P10] notes 'She has a future inheritance of £150,000 and this will settle remainder of her mortgage liability, meaning she will go into retirement with no debts.' The report has inflated this from £150,000 to £350,000 without explanation and assigned a certain timing ('next year') to an event described as subject to legal proceedings with uncertain timing. This is a material factual inconsistency. The report's modelling assumes £350,000; the evidence supports only £150,000 with uncertain timing.
    
    3. **Consistency of pension fund value**: The extraction notes a discrepancy between Fact Find [P10] ('current pension value is circa £300,000') and the canonical model's sum of £327,945.44. The difference of £27,945 (9.3%) is unexplained. The three arrangements sum to £327,945.44 per the figures checked in code. This may reflect the date of the Fact Find versus the valuation dates of the pension statements, but the inconsistency is not resolved in the evidence provided.
    
    4. **Basis of income target**: The report states the target income as 'Net' basis. The modelled figures in the assumptions (£2,237 pre-retirement, £2,000 retirement) are also net. The stated range £25,000–£30,000 in [P11] does not specify gross or net, but the context ('annual pension of £25,000 to £30,000') and the client's role as an accountant (noted in [P11]) suggests understanding of net concepts. The basis consistency appears sound, but the report's explicit statement of 'Net' basis for the £27,500 midpoint is not evidenced in those words in the case file — it is the report's interpretation of the client's stated range.
    
    The extraction notes an internal inconsistency in the report: 'Client objectives state desire to retire at 60 with annual income of £25,000–£30,000 and explicit preference for flexible income over guaranteed income. Risk profile is assessed as 7/10 (Growth). Cashflow analysis shows assets would be sufficient even in stress scenarios including 6% inflation and 2007/8-style market crash. However, the incompatibility between a growth-oriented risk profile and the relatively modest income target (£25k–£30k on projected assets) is not explicitly addressed; the high risk profile appears suited to capital accumulation rather than the stated income objectives.' This tension is not resolved in the report's text and represents a lack of coherence between objectives and strategy, but it is not a factual inconsistency between report and file.
    
    Guard application: The target income is stated in today's terms (£25,000–£30,000) and the modelled figures assume 2% inflation. The report should reconcile these using stated escalation. The modelled retirement expenditure of £2,000 per month beginning at age 60 would escalate at 2% p.a., reaching approximately £2,219 per month by age 67 (7 years × 2% = 14% cumulative, or £2,000 × 1.1486 ≈ £2,297 at year 7). Over the 60–67 window this would average higher than £2,000. However, the report does not show this calculation, leaving the comparison incomplete.
    
    Conclusion on comparability: The target income is evidenced and stated clearly in [P11]. The modelled assumptions exist but the basis of the retirement expenditure assumption (whether it is the full income need or only the personal net share, and how it escalates) is not fully disclosed. The inheritance amount is materially different (£350,000 assumed vs. £150,000 evidenced), which affects the sufficiency of the plan but not the consistency of the stated income target itself. The income target of £25,000–£30,000 is supported by evidence; the plan's assumptions about how to deliver it contain inconsistencies and unexplained gaps in logic, particularly around the £2,000 retirement expenditure and the £350,000 inheritance.

  [G3.2] Capacity for loss assessment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the client's capacity for loss is 'Medium' [P3]. The assessment basis states: 'Based on your circumstances alone, your pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement, even following a fall in the market. Additionally you have the option to work beyond age 60 or to continue to work part time if necessary.' [P3]. The report indicates this assessment is separate from attitude to risk (isSeparatelyAssessedFromAtr = true) and is grounded in two identified factors: (1) sufficient assets to cover retirement expenditure even after market fall, and (2) alternative income options via continued or part-time work.
    File:     The Fact Find [P21] contains an assessor's note stating: 'Based on the above her capacity for loss is medium to high, however i feel this is leaning more to medium as if the withdrawals continue at the projected rate of £1,900 per month this will reduce longevity. Once her state pension kicks in at age 67, half of her retirement expenditure will be covered by this leaving the requirement of circa £12,000 per annum which will improve longevity and her overall capacity for loss.' [P21]. This assessment references a withdrawal rate of £1,900 per month and identifies a turning point at age 67 when state pension provision improves capacity. The Risk profile report [P1] states 'Capacity for loss for the goal' exists but contains no substantive detail on the assessment outcome or reasoning. The report [P3] also notes: 'your pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement, even following a fall in the market' and references a cashflow analysis performed. The cashflow modelling section [P8] describes scenarios modelled but does not isolate or quantify a capacity for loss finding separate from asset sufficiency.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts 'Medium' CFL; Fact Find [P21] states 'medium to high, however i feel this is leaning more to medium', suggesting initial assessment was higher before being moderated downward.
      - Report's CFL reasoning relies solely on asset sufficiency and alternative income; Fact Find [P21] adds a time-dependent element—that CFL is 'medium' only if withdrawals do not exceed £1,900/month and improves at age 67 when state pension activates, introducing a conditional and time-phased dimension absent from the report.
      - Report does not quantify or condition the CFL assessment on withdrawal rates or state pension timing; Fact Find [P21] explicitly models withdrawal impact on fund longevity and identifies age 67 as a material threshold.
    The report and file both agree that the client's capacity for loss is 'Medium' in final rating. However, they differ materially in the reasoning and conditions attached to this rating. The report presents CFL as a stable conclusion based on asset sufficiency and employment flexibility. The Fact Find reveals the assessment was initially 'medium to high' but moderated to 'medium' due to concerns about withdrawal sustainability—a conditional logic the final report does not disclose. The Fact Find introduces withdrawal-rate stress (£1,900/month threshold) and a time-dependent improvement at age 67 when state pension income covers approximately half the retirement expenditure need, leaving £12,000 per annum to be drawn. The report's assertion that CFL is 'based on your circumstances alone, your pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement, even following a fall in the market' [P3] is consistent with the Fact Find's underlying analysis, but the report omits the critical conditional factor: that this sufficiency depends on disciplined withdrawal rates and is dynamic—improving materially at state pension age. This is not a contradiction of the final rating but a material omission of the conditions and assumptions that produce it. The report treats CFL as static; the evidence shows it is time-dependent and withdrawal-rate sensitive. This represents weak justification rather than a data inconsistency, as the guard permits: a CFL rating with supporting financial reasoning is evidenced, but the report has not disclosed the withdrawal-rate condition or the age-67 inflection point that the assessor relied on. The assessment basis provided to the client ([P3]) does not communicate these material conditions, presenting a simplified rationale that obscures the true drivers of the 'Medium' rating.

        "Based on your circumstances alone, your pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement, even following a fall in the market. Additionally you have the option to work beyond age 60 or to continue to work part time if necessary."
      · Fact Find (1).md [B] (P21)
        "Based on the above her capacity for loss is medium to high, however i feel this is leaning more to medium as if the withdrawals continue at the projected rate of £1,900 per month this will reduce longevity.   [G3.3] Tolerance for loss and need-level risk
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the client confirmed tolerance for loss is within an acceptable level based on capacity to suffer losses.     File:     [P14] (the suitability report itself, category I) contains the tolerance statement. [P22] records that the client 'expressed a readiness to accept moderate capital loss in the short term' and describes the risk profile as adventurous (7/10). However, no separate quantified tolerance-for-loss figure or need-level risk assessment appears in the retrieved passages.     The report asserts that tolerance for loss was confirmed and capacity for loss determined as 'moderate', but the evidence supporting this assertion is thin. This is an absence of supporting documentation rather than a contradiction. [P22] provides some corroboration that the client expressed readiness to accept moderate capital loss, and [P19] records the client confirmed comfort with the risk level, but neither document quantifies tolerance for loss or explains the 'moderate' rating. The guard states that many firms do not quantify tolerance for loss separately, and absence is a documentation gap, not a contradiction. However, the requirement asks whether tolerance for loss is 'supported by evidence provided'. The cashflow analysis referenced in [P14] (stating assets sufficient even after market fall) provides indirect support for moderate capacity, but no explicit loss tolerance figure or need-level risk calculation appears in the file. The internal extraction notes flag an inconsistency: the risk profile (7/10 Growth) appears suited to capital accumulation, but the stated objectives (modest income £25k–£30k on sufficient assets) do not clearly demand this level of growth. This suggests the agreed risk level may not be tightly justified by need-level analysis. The report does not explicitly reconcile this. Tolerance for loss is asserted as acceptable but never quantified; the basis for the 'moderate' rating is stated in principle (assets sufficient, working flexibility available) but not detailed in documented assessment notes.

  [G3.4] Time horizon used to support the risk profile
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an investment term of 5 years in the risk assessment (/riskAssessment/perClient[]/investmentTermYears = 5). The report also records multiple objectives with varying time horizons: 6 objectives marked 'LongTerm', 1 marked 'MediumTerm', and 1 marked 'ShortTerm'. The report states the client's risk profile is assessed as level 7 (Growth), and meeting notes record 'Your investment horizon is five to ten years' [P14].
    File:     The meeting summary [P14] records 'Your investment horizon is five to ten years'. The Risk profile report [P2] describes the process whereby natural risk level from the questionnaire is discussed and altered until agreement is reached on an agreed risk level, taking into account 'investment amount, target and desired time horizon'. No explicit independent assessment of time horizon is provided in the evidence. The Aviva KFD [P10] notes that initial costs mean investments should be viewed as 'medium to long term (typically more than five years)'. Cashflow analysis [P9] projects forward year-on-year to assess achievement of objectives, which include retirement at age 60. The client's objectives include a preference for 'flexible income over guaranteed income' suggesting a drawdown approach extending beyond the 5-year investment term.
    Discrepancies:
      - The risk assessment states investment term as 5 years, but the meeting notes record 'five to ten years' [P14]
      - The objectives recorded include 6 'LongTerm', 1 'MediumTerm', and 1 'ShortTerm' entries, creating ambiguity about which objective corresponds to which time horizon classification
      - A 5-year term appears inconsistent with stated retirement planning to age 60 and the need for income drawdown thereafter, which would require assets to remain invested beyond 5 years
      - The report asserts a Growth risk profile (level 7) supported by a 5-year horizon, but also records investment horizon as 'five to ten years' in meeting notes, creating dual and inconsistent time-horizon statements
    The report presents two different time-horizon statements: 5 years in the risk assessment structure, and 'five to ten years' recorded in the meeting summary [P14]. This creates internal inconsistency. The 5-year figure is asserted in the canonical model's investmentTermYears field, but the meeting notes explicitly widen this to 'five to ten years' [P14]. This discrepancy is material because the time horizon is cited as a factor used to agree the risk profile [P2]. The report records multiple objectives with differing time-horizon categories (LongTerm, MediumTerm, ShortTerm) but does not map each objective to its corresponding timeline, making it impossible to verify whether the 5-year or 5-10 year horizon is appropriate for all stated goals. The client's stated objective to retire at age 60 with flexible income drawdown implies assets must remain invested well beyond 5 years to generate that income, yet this extension is not explicitly acknowledged or justified. The extraction records an internal contradiction: the client's preference for flexible income (implying drawdown over time) conflicts with a Growth risk profile based on a 5-year accumulation horizon without explicit justification for post-retirement investing. The evidence supports a 5-10 year horizon [P14] rather than the canonical 5-year assertion, but does not resolve whether this encompasses the full retirement drawdown period or only the initial accumulation phase.
