  [G3.5] Investment strategy and asset allocation
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report recommends a target risk rating of 7. The investment strategy comprises two funds in equal 50% allocations: (1) HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio C Acc, rated 7, with ongoing charge 0.19%; (2) Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity, rated 7, with ongoing charge 0.20%. The strategy type is BespokePortfolio. The combined portfolio is stated to align with the client's risk profile.
    File:     The Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity fund factsheets and KIIDs ([P10], [P13]) disclose an SRRI risk rating of 4 (described as mid-range: 'The Fund is rated 4 due to the nature of its investments'). The HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio factsheets ([P8], [P16]) confirm an ongoing charge of 0.19% and the fund is designed for long-term investment (5+ years). FE fund research ([P14]) labels the combined proposed portfolio 'Risk 7 HSBC Gl Dyn/Vanguard LS 80 TR' with performance 59.29 and volatility 8.63, consistent with a Risk 7 positioning on the FE scale. No explicit SRRI rating of 7 is disclosed for the Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity fund in the case file; the only SRRI disclosed for that fund is 4.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity carries a risk rating of 7, but the fund's own KIID ([P13]) and narrative disclosures state an SRRI risk rating of 4.
      - The report's stated ongoing charge for Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity is 0.20%, but no passage in the evidence confirms this figure. The Vanguard factsheets provided do not isolate a 0.20% charge for this specific fund.
      - The report states HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio ongoing charge as 0.19%, which is corroborated by [P13] and [P16], but the figure of 50% allocation to each fund is not directly matched in any evidence; the nearest comparable is the FE scatter label showing a blended 'Risk 7' portfolio but no discrete 50/50 split is stated.
    The core discrepancy is material: the Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity fund's own regulatory SRRI disclosure ([P13]) states a risk rating of 4, not 7. The report asserts both funds carry a risk rating of 7 to match the client's agreed risk profile. This is contradicted by the Vanguard fund's own KIID. The guard states that 'Fund-level risk ratings from a provider are not necessarily on the firm's client risk scale,' and indeed the Vanguard SRRI (4) and the report's stated rating (7) are on different scales — SRRI being regulatory (1–7 ordinal) and the report's claim of 7 appearing to be on an internal or commercial scale (possibly FE Analytics' scale, which is corroborated for the blended portfolio in [P14]). However, the report nowhere discloses that these are different scales, nor does it explain the relationship between the fund's SRRI rating of 4 and the assigned rating of 7. The FE scatter chart ([P14]) does label the combined portfolio 'Risk 7,' confirming that on some scale the blended strategy is positioned at risk 7. This suggests the report may be using a 0–10 commercial scale where 7 is appropriate for a portfolio holding 80% equity, whereas the fund's SRRI is the regulatory 1–7 scale where 4 reflects a balanced multi-asset profile. The two are not contradictory in outcome (a 4 on SRRI can be consistent with a 7 on a commercial scale for a blended portfolio), but the failure to disclose that the fund's disclosed risk rating (4) differs materially from the claimed match (7) is a significant transparency gap. It leaves unresolved whether the client has been informed that one of the two recommended funds carries a regulatory risk rating (4) that is substantially lower than the stated target (7). The 0.20% ongoing charge for Vanguard is not corroborated in the evidence; no passage confirms this figure. The 50% allocation split is stated in the report but not independently verified in any retrieved passage, though the blended portfolio as a whole is referenced in [P14]. The discrepancy in the Vanguard ongoing charge figure (0.20% stated in report vs. no confirmation in evidence) and the absence of a disclosed scale reconciliation for the risk ratings constitute material gaps in justification.

  [G3.7] Recommendation or strategy exceeds risk tolerance, taking account of capacity for loss
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an agreed risk profile of 7 on a 1-10 scale [P2]. The report states capacity for loss is 'moderate' [P2]. The recommended investment strategy comprises HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio C Acc (50%, risk rating 7) and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity (50%, risk rating 7), with a combined target risk rating of 7 [P4]. The report describes the client as a Growth investor who views risk as opportunity, accepts that values will fluctuate, and understands the need to take risk to achieve investment goals [P2]. The report confirms the agreed risk level was reached following interactive discussion considering natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss, and required return, and that cashflow analysis shows sufficient funds for retirement even following a market fall [P2].
    File:     The risk profile report confirms the process: natural risk level is derived from the Attitude to Risk Questionnaire completed 16/01/2026, discussed and agreed [P1]. The agreed risk level process is described as mapping each risk level to asset classes designed to expose appropriate risk, with projections produced using stochastic modelling by Hymans Robertson [P1, P6]. The report confirms that following discussion considering natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss, and required return, an agreed risk level was reached, with 'No additional notes were recorded' [P6]. Projections for the agreed risk level are shown at an estimated 1% total charge [P6]. The existing arrangements show: Quilter at risk level 6 (1-10 scale), True Potential at risk level from a Balanced fund, and Nest Higher Risk Fund labelled as risk level 8 or 'Very Adventurous' (9 on an unspecified scale) [P8, P14]. The FE scatter chart shows the recommended Risk 7 strategy (HSBC Gl Dyn/Vanguard LS 80 TR) at performance 59.29 and volatility 8.63, alongside Nest Higher Risk at 59.28 and 8.50, True Potential at 52.25 and 8.45 [P14].
    Discrepancies:
      - Recommended strategy risk rating of 7 is on a 1-10 scale. Existing Nest arrangement is described as 'Higher Risk Fund' with label '8' on one reference and 'Very Adventurous' with implied rating 9 on another reference, with no clear scale mapping provided [P8, P14].
      - Existing arrangements show materially different risk levels: Quilter 6, True Potential unspecified or Balanced profile, Nest 8/9 (scales unclear). The recommended strategy at 7 sits between retained arrangements at 6 and 8, but comparability across these scales is not established [P8, P14].
      - True Potential existing arrangement risk profile is not numerically quantified in the evidence; it is referred to only as 'Balanced' in product documentation, making direct comparison to the agreed rating of 7 impossible [P11].
      - The report asserts capacity for loss as 'moderate' but does not quantify it or relate it numerically to the risk rating of 7 or to potential loss scenarios. No loss tolerance range, drawdown percentage, or portfolio volatility threshold is specified as the basis for this assessment [P2, P6].
      - Volatility of recommended strategy (8.63) from FE scatter chart exceeds volatility of existing Nest arrangement (8.50) and is materially higher than existing True Potential (8.45), suggesting the recommended strategy may be incrementally more volatile despite being on a lower risk rating (7 vs 8 for Nest) [P14].
    The core assertion—that the recommended strategy (target risk rating 7) does not exceed the agreed risk profile (7)—is internally consistent on the face of the report: both are stated as 7 on the same 1-10 scale [P2, P4]. The process is documented: the client completed the Attitude to Risk Questionnaire on 16/01/2026, discussed natural risk level with projections, and reached an agreed risk level through interactive discussion that considered capacity for loss and required return [P1, P6]. However, critical gaps arise when testing the coherence of the risk and capacity assessment itself. First, capacity for loss is asserted as 'moderate' but is never quantified [P2]. The report relies on cashflow analysis showing sufficiency even in stress scenarios (6% inflation, 2007/8-style market crash) as the basis for this moderate rating, but does not specify what loss percentage or volatility band 'moderate' represents on the agreed risk scale of 1-10. The report does not state, for instance, that the client can tolerate a loss of X% of capital, or that the portfolio volatility of 8.63 falls within an acceptable range. Without a quantified loss tolerance, the assertion that a risk rating of 7 is justified by the moderate capacity for loss cannot be directly tested: the reader cannot verify that the chosen risk level is calibrated to, rather than exceeding, the client's ability to absorb losses. Second, the existing arrangements present a comparability problem. The Quilter arrangement is at risk level 6 (1-10 scale), stated as 'Risk Level 6' [P8]. The Nest arrangement is labelled as 'Higher Risk Fund' with reference to '8' in one table and 'Very Adventurous' with an implied rating of 9 in another, but the scale is not identified [P8, P14]. The True Potential arrangement is described only as 'Balanced' in the fund documentation, with no numerical risk rating provided [P11]. Under the guard that a retained plan invested outside the agreed profile is a concern where the report claims alignment, the fact that Nest is rated 8 (or 9) against an agreed profile of 7 is material: if the Nest arrangement remains invested as part of the portfolio, it contradicts the stated risk alignment. However, this case is a pension switch: the existing arrangements are proposed for replacement, not retention. The passage of the recommended strategy at risk 7 with volatility 8.63 versus Nest at risk 8 with volatility 8.50 suggests that on raw volatility the recommended strategy is marginally higher despite a lower rating, which raises questions about scale calibration but does not directly violate the requirement—the requirement is that strategy does not exceed agreed risk, and both are stated as 7 [P14]. Third, the risk profile report notes that projections assume an estimated 1% total charge but no confirmation is given that the final recommended strategy's 0.20% combined charge aligns with or deviates from this assumption; this does not affect risk rating comparison but affects the reliability of projected outcomes used to justify the risk choice [P4, P6]. The factual statement—that target risk rating (7) equals agreed rating (7)—is supported. The logical statement—that this risk rating is justified by the moderate capacity for loss—cannot be tested against available quantified loss tolerance criteria. The client's stated objectives (retire at 60 with £25,000–£30,000 annual income, preference for flexible over guaranteed income) align with a Growth approach and a drawdown strategy, and the cashflow analysis confirms sufficiency, but the internal extraction notes flag an ambiguity: the report does not explicitly address the tension between a 7/10 growth profile (typically suited to capital accumulation and higher volatility acceptance) and relatively modest income objectives on a portfolio that appears over-provisioned on cashflow analysis [P2, P6]. This is not a breach of the requirement—it reflects suitability logic—but it underscores that the risk choice appears driven by goal achievement rather than being independently constrained by capacity for loss. Overall, the comparison required by [G3.7] can be performed on the rating figures themselves: strategy 7 does not exceed agreed 7. However, the foundation for this comparison—whether a moderate capacity for loss genuinely supports a risk rating of 7 on a 1-10 scale—is not quantitatively evidenced, making the entire suitability chain dependent on qualitative assessment that cannot be independently verified against documented loss tolerance thresholds.

  [G3.8] Conflicting risk scores, descriptions or labels across the file
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts a single, consistent risk profile for the client: assessed code 7, agreed code 7, on a 1-10 scale, labelled 'Growth'. The description provided is: 'As a Growth investor you do not see yourself as a cautious person and are inclined to view risk as a source of opportunity rather than as a threat.' The report states this assessment was completed on 2026-03-24 using a risk profiling questionnaire methodology. The reconciliation shows no override (hasOverride: false), with direction 'Unchanged' from assessed to agreed code.
    File:     The evidence contains multiple references to the client's risk profile across different documents: [P1] and [P3] reference a risk profile report dated 16 March 2026, describing a process where natural risk level from questionnaire is used as a starting point and adjusted through discussion to reach an agreed level. [P10] confirms 'Risk Profile for Melanie Anness' from the same report. [P11] and [P13] contain references to existing Nest holdings in the 'NEST Higher Risk Fund Pension' and state a risk classification of '9 Very Adventurous' with the description: 'As a Very Adventurous investor you do not see yourself as a cautious person and usually view risk as a source of opportunity rather than as a threat.' [P12] (Meeting Summary) states 'Your risk profile assessed as level seven, indicating a preference for growth-oriented investments' and confirms comfort with the risk level. [P14] references asset allocation data for Nest existing holdings. The Nest fund classification document [P11] shows the existing holding is in a fund rated '9 Very Adventurous', not 7 Growth.
    Discrepancies:
      - The Nest existing holding is classified as risk level 9 'Very Adventurous' [P11] whilst the report asserts the agreed risk profile is 7 'Growth'. These are on the same 1-10 scale but represent different points on that scale.
      - The label 'Very Adventurous' (Nest existing holdings, [P11]) differs from 'Growth' (suitability report assertion). While both descriptions emphasise viewing risk as opportunity, they are distinct categorisations on the same scale.
      - The existing Nest holding documentation [P11] describes the fund holder as 'Very Adventurous', whereas the client's agreed profile per the suitability report is 'Growth', a lower risk rating.
    The report asserts a single, clearly-stated agreed risk profile of 7 on a 1-10 scale labelled 'Growth', with no override documented. This is internally consistent within the report itself. However, evidence in the case file reveals that an existing arrangement (Nest Higher Risk Fund Pension) is classified as risk level 9 'Very Adventurous' on the same 1-10 scale [P11], [P13]. This is not a conflict within the suitability report itself (which shows no internal contradiction between assessed and agreed codes, both at 7), but it raises a material discrepancy between the client's stated agreed risk profile (7 Growth) and the actual risk profile of an existing holding (9 Very Adventurous). The distinction matters: the instruction states that conflicting scores, descriptions or labels must be identified across the file. The Nest fund is described as 9 'Very Adventurous' and the recommended strategy is 7 'Growth'. Both are on the 1-10 scale. The descriptions differ: 'Very Adventurous' is a more risk-seeking label than 'Growth'. The guard 'A joint case has a risk profile per client; two different scores for two clients are not a contradiction' does not apply here—this is a single client with two different risk ratings in the file on the same scale. The guard about labels ('The same profile carries different labels in different houses...a label difference with a matching description is not a conflict') does not fully protect this discrepancy because the numeric codes differ (9 vs 7) on the same scale. The existing Nest fund (9 Very Adventurous) represents an actual current holding, not a recommended strategy. The suitability report asserts an agreed profile of 7 Growth for the recommended approach. The presence of an existing holding at 9 Very Adventurous is not itself evidence that the agreed profile is wrong—but it is an unresolved conflict in the file: two risk scores for the same client on the same scale, with differing labels and descriptions. The meeting summary [P12] confirms the client's comfort with the agreed level 7 risk profile, but does not address or explain why the existing Nest holding remains at level 9. This is a data quality and consistency issue: the file contains conflicting risk profile assignments (7 vs 9) for the same client on the same measurement scale.

  [G3.9] Recommended funds and portfolio align with the client's attitude to risk, capacity for loss and investment term
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report recommends a bespoke portfolio comprising 50% HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio C Acc (risk rating 7, active management, 0.19% OCF) and 50% Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity (risk rating 7, passive management, 0.20% OCF). Both funds are stated to have a risk rating of 7 on a 1-10 scale, matching the client's agreed risk profile of 7 (Growth). The report states the client's capacity for loss is 'moderate' based on sufficient assets to cover retirement expenditure even after market falls, with options to work beyond age 60 or part-time. The investment term is 5 years. The rationale emphasises that the funds 'reflect your risk profile both individually and combined' and provide diversification across asset classes and market sectors. The benchmark is UT Mixed Investment 40-85% Shares Retail TR in GB. The strategy is described as a blend of active and passive investments keeping costs low.
    File:     Evidence confirms both recommended funds carry a risk rating of 7. The HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio is evidenced with an ongoing charge of 0.19% [P1]. The Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity fund is confirmed with 0.20% OCF [P1]. Portfolio analysis data [P10-P13] shows the combined 50/50 allocation with both funds rated risk 7, producing 5-year annualised return of 9.85% and standard deviation of 8.62. A scatter chart [P2] plots 'Risk 7 HSBC Gl Dyn/Vanguard LS 80 TR in GB' with performance 59.29 and volatility 8.63, substantially higher volatility than the benchmark UT Mixed Investment 40-85% Shares (volatility 7.48). The HSBC fund prospectus [P15] describes its risk profile as 4 on a 1-5 HSBC scale (equivalent to a lower-to-mid risk positioning), with a stated objective to 'provide growth in line with its risk profile' and minimum holding period of 5 years or more. The Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% fund composition [P6-P9] shows 80% equity exposure (approximately 59% global equities, 19.76% UK equities, 18.98% developed world ex-UK, 6.71% emerging markets) and 20% fixed income (14.07% global bonds, 3.29% UK government bonds, 1.34% investment-grade bonds), consistent with a growth-oriented strategy. The client's capacity for loss is documented as 'moderate' with assets (£327,945) assessed as sufficient for retirement income of £25,000-£30,000 even under stress scenarios including 6% inflation and 2007/8-style market crash. Investment term stated as 5 years.
    Discrepancies:
      - HSBC fund's stated risk rating differs by scale: the report asserts risk 7 on a 1-10 scale, but the HSBC prospectus [P15] states the fund's 'risk profile is rated as 4 where 1 is a lower level of risk and 5 is a higher level of risk' (HSBC's proprietary 1-5 scale). This is a cross-scale comparison.
      - Volatility of the recommended portfolio (8.63 per [P2]) exceeds the volatility of the benchmark stated in the report (UT Mixed Investment 40-85% Shares Retail TR in GB, volatility 7.48 per [P2]). The portfolio's risk metric is materially higher than the benchmark risk metric.
    The requirement tests whether the recommended funds align with the client's attitude to risk (ATR), capacity for loss (CFL), and investment term. The stated investment term of 5 years is met by both funds, each carrying a minimum holding period of 5 years or more [P15, P17-P19]. The ATR and CFL alignment presents a more complex picture. The report asserts that both funds carry a risk rating of 7 on a 1-10 scale, matching the client's agreed ATR of 7 (Growth). However, the HSBC prospectus [P15] states that the HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio 'risk profile is rated as 4' on a 1-5 scale. This is a cross-scale comparison, and the guard note in the method states: 'A fund's SRRI or risk-and-reward indicator runs on its own scale and does not map onto the profiler's; use it as corroboration, never as a value match.' The adviser has not disclosed the mapping methodology or shown working for how a 1-5 rating of 4 translates to a 1-10 rating of 7. A direct proportional conversion (4/5 × 10 = 8) would suggest the HSBC fund maps to 8 on a 1-10 scale, not 7, creating a potential overstatement. The Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity fund's internal risk labelling is not provided in the evidence; the report simply asserts its risk rating as 7. No discrepancy can be established on that fund. The portfolio volatility of 8.63 [P2] materially exceeds the benchmark volatility of 7.48 [P2]. The report states the recommended funds are chosen because they 'reflect your risk profile both individually and combined' and the strategy is 'fairly simple' [H section]. However, the portfolio exhibits higher market risk (volatility) than its own stated benchmark. The report assesses the client's CFL as 'moderate' and rationalises this on the basis of sufficient assets (£327,945) to fund retirement objectives (£25,000-£30,000 annually) even under stress scenarios including a 2007/8-style market crash. The higher-than-benchmark volatility creates tension with this 'moderate' CFL assessment: under the same crash scenario that the CFL assessment assumes the portfolio can withstand, a portfolio with 8.63 volatility would experience larger drawdowns than one with 7.48 volatility. This is not necessarily a finding of unsuitability (the assets may be sufficient even with larger drawdowns), but it represents an internal inconsistency between the stated CFL rationale and the portfolio's risk exposure relative to its benchmark. The report does not explain or acknowledge this tension. The investment term of 5 years is satisfied and the client's preference for flexibility (drawdown rather than annuity) is noted as OBJ5, but the intersection of the high volatility portfolio with the relatively short 5-year term and moderate CFL is not explicitly addressed. In summary: (1) the HSBC fund's risk rating mapping from HSBC 1-5 scale (4) to 1-10 scale (7) is not explained and may be incorrect; (2) the portfolio's volatility (8.63) materially exceeds the benchmark (7.48), creating tension with a 'moderate' CFL assessment; and (3) these misalignments are not disclosed or explained in the report's rationale.

  [G3.10] Unsupported, contradictory or unexplained statements about risk, loss capacity, term or strategy
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Attitude to Risk: Assessed code 7 on 1-10 scale, agreed code 7 (Growth). Description: 'As a Growth investor you do not see yourself as a cautious person and are inclined to view risk as a source of opportunity rather than as a threat.' Tolerance for Loss: Client confirmed acceptable tolerance based on capacity to suffer losses. Investment Term: Not explicitly stated in canonical model. Investment Strategy: Bespoke portfolio with target risk rating 7 on 1-10 scale; HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio (50%, risk rating 7, 0.19% OCF) and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity (50%, risk rating 7, 0.20% OCF). Both funds individually and combined reflect risk profile 7. Strategy rationale includes risk alignment, diversification, blend of active/passive, simplicity, competitive charges, and peer performance.
    File:     Risk Profile Report [P1, P2]: Risk assessment conducted via attitude to risk questionnaire (16/01/2026) and discussion. Meeting [P5, P6]: Client objectives include consolidating pensions, exploring drawdown and annuities, achieving 'sustainable and flexible retirement'. Retirement goal: age 60, annual income £25,000–£30,000, preference for 'flexible income over guaranteed income' (extracted note). Cashflow discussions address 12-month expense buffer. Recommended portfolio [P9]: Risk 7 HSBC Dyn/Vanguard LS 80 shows volatility 8.63 and performance 59.29%, exceeding benchmark volatility of 7.48.
    Discrepancies:
      - 1. Capacity for Loss assessment framed as 'moderate' but described entirely via asset sufficiency and alternative work options, with no explicit quantification of loss tolerance or adverse scenario modelling (e.g., % fall, stress test outcome).
      - 2. Investment term stated in file as 'five to ten years' (P4) but suitability report does not state a specific investment term in the canonical model; relationship between term and target retirement age 60 is not explicit.
      - 3. Recommended portfolio volatility (8.63, P9) exceeds benchmark volatility (7.48, P9) by 1.15 points, yet report asserts funds 'reflect your risk profile' without explaining whether this volatility increase is acceptable or explained.
      - 4. Strategy assigned to risk rating 7, but existing True Potential holdings carry risk rating 8 ('Growth' per [P22] vs. 'Very Adventurous' for Nest at 9), creating potential inconsistency in how risk ratings are calibrated across existing and recommended arrangements.
      - 5. Client objectives include 'preference for flexible income over guaranteed income', suggesting inclination towards drawdown; however, no drawdown vs. annuity comparison or suitability analysis is documented for a client age 60 retirement.
      - 6. Capacity for Loss rationale cites option to 'work beyond age 60 or part-time', yet client is already age 55 and considering early retirement; the credibility of working beyond 60 as a loss mitigation is unclear given life circumstances.
    The report asserts a consistent risk profile (assessed 7, agreed 7, strategy target 7) with corroborating evidence from risk profiling process [P1, P2] and client confirmation [P4]. However, several tensions emerge. First, capacity for loss is rated 'moderate' and justified solely by asset sufficiency and work optionality [canonical model], but no quantification of acceptable loss percentage or stress outcome is provided. The file contains no adverse scenario analysis showing portfolio resilience to a 'fall in the market' as claimed. Second, the recommended portfolio exhibits volatility 8.63 [P9], which exceeds the benchmark 7.48, yet this increase is not explained or justified given the 'moderate' capacity for loss rating. Third, the client's stated objective for 'flexible income over guaranteed income' and retirement age 60 with modest income target (£25k–£30k) sits uneasily with a growth-oriented risk profile 7; no drawdown/annuity analysis is documented. Fourth, the existing True Potential holding carries an 8-risk rating ('Growth', [P22]), while the recommended strategy is rated 7, yet no reconciliation explains this apparent downgrade or whether ratings use the same scale/methodology. Fifth, the investment term is stated in the file as 'five to ten years' [P4], but this does not appear in the canonical model; this gap obscures whether the strategy timeline aligns with retirement planning. The capacity for loss remark that client 'can work beyond age 60 or part-time' [canonical model] conflicts with the meeting notes indicating client is transitioning out of employment at 55 [P7, P8] and planning part-time return at ~£20k from January; the assumption that continued work is a viable loss buffer is not evidenced. This mirrors the finding here: the strategy and profile are internally consistent in numerical terms (7=7), but their relationship to client circumstances, objectives, and loss capacity is unexplained. No override or adjustment is documented [canonical model reconciliation shows 'Unchanged'], so the coherence between strategy, term, capacity, and objectives rests on assertion rather than demonstrated alignment.

  [G3.11] Funds inside a retained arrangement align with the agreed risk rating
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states that all three existing arrangements (Quilter, True Potential, and Nest) are subject to FullTransfer advice actions. The report does not assert that any funds are being retained. The report does identify risk ratings for existing arrangements: Quilter at Risk Level 6 (1-10 scale), and Nest Higher Risk Fund at 8 (Nest Higher Risk Fund scale). The report's agreed client risk rating is 7/10. The report asserts that True Potential's current portfolio is 'not in line with the agreed risk level' and recommends transfer. For Nest, it states 'The current fund is not in line with the agreed risk level' and recommends transfer.
    File:     The evidence file contains risk rating information from multiple sources. [P1] describes the risk profiling process and notes an agreed risk level was reached through discussion, but does not state the specific agreed rating. [P5] shows Nest Higher Risk Fund with discrete performance data and confirms it is labeled as 'Higher Risk'. [P4] shows a fund rated 4 on a lower-to-higher risk scale (KIID scale). [P2] and [P3] show performance data but no explicit risk ratings. [P6], [P7], [P8] contain fund factsheets with risk disclosures but no comparative risk ratings against the client's agreed profile. No document in the evidence file provides the client's agreed risk rating of 7/10 or explicitly maps existing fund holdings to that agreed rating on a comparable scale.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts Quilter holdings are at Risk Level 6 (on a 1-10 scale) but the evidence file provides no corroborating documentation of this rating or confirmation it is on the same 1-10 scale used for the agreed client rating of 7.
      - The report asserts Nest Higher Risk Fund is at risk level 8 but states this is on a 'Nest Higher Risk Fund' scale, not the 1-10 scale. No mapping between the Nest scale and the client's 1-10 scale is provided in the evidence.
      - The report asserts True Potential funds are 'not in line with the agreed risk level' but no individual fund risk ratings are provided in the evidence to substantiate this claim.
      - The report claims an agreed client risk rating of 7/10 but [P1], the risk profile document, does not state the specific agreed rating; it only describes the process for reaching one.
      - The advice action for all three arrangements is FullTransfer, meaning no funds are actually being retained. The requirement [G3.11] asks about funds inside a retained arrangement, but the evidence shows all arrangements are being transferred out.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Confirmation of the agreed client risk rating (7/10) in the evidence file; [P1] describes the process but does not state the final agreed rating., Risk ratings for each existing fund holding, stated on a scale comparable to the client's agreed 1-10 scale., Evidence that the Nest risk scale ('Nest Higher Risk Fund' at 8) is mappable to or comparable with the 1-10 client scale., Any retained arrangement or fund; all three existing arrangements are marked for FullTransfer, so no funds are retained., Documentation of the rationale for transferring each arrangement if the assessment is that funds do not align; the report provides cost and oversight rationales but not explicit risk-profile alignment evidence.
    The requirement [G3.11] is triggered when the canonical model shows investmentOrPensionRecommendation = true. However, the trigger asks specifically about 'Funds inside a retained arrangement.' The canonical model shows adviceAction = 'FullTransfer' for all three existing arrangements (Quilter, True Potential, Nest). This means no funds are being retained; all are being transferred. The requirement's decision logic states 'N/A: Trigger absent' when no retention occurs. However, the report does assert risk ratings for the existing arrangements (Quilter 6, Nest 8) and claims misalignment ('not in line with the agreed risk level'). A comparison cannot be performed because: (1) the agreed client risk rating is stated in the canonical model as 7/10 but is not corroborated in the evidence; [P1] describes the process but does not state what the agreed rating is; (2) the Quilter rating of 6 is stated with no scale identified in the evidence; (3) the Nest rating is stated to be on a 'Nest Higher Risk Fund' scale (a provider-specific label, not a 1-10 scale) with no mapping to the client's 1-10 scale provided; (4) no individual fund risk ratings are given for the True Potential holdings, only an assertion they do not align. The mismatches between report assertion and evidence—particularly the absence of the agreed rating from the evidence file and the incomparability of scales—mean the alignment claim cannot be substantiated. Because all arrangements are marked for transfer, the strictest reading of [G3.11] is that it does not apply (no funds retained). However, the report's claim that existing funds are misaligned with the agreed profile rests on unsupported assertions: the agreed rating is not confirmed in evidence, and scale comparability is not established. The report cites cost savings and oversight as reasons to transfer, not alignment; alignment is asserted as a problem but not evidenced.

CHK-004 — Knowledge, Experience and Product Complexity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G4.1, G4.2, G4.3, G4.4, G4.5, G4.6, G4.7). The report states 5 years of self-directed pension experience 'without taking any advice', but the case file shows the client is now receiving advice from TFAS Wealth Ltd and has made switches/consolidations being recommended, which contradicts the 'without taking any advice' framing for recent years. The report asserts pension experience but provides no detail of when, how long, or what type of pension arrangements were previously held. The Fact Find [P18] shows three different personal pension arrangements (Aviva personal pension, Aviva wrap, and potentially others), but does not record in detail the client's previous self-directed investing activity or knowledge of pensions prior to the current advisory engagement. 10 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G4.1] Client's stated product and investment knowledge level
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states the client's knowledge level as 'Good'. The report asserts: 'Your background in accounting and currency trading has equipped you with a solid understanding of financial matters. You confirmed that you have invested in pensions without taking any advice within the last 5 years and you regularly check the value of your funds. You feel you have a fair degree of knowledge and understanding in relation to [investment matters].' The report characterizes the client as someone who 'have experience of investments and find financial matters fairly easy to understand' and notes 'you understand that some risk needs to be take in order to achieve investment growth.'
    File:     The case file contains multiple corroborating pieces of evidence for the client's stated knowledge level. [P6] states: 'You are a seasoned professional with a background in accounting and currency trading, which provides you with a solid understanding of financial matters.' [P6] further notes: 'Your experience as a currency trader in the city has equipped you with a keen awareness of market dynamics, although you do not currently maintain a stocks and shares portfolio.' [P7] confirms the client's objectives include 'exploring tax-efficient strategies to optimise your retirement income' and interest in 'understanding the options available to you, such as the flexi-access drawdown and annuities.' The risk profile assessment [P2] and [P24] note the client's agreement to a risk level 7 (Growth) and confirm discussion of capacity for loss and investment understanding. [P5] records 'You confirmed your comfort with the level of risk associated with your investment strategy.' The meeting summaries document active engagement with complex financial concepts and strategic decision-making.
    The report's characterization of the client's knowledge level as 'Good' is consistent across all available evidence. The client's background in accounting (employed as an accountant at Bridgeport Interiors Limited) and prior experience as a currency trader in the city provide a documented foundation for financial acumen. The fact find [P6] explicitly confirms this professional background. The client's stated behaviour — regularly checking pension values, having invested in pensions independently within the last 5 years, and actively engaging with tax-efficiency and drawdown concepts — aligns with a 'Good' knowledge level. The risk profile materials [P2], [P24] and meeting records [P5] show the client understood and engaged with discussion of their risk level, capacity for loss, and investment objectives at an appropriate level of sophistication. The client is not recorded as having limited, no, or poor understanding. No fact find entry contradicts or qualifies the stated 'Good' knowledge level. The client's stated comfort with risk ('You confirmed your comfort with the level of risk associated with your investment strategy' [P5]) and engagement with complex pension strategy matters (consolidation, tax-efficiency, drawdown vs. annuity options [P7]) support a knowledge level consistent with 'Good'. There is no evidence the client was assessed as uncomfortable or lacking comprehension. The one caveat — 'although you do not currently maintain a stocks and shares portfolio' [P6] — does not undermine knowledge or understanding; it reflects current holdings, not capability. The recommended products (pension consolidation into drawdown, Vanguard LifeStrategy Funds) are consistent with a Growth risk profile and do not exhibit unusual complexity that would require higher evidence of comprehension in this case.

  [G4.2] Previous investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the client 'have invested in pensions without taking any advice within the last 5 years', indicating 5 years of pension experience with hasHeld=true and assertionStatus=Stated.
    File:     The Fact Find [P18] shows 'Do you have any existing money purchase schemes?: No' in the Money Purchase Pension Schemes section. The Risk profile report [P10] and [P13] records 'Investor Experience' section but the notes recorded following discussion are blank: 'The notes recorded following the discussion were:' with no content provided. The True Potential Defaqto holdings [P6] and Nest Defaqto holdings [P11] show the client currently holds three pension arrangements (Quilter £260,000, True Potential £46,672.80, Nest £21,272.64) totalling £327,945.44, demonstrating active pension holdings. The Fact Find [P7] shows Total Assets of £587,000 including all savings and investments.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states 5 years of self-directed pension experience 'without taking any advice', but the case file shows the client is now receiving advice from TFAS Wealth Ltd and has made switches/consolidations being recommended, which contradicts the 'without taking any advice' framing for recent years
      - The report asserts pension experience but provides no detail of when, how long, or what type of pension arrangements were previously held
      - The Fact Find [P18] shows three different personal pension arrangements (Aviva personal pension, Aviva wrap, and potentially others), but does not record in detail the client's previous self-directed investing activity or knowledge of pensions prior to the current advisory engagement
      - The Risk profile report [P10] and [P13] has a blank 'Investor Experience' section with no recorded notes about previous pension or investment experience despite being central to the suitability assessment
    The report asserts the client has 5 years of pension experience acquired 'without taking any advice'. The Fact Find [P18] records existing pensions but does not narratively establish prior self-directed pension experience. The Risk profile report [P10] [P13] contains a dedicated 'Investor Experience' section but records no notes, leaving the discussion that led to the 5-year assertion undocumented. The report's phrasing 'without taking any advice within the last 5 years' is ambiguous: it may mean the client made unaided decisions during that period, or it may mean the client has not sought regulated advice, which is now contradicted by the current advisory engagement. The file shows the client now holds multiple platform-based pensions with complex fund allocations (Nest Higher Risk Fund at 'Very Adventurous' rating per [P11], True Potential Growth-Aligned holdings per [P6]), but no evidence is provided that the client previously understood or selected such complexity independently. The guard permits distinguishing passive workplace pension holding from self-directed investing; if the 5 years refers to passive holding (e.g., an employer scheme), the report should have stated so. The current holdings evidence current experience but do not corroborate the assertion of 5 years of prior unaided investing without additional narrative support.

  [G4.3] Complexity of recommended products and investments, including risks and features
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts the recommended product (Aviva Platform Personal Pension with Flexi-access Drawdown) is 'Complex' with drivers including: pension platform with discretionary fund selection; flexi-access drawdown with variable withdrawal options; tax implications including marginal rate taxation on drawdowns after age 75; inheritance tax treatment changes from 6 April 2027; and multiple investment funds requiring ongoing monitoring. The client's knowledge is described as: background in accounting and currency trading providing 'solid understanding of financial matters'; self-directed pension investment without advice in the last 5 years; regular checking of fund values; 'fair degree of knowledge and understanding' in relation to pensions and investments; and self-assessed comfort with medium-risk portfolios and knowledge of tax implications.
    File:     Product literature [P1, P2, P3, P6, P7, P8, P10, P11, P12, P14] confirms Aviva Platform Personal Pension offers flexi-access drawdown with: discretionary fund selection; variable withdrawal mechanics (single drawdown, phased drawdown, one-off withdrawals); tax-free lump sum up to 25% subject to Lump Sum Allowance of £268,275, remainder taxed at marginal rate; death benefits varying by age (tax-free before age 75, taxable at beneficiary's marginal rate after age 75); and from 6 April 2027, most unused DC pensions and death benefits included in taxable estate for IHT [P3]. Investment literature [P13, P18, P23] shows recommended funds (Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity, HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic) with ongoing charge of 0.19% and risk category 4. Client's factual background [P15] confirms accounting and currency trading experience, self-directed pension investment, regular monitoring, and states 'fair degree of knowledge' but does not specify comprehension of flexi-access drawdown mechanics, tax variability at age 75, or 2027 IHT reform. Risk profile assessed as 7/10 (Growth) with capacity for loss 'moderate'.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims client has 'fair degree of knowledge and understanding in relation to pensions and investments' but file contains no evidence client understands flexi-access drawdown withdrawal mechanics, tax crystallisation rules, or the 6 April 2027 IHT reform affecting unused pension funds. These are material features of the recommended product's complexity.
      - Report describes product complexity drivers including 'tax implications including marginal rate taxation on drawdowns after age 75' and 'inheritance tax treatment changes from 6 April 2027' but client knowledge evidence [P15] does not record client comprehension, questions, or adviser explanation of these tax features.
      - Report asserts 'multiple investment funds requiring ongoing monitoring' but investment selection evidence shows client will invest in two specified funds (Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity and HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic), not discretionary multi-fund selection requiring active ongoing monitoring.
    The report correctly identifies the product as complex. The Aviva Platform Personal Pension with flexi-access drawdown does carry material complexity: discretionary fund selection, variable withdrawal options with tax consequences varying by age, crystallisation timing decisions, and the pending 2027 IHT reform that brings pension death benefits into taxable estates — a significant change from current treatment. The client's background in accounting and currency trading supports competence with financial concepts, and documented self-directed pension investment and regular monitoring evidences some investment experience. However, a material gap exists between the complexity features and the evidence of client comprehension. The report states the client has 'fair degree of knowledge and understanding' but the file records no evidence the client understands: (1) flexi-access drawdown mechanics and withdrawal sequencing options; (2) the tax crystallisation on death after age 75 (a critical feature given the client is age 50 with 50-year life expectancy to age 100); (3) the 2027 IHT reform that will change the tax treatment of death benefits from tax-free inheritance to inclusion in the taxable estate. The report does not record client questions on these topics, adviser explanations of these features, or confirmation the client understands their implications for retirement planning. Category [I] evidence [P15] records that 'knowledge of tax implications' was discussed but does not evidence comprehension of the specific tax rules governing drawdown after age 75 or the IHT reform. The 2027 IHT reform is described in the report [P16] as affecting the recommendation (it is cited as a complexity driver and as affecting objectives OBJ6 and OBJ7), yet the file does not record whether the client was advised of this change or whether its impact on the suitability analysis was explained. The client's stated preference for 'flexible income over guaranteed income' aligns with drawdown architecture, but the file does not record discussion of the trade-offs: flexibility in access and withdrawal timing versus the annual income tax charges, versus the change in death benefit treatment from 2027. A Growth profile (7/10) with moderate capacity for loss is consistent with a recommendation for an equity-heavy portfolio (80% equity LifeStrategy), but this does not itself evidence comprehension of the drawdown mechanics or tax rules. The gap is not that the product is unsuitable for the client's knowledge — accounting and currency trading experience, combined with self-directed investment practice, suggests reasonable competence — but that the evidence of specific comprehension of the product's material complexities is absent. Where the report describes a product as complex and relies on complexity drivers that include tax crystallisation rules and regulatory changes, evidence that the client understood these features should be on file. A complex product recommended to a client recorded as having 'fair' but unspecified knowledge, without evidence of comprehension of its material features, presents a Potential Concern.

  [G4.4] Recommended product is too complex for the stated level of knowledge, or the knowledge level has been misinterpreted
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states the client has 'Good' knowledge level and that the recommended product complexity is 'Complex'. The report describes the client's background: 'Your background in accounting and currency trading has equipped you with a solid understanding of financial matters. You confirmed that you have invested in pensions without taking any advice within the last 5 years and you regularly check the value of your funds.' The report asserts: 'You feel you have a fair degree of knowledge and understanding in relation to [text truncated in source]'. The risk profile is assessed as 7/10 (Growth), and the report notes the client is 'a Growth investor' who 'typically look[s] for higher returns rather than safer investments and understand[s] that you need to take some risk to meet your investment goals.' The report justifies the recommended solution against investment pathways, stating it requires 'a more tailored solution than any of the four generic solutions' and reflects 'individual circumstances, withdrawal requirements and risk profile.'
    File:     The fact find and meeting summaries corroborate the accounting and currency trading background. [P13] states: 'You are a seasoned professional with a background in accounting and currency trading, which provides you with a solid understanding of financial matters. Your career as an accountant at Bridgeport Interiors Limited...Your experience as a currency trader in the city has equipped you with a keen awareness of market dynamics, although you do not currently maintain a stocks and shares portfolio.' [P14] confirms objectives include 'consolidating your various pension pots for better management and exploring tax-efficient strategies to optimise your retirement income' and interest in 'flexi-access drawdown and annuities.' The risk profile assessment [P3] describes the process: natural risk level from questionnaire mapped to asset classes, discussed with client, and 'we came to an agreed risk level for your specific investment objective.' [P12] shows the existing Nest holding is 'NEST Higher Risk Fund Pension' classified as 'Very Adventurous' with 10-year volatility of 17.68%, described as suited to clients who 'find financial matters easy to understand' and 'normally look for higher returns rather than safer investments.' No evidence appears in the file recording what the client understood about the specific recommended products, their features, risks, or structures. No category C (advice communication or comprehension record) passage addresses whether the client demonstrated understanding of complex product features, drawdown mechanics, switching implications, or the tailored asset allocation strategy being recommended.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the client has 'Good' knowledge and describes this as adequate for a 'Complex' recommended product, but the file records no demonstration of the client's understanding of the specific recommended products themselves, their structures, or complex features such as drawdown access, fund switching, platform mechanics, or the tailored portfolio construction being implemented.
      - The report justifies complexity by reference to the client's 'accounting and currency trading background' and states the client 'regularly check[s] the value of your funds', but the file explicitly notes the client 'do[es] not currently maintain a stocks and shares portfolio', which contradicts the implication of active investment management experience.
      - The report describes the recommended solution as properly tailored and more suitable than investment pathways because it meets 'individual circumstances, withdrawal requirements and risk profile', but provides no evidence the client was asked to or did demonstrate comprehension of the tailoring, the withdrawal mechanics, or how the solution differs functionally from the pathways rejected.
    The requirement asks whether the client's knowledge, experience and investment understanding are accurately reflected in the report and whether the recommended product's complexity is consistent with that knowledge, with adequate rationale. The report states 'Good' knowledge level grounded in accounting, currency trading background, and regular fund monitoring. The file corroborates the professional and trading background [P13] and confirms the client expressed interest in understanding options like drawdown and annuities [P14], indicating engagement with complexity. However, two material gaps emerge. First, the file explicitly records the client 'do[es] not currently maintain a stocks and shares portfolio' [P12], which weakens the inference that active investment management experience supports 'Good' knowledge of pension product complexity. The currency trading experience is market-awareness, not necessarily pension product or platform knowledge. Second, and more significant, the file contains no category C record (advice communication, comprehension record, or meeting summary) documenting that the client understood or demonstrated comprehension of the specific recommended products, their structures, or their complexity. The report recommends rejecting investment pathways on the basis that the client requires a 'more tailored solution' and regular review [P6], but offers no evidence the client was explained the complexity of that tailored solution or confirmed their understanding of it. The risk profile assessment process [P3] confirms discussion and agreement on risk level, but this is risk tolerance (CHK-003), not product comprehension. The existing Nest holding [P12] is described as 'Very Adventurous', suggesting some tolerance for complexity, but tolerance for risk is distinct from demonstrated knowledge of product structures. Where a complex product is recommended to a client recorded as having no current holdings in stocks and shares, the absence of a comprehension record—particularly one addressing the specific features and mechanics of the recommended solution—means the claim of adequacy of knowledge and justification is unsupported. The report's rationale relies on past professional background and stated intentions but not on evidence that the client understood what they were being recommended to do with it.

  [G4.5] Evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product, based on their questions and comprehension
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts three elements of the client's knowledge and understanding: (1) 'Your background in accounting and currency trading has equipped you with a solid understanding of financial matters' [P10, section Knowledge & Experience]; (2) 'you regularly check the value of your funds'; and (3) 'You feel you have a fair degree of knowledge and understanding in relation to pension planning.' The report positions these as evidence that the client possesses sufficient knowledge to evaluate the recommended products.
    File:     The case file contains multiple fact-find and meeting records spanning 16 January 2026 to 21 July 2026. P1 (20 February 2026 Fact Find) confirms the client's 'background in accounting and currency trading' and notes she 'provides you with a solid understanding of financial matters' and 'do[es] not currently maintain a stocks and shares portfolio.' P2 states the client is 'particularly interested in understanding the options available to you, such as the flexi-access drawdown and annuities.' P4 (16 January 2026 Fact Find) describes the client as engaging with advisers but frames her primary concern as 'transfer of your pension information' post-divorce. P6 records the risk profile as 'level seven, indicating a preference for growth-oriented investments' and notes discussion of 'implications of your risk profile' but records no detail of the client's questions, comprehension checks, or documented understanding of product features. P7 and P8 (21 July 2026 Ad-hoc Meeting) show the client was 'in a transitional phase after leaving her job' at age 55, discussing 'flexible drawdown and lump sum withdrawal' options and tax efficiency; Morgan Seddon discussed 'strategies for tax efficiency' and 'various pension options.' No evidence records client questions or demonstration of comprehension of the complex features of the recommended products (Vanguard LifeStrategy funds or any other investment vehicles recommended). P16 (Risk Profile Report) contains multiple blank sections under 'Attitude to Risk,' 'Investor Experience,' 'Capacity for loss questionnaire,' and 'Agreed risk level,' with notation 'No additional notes were recorded.'
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts client 'regularly checks the value of your funds' but file contains no evidence of this monitoring behaviour or its frequency; no portfolio statements or evidence of active engagement with existing holdings are recorded.
      - Report states client 'feel[s] you have a fair degree of knowledge and understanding in relation to pension planning' but file records no basis for or substantiation of this self-assessment; no exploration of what pension planning matters the client understands or why this degree of knowledge is 'fair' rather than limited.
      - Report asserts background in accounting and currency trading as evidence of 'solid understanding of financial matters' but file notes client 'do[es] not currently maintain a stocks and shares portfolio,' which contradicts any recent or active investment experience; the file does not record how historical trading experience translates to current pension product complexity.
    The requirement asks whether the client can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product based on their questions and comprehension. The report grounds this in three assertions: professional background, active monitoring, and self-assessed pension knowledge. Examination of the file reveals material gaps. First, the client's professional background in accounting and currency trading is confirmed in the file, but it is historical and does not establish current investment knowledge; the file explicitly states she maintains no stocks and shares portfolio, undermining claims of recent or active investment experience. Second, the assertion that she 'regularly checks the value of your funds' appears nowhere in the file; the fact-find records do not corroborate this monitoring behaviour. Third, the claim that she possesses 'fair degree of knowledge and understanding in relation to pension planning' is unsupported by any evidence of what she understands, how this was assessed, or why it is characterised as 'fair' rather than limited. The risk profile report (P16) contains multiple blank sections labelled 'No additional notes were recorded' precisely where comprehension, investor experience, or risk level justification would be documented. The file records that the client wished to 'understand the options' (P2) and shows advisers discussing tax efficiency and drawdown mechanisms (P7, P8), but nowhere does it record the client asking questions about product features, demonstrating comprehension, or articulating understanding of the specific risks and mechanics of Vanguard LifeStrategy funds or other recommended vehicles. The Vanguard KIID (P20, P21) sets out complex features including counterparty risk, liquidity risk, credit risk, inflation risk, and use of derivatives. No evidence in the file shows the client was walked through these, questioned on them, or demonstrated comprehension. The guard permits absence of recorded questions not to be proof of non-comprehension, but where the product is complex and the stated knowledge level is self-assessed as 'fair' (not explicitly graded in the file), and no documentation of comprehension testing exists, the evidence required by the requirement is absent. The report's claims rest on professional background and self-assessment; they are not corroborated by documented evidence of the client's ability to evaluate the specific products recommended.

  [G4.6] Existing holdings evidencing investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states the client 'have invested in pensions without taking any advice within the last 5 years' ([P1], page 6, Knowledge & Experience section). The report identifies three existing pension arrangements: a Quilter Personal Pension with holdings including True Potential Goldman Sachs Balanced (£2,787.29), True Potential Growth Aligned Aggressive (£10,764.88), True Potential Schroders Balanced (£2,378.80), True Potential SEI Aggressive (£7,254.00), True Potential Trinity Bridge Balanced (£7,771.29), and True Potential UBS Aggressive (£10,099.00) totalling £46,672.80 across True Potential funds in the Quilter platform; a True Potential Personal Pension with £260,000.00; and a Nest occupational DC scheme with £21,272.64. The holdings span Balanced and Aggressive fund classifications across multiple investment managers (Goldman Sachs, Schroders, SEI, Trinity Bridge, UBS). The fact find ([P16]) confirms 'Do you have any existing Personal Pension arrangements?: Yes' with Aviva Platform provider listed.
    File:     The case file contains six product factsheets describing the funds held: True Potential Schroders Balanced ([P4], OCF 1.19%, risk profile Balanced, 5+ year recommended holding period); True Potential UBS Aggressive ([P5], [P6], OCF 0.63%, risk profile Aggressive, 5 year minimum holding period); True Potential Growth Aligned Aggressive ([P10], OCF 0.54%, suitable for all investors under professional advice, 5 year minimum holding period); True Potential Goldman Sachs Balanced ([P13], Balanced fund with global equity and bond exposure); and Nest Higher Risk fund ([P8], [P9], [P11], [P12] describing Nest's fund range including Higher Risk fund, Guided Retirement Fund, and growth funds for different lifecycle stages). The Aviva Pension illustration ([P21], [P22]) dated 24.3.26 shows transfers totalling £327,945.44 (uncrystallised transfers of £46,672.80, £21,272.64, and £260,000.00) being invested across a portfolio including Vanguard Global Stock Index Fund (52.90%), Vanguard Emerging Markets (16.90%), CT Emerging Market Bond (7.30%), and other holdings ([P19] showing Growth risk profile allocation). The fact find ([P16]) lists an Aviva pension (Personal Pension Plan, AV2966413-001, start date 13 [incomplete]) under Personal Pensions section. No detailed prior investment history, no record of self-directed trades, no evidence of market analysis or investment decision-making is present in the file.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts 5 years of pension investment experience 'without taking any advice'; file shows current arrangements were set up through advisers (Quilter platform described as adviser-serviced per [P20] Aviva Terms stating 'Pension Portfolio is aimed at Clients with Advisers'; True Potential funds described in factsheets as 'suitable for investors under the advice of a professional adviser').
      - Report identifies holdings across True Potential funds (Goldman Sachs Balanced, Growth Aligned Aggressive, Schroders Balanced, SEI Aggressive, Trinity Bridge Balanced, UBS Aggressive) in a Quilter arrangement totalling £46,672.80; Aviva pension illustration ([P22]) shows the same transfers (£46,672.80, £21,272.64, £260,000.00) but proposes to reinvest them in a completely different portfolio structure (Vanguard indices 52.90%, Emerging Markets 16.90%, etc.), indicating the current holdings are about to be replaced, not held forward.
      - Report describes existing holdings as evidence of 5 years' unaided investment experience; file provides no evidence of when these holdings were acquired, by whom, on what basis, or whether the client selected them independently. Aviva illustration ([P21]) shows 'Intended retirement date: 15 December 2030' and transfers 'Uncrystallised' from existing arrangements, confirming these are crystallisation events (transfers out), not ongoing evidence of active management or prior independent investment decisions.
      - No record in fact find ([P16]) of prior investment experience beyond the pension question 'Do you have any existing Personal Pension arrangements?: Yes'. No investment history, share dealing experience, or prior non-pension holdings documented.
    The report claims 5 years of unaided pension investment experience, grounded in the assertion that the client 'have invested in pensions without taking any advice within the last 5 years' ([I] quote from report). However, the existing arrangements reveal material inconsistencies: (1) The holdings are held through adviser-serviced platforms (Aviva Pension Portfolio Terms [P20] state the arrangement 'is aimed at Clients with Advisers'); the True Potential fund factsheets ([P5], [P10]) explicitly describe suitability 'for investors under the advice of a professional adviser'. The phrase 'without taking any advice' contradicts the structural reality that these holdings sit on platforms designed for advised clients. (2) The claim of '5 years' experience cannot be corroborated against the holdings themselves: the file contains no dates of acquisition, fund selection rationales, or evidence the client made independent investment decisions. Holding a pension is not the same as understanding it or choosing its contents ([guard: 'Holding a product is not the same as understanding it. Holdings corroborate experience; they are not proof of knowledge']). (3) The current holdings are in transition: the Aviva illustration ([P21], [P22]) dated 24.3.26 shows these exact arrangements being transferred (uncrystallised transfers out) and reinvested into a new portfolio structure. This is a crystallisation event triggered by the suitability advice itself, not evidence of autonomous long-term holdings. (4) The holdings themselves — a mix of Balanced and Aggressive funds across different managers (Goldman Sachs, Schroders, SEI, Trinity Bridge, UBS) with allocations ranging 5.97% to 23.05% — show diversity but no documented rationale. The file contains no fact-find responses recording investment knowledge level, attitude to risk, or prior experience with specific asset classes or fund types. The report's assertion rests on the single phrase in the Knowledge & Experience section; the supporting evidence (holdings + arrangement structure + documentation) contradicts rather than corroborates the 'without advice' claim. The guard applies: 'An arrangement inherited or set up by a previous adviser says little about the client's own experience.' The file does not show whether the client selected these funds, understood their risk profiles, or independently rebalanced them over 5 years. The holdings evidence ownership of pensions, not autonomous investment experience.

  [G4.7] Adequacy of the rationale supporting a complex recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that recommended products are Complex, driven by: pension platform with discretionary fund selection, flexi-access drawdown with variable withdrawal options, tax implications including marginal rate taxation on drawdowns after age 75, inheritance tax treatment changes from 6 April 2027, and multiple investment funds requiring ongoing monitoring. The report provides multiple rationales for the recommendations: lower cost structures (Quilter platform charges 0.27% vs receiving scheme 0.19%, True Potential 0.40% vs Aviva 0.21%), client preference to leave Quilter, ongoing monitoring capability, risk alignment (current portfolio not in line with agreed risk level, new scheme offers wider range of investment options), and ability to adjust investments for changing needs and market conditions.
    File:     The evidence shows the client is described as a seasoned professional with background in accounting and currency trading, providing solid understanding of financial matters [P2]. The client's objectives include consolidating pension pots, exploring tax-efficient strategies, understanding flexi-access drawdown and annuities options, and developing comprehensive retirement strategy aligned with risk profile [P3]. Risk profile assessed as level 7 indicating preference for growth-oriented investments [P1]. Meeting records confirm discussion of risk profile, investment preferences, and pension fund options [P1, P4]. The client confirmed comfort with risk level associated with investment strategy [P1]. However, no evidence in the file records specific client questions, discussions or demonstrations of understanding regarding the complexity drivers identified in the report: discretionary fund selection mechanics, flexi-access drawdown variable withdrawal options, tax implications of drawdowns after age 75, or inheritance tax treatment changes from 6 April 2027. The fact find records show standard information gathering but no specific exploration or client comprehension documentation of complex product features.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report identifies five specific complexity drivers but file evidence does not show client questions, discussions or documented understanding of these specific features
      - Report states recommendations based on risk alignment and wider investment options, but risk profile justification section [P21] shows blank notes for multiple discussion areas including Attitude to Risk, Investor Experience, Capacity for loss questionnaire, Investment target, and Capacity for loss for the goal
      - Report emphasizes ongoing monitoring as rationale but no documented protocol or client instruction regarding monitoring frequency, triggers or adjustment procedures
      - Report cites client preference for leaving Quilter but no documentary evidence of this preference in the file beyond the assertion itself
    The report identifies the recommended products as Complex and lists five specific complexity drivers. It provides business rationales focused on costs, client preference, and capability to monitor and adjust. However, the evidence standard for complex products requires corroboration in category C (meeting records) showing client comprehension or discussion of the complex features themselves. The file shows: (1) The client has relevant financial background (accounting, currency trading) which supports capability to understand complexity [P2]. (2) Risk profile was discussed and client confirmed comfort with level 7 growth orientation [P1, P4]. (3) However, the risk profile justification document [P21] contains systematic blank entries for the discussion notes on Attitude to Risk, Investor Experience, Capacity for loss, Investment target, and Capacity for loss for the goal — indicating either incomplete fact-finding or documentation failure. (4) The specific complexity drivers — discretionary fund selection, drawdown mechanics, tax treatment after 75, inheritance tax changes, and multi-fund monitoring — are identified in the report but not evidenced in meeting records as subjects of client discussion or comprehension. (5) The cost-based rationales are business justifications but do not address suitability of the product's complexity to the client's knowledge. The report's assertion that the client has 'solid understanding of financial matters' and 'keen awareness of market dynamics' [P2] provides some foundation for complexity tolerance, but this general characterisation does not substitute for specific evidence that the client understood or was adequately explained the particular complexities of flexi-access drawdown, variable withdrawal options, tax implications, and discretionary fund selection. The absence of documented discussion of these features in category C evidence represents a gap between the complexity identified and evidence of informed client comprehension. Where a product is complex, the adequacy of rationale requires not only business justification but evidence that the client's knowledge supports the complexity — either through documented discussion or through demonstrated prior experience. The general statement of financial background is present but specific comprehension of the identified complexity drivers is not evidenced.

Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G5.1, G5.2, G5.3, G5.4, G5.5, G5.6, G5.7, G5.8).   [G5.1] Affordability of initial and ongoing commitments
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   Initial commitment: £67,945.44 (transfer to Aviva Pension). Ongoing commitment: £0 (nil portfolio contributions after transfer). The report asserts affordability based on: (1) pension funds plus inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement even following market falls [P11, P16]; (2) client has option to work beyond age 60 or continue part-time if necessary [P16]. Monthly expenditure modelled at £2,237 until retirement, then £2,000 net per month in retirement. Client receives partner contribution of £800 monthly. Inheritance of £350,000 anticipated next year [P11]. Adviser fee: 3% initial (£9,838.36) and 1% ongoing [P10, P15].
    File:     Fact Find [P17, P14]: Current household expenditure £2,986 per month with partner contribution of £600 (not £800). Client's personal share approximately £2,386. Total assets £587,000 [P23]. Current pension value circa £300,000 [P21]. Emergency reserves currently £7,000, forecast to drop to £4,000 by pension setup; client prefers 12-month reserve (~£24,000) [P14]. Future inheritance £150,000 stated in Fact Find [P21], not £350,000. Client expects to work part-time from start 2027, estimating minimum £20,000 per annum income [P14]. Tax-free cash (TFC) withdrawal planned: £40,000 total—£20,000 for emergency funds and legal fees, £20,000 to clear personal loan and credit card (saving ~£480/month ongoing) [P14]. Current savings reduced to £7,000 and 'making her feel uncomfortable' [P14]. No mortgage stated in Fact Find protection section [P24], but cashflow model includes £750/month mortgage for 7 years [P11].
    Discrepancies:
      - Inheritance amount: Report models £350,000 [P11]; Fact Find states £150,000 [P21]—226% difference.
      - Partner's monthly contribution: Report uses £800; Fact Find shows £600 [P17]—33% difference.
      - Household expenditure: Report uses £2,237 personal; Fact Find total is £2,986 with £600 partner contribution, implying client's share ~£2,386—not clearly reconciled.
      - Emergency fund status: Report asserts affordability without quantifying emergency reserves; Fact Find shows client anxiety about reserves depleting to £4,000 when 12-month target (~£24,000) is preferred [P14].
      - Initial commitment basis: Report states £67,945.44 transfer but this figure is not corroborated in retrieved passages. Nearest figure in passages is £260,000 (Quilter), £46,672.80 (True Potential), or £21,272.64 (Nest)—total existing arrangements £327,945.44. The £67,945.44 appears to be derived (£327,945.44 − £260,000 = £67,945.44), but basis not explained in report.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Reconciliation of inheritance amount (£350,000 vs £150,000) not resolved in any document., Explanation of how £67,945.44 initial commitment was calculated or which existing funds are being transferred., Clarification of partner contribution basis (£600 or £800 monthly)., Detailed breakdown reconciling household expenditure (£2,986 total) with modelled personal expenditure (£2,237) and post-debt-clearance impact (£480 saving)., Confirmation of mortgage term and ongoing liability (£750/month stated for 7 years but not confirmed in Fact Find)., Quantification of emergency fund adequacy post-transfer and whether TFC withdrawal of £20,000 meets client's stated 12-month reserve target., Documentation of state pension entitlement and age 60 retirement feasibility assessment.
    The requirement tests whether affordability is detailed and consistent. Two material factual discrepancies prevent a reliable comparison: (1) The inheritance is modelled at more than double the Fact Find amount (£350,000 vs £150,000). This directly affects the retirement cashflow calculation the report cites as the basis for affordability. (2) The partner contribution differs by 33% (£800 vs £600), affecting monthly surplus calculation. (3) The initial commitment figure of £67,945.44 cannot be verified from the passages provided. Existing arrangements total £327,945.44; the report's figure may be a residual or portion, but the derivation is not evidenced in the case file. The report asserts that the pension transfer, combined with inheritance, provides sufficient security for retirement expenditure and that emergency reserves will be adequate [P11], but: (i) the inheritance figure contradicts the Fact Find; (ii) the emergency fund is asserted as adequate without quantification, while the Fact Find shows the client currently has £7,000 (forecast to £4,000), well short of her stated 12-month target of ~£24,000 [P14]; (iii) the TFC withdrawal of £20,000 is planned for emergency and legal fees, leaving only remaining inheritance to cover the shortfall—this is not explicitly modelled. The report's affordability narrative [P11, P16] relies on cashflow projections that appear to incorporate the incorrect inheritance amount and does not reconcile with the Fact Find's stated emergency fund concerns. The comparison cannot be performed reliably because: the key input (inheritance) is materially inconsistent, the partner contribution differs, and the initial commitment quantum is not traced to source documents. A false-positive guard notes that a transfer of existing pension money is not a new affordability commitment, and this applies here—the £67,945.44 is a reallocation of existing funds. However, the guard does not excuse the discrepancy between stated and evidenced figures, nor does it support the affordability assertion when the inheritance amount and emergency fund position contradict the report's implicit assumptions. No vulnerability overlay issues specific to this check have been flagged in the evidence.
