  [G5.2] Financial situation plus cashflow supports the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts total assets of £797,000 (comprising main residence £430,000, cash £17,000, and expected inheritance £350,000), total liabilities of £70,000, and net asset value of £377,000 as at 2026-03-24. It models cashflow to age 100 incorporating: earnings of £45,000 per annum until retirement at age 60; personal expenditure of £2,237 per month until retirement; mortgage payments of £750 per month for 7 years; one-off expenditure of £2,500 (sofa); inheritance of £350,000 received next year; retirement expenditure of £2,000 net per month; state pension from age 67; and 6% investment growth. The baseline scenario shows no shortfall to age 100. Stress tests show: high inflation at 6% per annum causes asset depletion at age 81; a 2007-2008 market crash at pension access results in no shortfall; and against ONS life expectancy of age 87 for a 55-year-old female, funds would last throughout lifetime. The report states 'your assets are sufficient to make up your income shortfall throughout your lifetime' and recommends flexible income drawdown via Aviva with liquid cash held for emergency access. Client has capacity for loss assessed as moderate; pension funds plus inheritance sufficient to cover retirement expenditure even after market fall; and option to work beyond age 60 or part-time.
    File:     The Fact Find records total assets of £587,000 (main residence £430,000, cash £7,000, potential inheritance £150,000 estimated in 2-3 years, maybe sooner). Liabilities total £70,000 (mortgage £600 monthly to age 65, unsecured debts £175 and £308 monthly). Current household monthly expenditure is £2,986 with partner Tony contributing £600 per month, implying client personal expenditure of approximately £2,386. Mortgage term ends at age 65 (7 years from current age 55). Income is £45,000 gross per annum; partner contributes £800 monthly other income. ONS life expectancy for a 55-year-old female is 87 years. The Fact Find shows monthly cash reserve position but does not detail emergency fund provision or quantified cashflow modelling. The report notes the client recently stopped working through self-employment (July 2026 update) due to frustration and disagreement. Profile records client has commitment to family, type 2 diabetes managed, lost three stones, never smoked. Partner Tony and household maintain separate financial arrangements.
    Discrepancies:
      - Total assets: report states £797,000 (including projected inheritance of £350,000 next year); Fact Find shows £587,000 (inheritance stated as £150,000 in 2-3 years, maybe sooner)—difference of £210,000 in total and £200,000 in inheritance amount and timing certainty
      - Cash reserves: report states £17,000; Fact Find shows £7,000—difference of £10,000
      - Inheritance timing: report assumes receipt 'next year' (within 12 months, year 2 of projection); Fact Find states '2-3 years time, maybe sooner'—creates uncertainty over cashflow modelling timing
      - Inheritance amount: report uses £350,000; Fact Find shows £150,000—£200,000 discrepancy
      - Monthly expenditure: report states £2,237 personal expenditure used in cashflow modelling until retirement; Fact Find shows total household expenditure £2,986 with partner's £600 contribution, implying client share of £2,386—difference of £149 and basis ambiguity (net vs gross, personal vs household)
      - Retirement expenditure: report models £2,000 net per month in retirement; Fact Find current personal expenditure £2,386, creating question of whether £2,000 is realistic or whether inflation/lifestyle changes are factored
      - Mortgage term: report states £750 per month for 7 years; Fact Find confirms £600 primary mortgage plus £175 and £308 other liabilities monthly, totalling £1,083 not £750, and end date age 65 in loan term field
      - Emergency fund quantification: report asserts 'liquid cash' is held for emergency access and states this within capacity for loss assessment; no specific emergency fund size or adequacy metric is disclosed in either document
      - Stress test resilience: report shows high inflation at 6% causes depletion at age 81 (26 years into retirement, before ONS life expectancy of 87); this is flagged as a shortfall outcome in the stress scenario, inconsistent with the conclusion that assets are 'sufficient throughout lifetime' under baseline
      - Recent employment change: Fact Find July 2026 update shows client 'stopped working through self employment'—report dated 23/03/2026 does not reflect this change; current employment status at report date versus later update creates question whether cashflow assumptions (£45,000 earnings to age 60) remain valid
    The comparison reveals material discrepancies between the Fact Find and the suitability report's assertion of affordability. The two largest are inheritance (£200,000 difference) and timing (next year vs 2-3 years). These are not rounding differences but reflect fundamentally different assumptions about available resources. The report's net asset value of £377,000 depends critically on which inheritance figure and timing is correct: using £150,000 received in 2-3 years would reduce the present value available for modelling, and deferring receipt by 1-2 years changes the cashflow profile at the critical retirement transition (age 60). The report's assertion that 'assets are sufficient throughout your lifetime' rests on the baseline scenario showing no shortfall to age 100. However, the high inflation stress test (6% per annum, explicitly modelled by the report itself) shows asset depletion at age 81—before the client's life expectancy of 87. The report does not reconcile this: it presents the 6% inflation test as a stress scenario but does not revise its sufficiency conclusion or flag that high inflation creates material risk. This is internal inconsistency: a stress outcome showing 6-year shortfall before life expectancy contradicts the headline 'sufficient throughout lifetime' unless the baseline (2% inflation) is explicitly treated as the only acceptable scenario and high inflation as tail risk. The report does not make this distinction clear. The monthly expenditure ambiguity (£2,237 vs £2,386) is material to affordability. The Fact Find records household total of £2,986 with partner contributing £600, implying the client's share is £2,386 (approximately). The report uses £2,237 in cashflow modelling. The £149 monthly difference (£1,788 annually) compounds over 40+ years of retirement. The extraction note flags this as an ambiguity: whether £2,237 is adjusted forecast or client-only net of partner's contribution. The mortgage liability is also unclear: report states £750 per month for 7 years, but Fact Find shows three separate liabilities (£600, £175, £308 monthly), totalling £1,083. The report does not clarify whether the £750 is principal mortgage only or whether the other liabilities are separate or included. This affects the modelled cashflow accuracy. The cash reserve position is weaker than reported: £7,000 (Fact Find) vs £17,000 (report)—a 58% difference. For a client with £2,237 monthly expenditure, £7,000 covers 3.1 months of pre-retirement spending or 3.5 months post-retirement. Neither document specifies a target emergency fund or comments on adequacy. The July 2026 Fact Find update states the client 'stopped working through self employment'—this post-dates the report (23/03/2026) and is unexplained. If the client has ceased employment, the assumption of £45,000 earnings to age 60 (5 years away) becomes questionable. The profile notes frustration with work and disagreement; the July update suggests employment has ended entirely. If so, the modelled income is not available to support the mortgage and expenditure until state pension and drawdown begin. This is a material change not reflected in the cashflow. The inheritance timing is especially problematic: the Fact Find states 'estimated to be in 2-3 years time, maybe sooner' and notes 'currently pending legal proceedings'. If legal proceedings delay receipt beyond the 2-3 year window, or if the amount is reduced, the baseline cashflow model is invalidated. The report uses next-year receipt as a core assumption but does not flag the legal uncertainty. On stress testing: the report's own stress scenarios show that under high inflation (6% per annum), assets are depleted at age 81. The client's life expectancy is 87. Under this scenario, the client would face 6 years without adequate assets. The report does not quantify the risk of high inflation or address whether 6% inflation is foreseeable or tail risk. It also does not propose a mitigating strategy (e.g., annuity purchase at certain age, reduced spending triggers, or part-time work). This leaves the sufficiency conclusion unsupported in stress conditions. The capacity for loss is assessed as 'moderate' based on cashflow analysis showing funds sufficient even after market fall. However, this is circular reasoning if the underlying cashflow assumptions (inheritance timing, cash reserves, employment income, expenditure) are uncertain or have changed. On charges: the report recommends Aviva consolidation and drawdown. The critical yield analysis in the report shows Aviva is more expensive than existing arrangements (True Potential and Nest). The report does not reconcile the cost difference with the 6% growth assumption or show how drawdown income will sustain affordability after charges. The emergency fund is mentioned only in passing ('you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds') and not quantified or assessed against a stated sufficiency target. For a client approaching retirement with limited emergency reserves (£7,000) and one-off expenditure planned (£2,500 sofa), the fund adequacy is not demonstrated. In summary: the report asserts affordability on baseline cashflow modelling, but material inputs differ from the Fact Find or have changed since the report date. The inheritance (amount and timing) is the largest uncertainty and is not flagged. The high inflation stress test contradicts the sufficiency headline. Employment income is assumed to continue but the client has reportedly stopped working. Expenditure assumptions are ambiguous (£2,237 vs £2,386). Cash reserves are lower than reported (£7,000 vs £17,000). The emergency fund is not quantified. Charges are not integrated into the affordability assessment. The comparison can be performed, but material discrepancies prevent the report's sufficiency conclusion from being fully supported by the file.

  [G5.3] Emergency fund and liquidity needs, foreseeable and unforeseeable
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an emergency fund of £17,000 held as cash (as at 23/03/2026), states this is confirmed sufficient, and quotes the client: 'You are comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds.' The report identifies planned retirement access at age 60 (from 2031-03-24) requiring £25,000–£30,000 annual net income. It recommends Flexi-Access Drawdown via an Aviva Platform wrapper to provide regular income drawdown. The cashflow analysis is presented graphically but key modelling inputs are stated as blank in the report template ('We have input the following information into the cashflow modeller to generate the charts below:: [blank]'). The report does not quantify a required emergency fund level or explicitly reconcile the £17,000 held against any stated requirement.
    File:     The Fact Find (1).md records total household monthly expenditure of £2,836.23 as at 21/07/2026, with the client's personal net monthly income stated as £800.00, leaving a monthly disposable deficit of £2,036.23. The Fact Find notes that the client 'is currently living off savings which is now reduced to £7,000 and this also acts as an emergency pot. She prefers to have 12 months of expenditure as an emergency reserve so close to £24,000.' The client states 'The £7,000 will likely be down to circa £4,000 by the time the pension income is set up and this is making her feel uncomfortable.' The Fact Find also records that the client wishes to withdraw a tax-free cash lump sum of £40,000, of which '£20,000 to replace emergency funds.' Total assets (including all Savings and Investments) are stated as £587,000.00. The note 'Cash reserves will be replenished through TFC [Tax Free Cash] and barrister fees covered for the foreseeable future' appears, but the underlying liabilities (personal loan, credit card) total £88,000 before the proposed £20,000 paydown. The report's cashflow modelling inputs are marked blank, preventing verification of how projected income, expenditure and asset adequacy were tested.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts emergency fund of £17,000 as at 23/03/2026; Fact Find dated 21/07/2026 records savings reduced to £7,000 with projection to circa £4,000 by pension income setup date.
      - Report states client will receive £25,000–£30,000 annual retirement income; Fact Find indicates planned regular taxable income of £1,900 per month (£22,800 per annum) plus partner contribution of £800 per month (£9,600 per annum, total household £32,400), but client's personal income share is closer to £22,800–£25,000.
      - Report does not quantify emergency fund requirement; Fact Find explicitly states client preference for 12 months expenditure (approximately £24,000 based on £2,836.23 monthly expenditure) versus the £17,000 held.
      - Report presents cashflow analysis graphically but template shows modelling inputs as blank ([blank]), preventing verification of how affordability was calculated or what assumptions underpin the income and expenditure projections.
      - Report records current household total expenditure of £2,986 per household with partner's £600 contribution (implied in Fact Find affordability modelling), but Fact Find shows total monthly household expenditure as £2,836.23, a difference of approximately £150 unreconciled.
    The requirement tests whether affordability is fully supported and consistent with evidence. Multiple material inconsistencies emerge:
    
    1. Emergency Fund Trajectory: The report records £17,000 as at 23/03/2026. However, the Fact Find (dated 21/07/2026, approximately 4 months later) records savings already reduced to £7,000 with further decline anticipated to £4,000 by the time of pension income setup. This contradicts the stability implied by the report's statement of sufficiency. The client is quoted in the Fact Find as 'making her uncomfortable' about this decline, indicating the level is below her needs.
    
    2. Emergency Fund Requirement: The Fact Find explicitly states the client 'prefers to have 12 months of expenditure as an emergency reserve so close to £24,000.' The report asserts the £17,000 is 'confirmed sufficient' but provides no justification, quantified requirement, or evidence of discussion reconciling the stated preference (£24,000) against the held amount (£17,000). The deficiency is material: approximately £7,000 short of stated preference.
    
    3. Expenditure Consistency: Report references show household expenditure of approximately £2,986 (£2,986 less £600 partner contribution noted in one section equals £2,386 client share, cited in extraction report as approximate to £2,237 used in modelling). The Fact Find records total household monthly expenditure as £2,836.23. The report does not reconcile these figures or explain whether £2,237 is a forecast reduction or a different basis (e.g., excluding certain items or adjusted for planned changes post-TFC withdrawal).
    
    4. Retirement Income: The report asserts target of £25,000–£30,000 annual net income. The Fact Find notes planned regular income of £1,900 per month from pension (£22,800 per annum) plus £800 partner contribution per month (£9,600 per annum), totalling £32,400 household or approximately £22,800 client-only. The lower end of the report's stated range (£25,000) is not clearly supported by the documented £1,900 monthly pension income figure; the Fact Find shows the client is "very close to overall expenditure for the month" once this income is received, implying limited surplus.
    
    5. Cashflow Modelling: The report's cashflow analysis states 'We have input the following information into the cashflow modeller to generate the charts below:: [blank]' and 'Any input information is based on the assumptions detailed at the end of this report:: [blank]'. The template shows modelling inputs as entirely blank, preventing verification of assumptions, drawdown rates, asset depletion timescales, or stress-test parameters. The extraction report notes the cashflow shows stress scenarios including "6% inflation and 2007/8-style market crash" but these are not quantified in the evidence. Without visibility of inputs, the affordability conclusion cannot be corroborated.
    
    6. Tax-Free Cash Use: The Fact Find records the client wishes to withdraw £40,000 TFC, of which £20,000 to "replace emergency funds" and £20,000 to pay off liabilities. If £20,000 is deployed to liabilities, the emergency fund rebuilding is dependent on that being completed before further drawdown occurs. The report does not sequence these uses or confirm the timing of when the emergency fund is replenished.
    
    7. Vulnerability Overlay: The Fact Find describes the client as having "lost employment income," currently "living off savings," and holding savings that are "making her feel uncomfortable." These indicators of financial vulnerability are not explicitly considered in the report's sufficiency statement. The client is transitioning to pension income with a partner contribution, reducing income flexibility. The affordability assessment does not address whether this configuration provides adequate resilience for unforeseeable needs.
    
    Guard Application: The guard "An asserted sufficiency with no quantified requirement cannot be verified — record it as unquantified rather than as a contradiction" applies: the report asserts the £17,000 emergency fund is sufficient but provides no stated requirement benchmark. However, the Fact Find explicitly documents a stated requirement (12 months expenditure ≈ £24,000), creating an inconsistency between the report's assertion and evidence of the client's stated need.
    
    Guard Application: "Cash held may be earmarked elsewhere in the file; check for competing claims on the same money." The £17,000 is treated solely as emergency fund in the report. No competing claim is evident in the passages.

  [G5.4] Initial and ongoing commitments are unaffordable based on income, expenditure and cashflow
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an initial commitment of £67,945.44 (a transfer into Aviva Pension) and an ongoing commitment of £0 (Nil portfolio contribution). The report states affordability is supported by: (1) 'Based on your circumstances alone, your pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement, even following a fall in the market' (page 6, Capacity for loss section); and (2) 'Additionally you have the option to work beyond age 60 or to continue to work part time if necessary' (page 6). The report concludes the recommendation is affordable and suitable.
    File:     The Fact Find [P1] shows: monthly partner's contribution £800.00; detailed expenditure analysis elected; total net monthly expenditure itemised by category. Meeting Summary [P18, P19] records: client's employment income approximately £2,900 net per month before leaving job; annual income was approximately £45,000; anticipated return to part-time work January with estimated salary £20,000 (implying approximately £1,250–£1,300 net monthly); preference for 12-month emergency buffer; inheritance of £150,000 anticipated (complicated by legal challenges and potential £20,000 contribution required); £20,000 cash buffer intended; existing debts: credit card £7,000, personal loan £13,000. Fact Find [P21] itemises expenditure including £600 mortgage, £440 car PCP, £200 fuel, £300 food/personal care, £221 council tax, £120 electricity, £42 water, £20 telephone, £61 TV/internet, £15 TV licence, £41.40 sports, £3,000 annually holidays (£250 monthly), £150 entertainment, £30 life insurance. The extraction report notes ambiguity: two different monthly expenditure figures stated—£2,986 current household total (with partner's £600 contribution) and £2,237 personal expenditure used in cashflow modelling. Fact Find [P24] states: 'capacity for loss is medium to high, however i feel this is leaning more to medium as if the withdrawals continue at the projected rate of £1,900 per month this will reduce longevity. Once her state pension kicks in at age 67, half of her retirement expenditure will be covered by this leaving the requirement of circa £12,000 per annum.'
    Discrepancies:
      - Monthly expenditure: two figures in evidence—£2,986 household total (with partner £600 contribution, implying client share ~£2,386) versus £2,237 used in cashflow modelling; relationship not clarified
      - Monthly surplus: Fact Find shows projected £1,900 monthly withdrawal rate (from [P24]) against two different expenditure baselines; no monthly surplus figure extracted or stated in report
      - Employment income: client earned ~£2,900 net monthly before leaving job; plan to return part-time at estimated £20,000 annual (net ~£1,250–£1,300 monthly); no reconciliation of affordability across employment transition
      - Inheritance: £150,000 anticipated but subject to legal challenge and potential £20,000 outlay; report treats as certain future income but Meeting Summary records contingency
      - Emergency reserves: Meeting Summary records preference for 12-month buffer; Fact Find and report do not quantify emergency fund requirement or confirm adequacy post-transfer
      - Initial transfer amount £67,945.44: no passage corroborates this exact figure; nearest figure is £70,000.00 life insurance sum assured [P23], 3% away but unrelated
      - Report basis of affordability: asserts pension funds + inheritance sufficient but does not detail monthly surplus, projected expenditure in retirement, or reconcile against stated withdrawal rate of £1,900/month
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Monthly net surplus or deficit: required to test whether ongoing nil commitment is affordable; not stated in report or extracted from cashflow model, Reconciled current expenditure figure: two figures in evidence (£2,986 household vs £2,237 personal); relationship and client's attributable share not established, Confirmed monthly net income post-employment transition: return to part-time work £20,000 annual is estimated; net monthly equivalent not calculated or reconciled against expenditure, Quantified emergency fund requirement: Meeting Summary notes preference for 12-month buffer but no figure asserted or supported in report, Inheritance timing and certainty: described as 'anticipated' and subject to legal challenge; report treats as certain without qualification of delay or contingency risk, Longevity cashflow model: Fact Find [P24] references projected withdrawal rate of £1,900/month but full annual cashflow projection is not provided in evidence pack, State Pension start date and amount: report assumes State Pension at 67 but does not state anticipated annual amount; Fact Find [P24] assumes 'half of retirement expenditure' covered (~£12,000 p.a.) but baseline expenditure not fixed
    The requirement tests whether initial and ongoing commitments are supported by evidenced income, expenditure and cashflow. The report asserts affordability qualitatively (pension funds plus inheritance will be sufficient; flexibility to work longer) but does not detail the monthly cashflow on which this conclusion rests. Comparison cannot be fully performed because the report does not extract or state a monthly surplus or deficit figure, and the case file contains two unreconciled expenditure figures (£2,986 household total with partner contribution vs £2,237 personal modelling figure). The relationship between these is ambiguous per the extraction report's own note. Fact Find [P24] records a projected withdrawal rate of £1,900/month and notes that capacity for loss is 'medium to high, however...leaning more to medium' if withdrawals continue at this rate, deteriorating longevity until State Pension at 67. This contradicts the report's unqualified assertion of affordability. The initial transfer of £67,945.44 cannot be corroborated from any passage (nearest figure is £70,000 life insurance sum assured, unrelated). Meeting Summary [P18, P19] establishes: client left employment earning ~£2,900 net monthly; plans to return part-time January at estimated £20,000 annual; has debts of £20,000 (£7k credit card, £13k personal loan); intends £20,000 cash buffer; anticipates £150,000 inheritance (contingent on legal challenge resolution, with potential £20,000 outlay). No monthly surplus is calculated against expenditure in either income state (unemployed transitional phase vs part-time post-January). The report's affordability basis—'pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement'—relies on the inheritance being received, on-time, and undiminished by legal challenges or sister's contribution. It does not address the gap between now (transitional phase) and retirement, or reconcile the £1,900/month withdrawal rate against evidenced household and personal expenditure. The emergency fund is mentioned in Meeting Summary as desired at 12-month level but never quantified. Fact Find [P24] cautions that medium capacity for loss applies 'if the withdrawals continue at the projected rate...will reduce longevity', which is a material qualification absent from the report's conclusion. The ongoing nil commitment is supported if no further contributions are made, but the initial transfer feasibility (drawing £67,945.44 from which arrangements, at what net cost, and against what monthly surplus) is not evidenced. No passage supports the exact transfer amount or its affordability from current liquid resources. Given the extraction's recorded ambiguities about the expenditure baseline and the guards around employment income ceasing (client has left employment, returns part-time), a full cashflow comparison cannot be made. The report's assertion of affordability is not contradicted by a single clear monthly surplus figure because no such figure is stated or extracted; instead, the evidence holds contradictory indicators: Fact Find [P24] warns withdrawal rate reduces longevity if sustained; Meeting Summary records contingent inheritance and dual income scenarios; two expenditure figures remain unreconciled. A comparison that cannot be made because a required input is missing is not a false finding; it is a legitimate absence of support.

  [G5.5] Emergency funds not taken into account, or depleted or unavailable when required
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that emergency funds are retained: [P3] shows a cashflow chart displaying 'Emergency funds' of £600,000 with separate lines for 'DMB Aviva Pension (UFPLS)', 'DMB savings', and 'State pension (Melanie)'. [P2] states 'You are comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds' and references a 10-15 working day access timeframe for the recommended Aviva pension. [P1] confirms 'your pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement, even following a fall in the market' and notes capacity for loss is 'moderate' based on having 'sufficient funds for retirement'. The report identifies liquidity needs as funded from a 'Personal pension on Aviva Platform' [extract liquidityAndAccessNeeds]. The fact find [P5] states 'Your monthly expenditure totals £2,986 and Tony contributes £600 per month towards the household bills', yielding net personal expenditure of £2,386 monthly (£28,632 annually). The suitability report's cashflow modelling [P4] references income of '£45,000 per ann' and uses unspecified 'information' for modelling.
    File:     [P5] (Fact Find) records current household expenditure of £2,986 monthly with partner contribution of £600, implying client's net share of £2,386 monthly. The extraction notes [ambiguities] identify two different monthly expenditure figures used in different contexts: '£2,986 current household total with partner's £600 contribution, and £2,237 personal expenditure used in cashflow modelling'. [P21] (Meeting Summary) confirms risk profile 7 ('adventurous') and notes 'willingness to accept a higher level of risk in pursuit of potentially greater returns' and 'readiness to accept moderate capital loss in the short term'. [P1] states the client 'recently divorced' and 'recently altered your employment status, going from employed to self-employed with the same company' with expected earnings 'around £45,000 gross going forward'. [P5] notes inheritance of £350,000 'currently pending legal proceedings'. The Aviva pension terms [P13], [P14], [P16] establish that access to drawdown income 'will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete' and that insufficient cash requires automatic disinvestment with additional charges (greater of £10 or 10% of disinvestment). [P11] shows total existing arrangements value £67,945 (True Potential £46,672 + Nest £21,272) transferring to Aviva at £65,907, a reduction of £2,038. No document evidences the quantified value, deployment strategy, or accessibility timeline for the stated £600,000 emergency fund. [P9] provides ONS data on life expectancy (female aged 55: 1 in 4 chance of living to 95, 1 in 10 chance to 99 years) but no personalised cashflow beyond the chart in [P3].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts emergency funds of £600,000 are retained; no source document quantifies, defines, or evidences this reserve
      - Expenditure basis differs: fact find states £2,986 household (client's net share £2,386); extraction notes identify £2,237 used in cashflow modelling — relationship and adjustment basis not explained
      - Report claims capacity for loss is 'moderate' and funds 'sufficient for retirement even following a fall in the market'; no explicit stress-test outputs, market shock scenarios, or residual fund values provided
      - Access to emergency funds pledged as 'liquid cash' but recommended solution is a pension (Aviva UFPLS) requiring 10-15 working days; no separate, immediately accessible emergency reserve evidenced outside the pension wrapper
      - Inheritance of £350,000 cited as improving resilience but noted as 'pending legal proceedings' with no completion date, security of receipt, or factoring into cashflow projections
      - Report does not disclose the contribution commitment or whether ongoing contributions are assumed in the cashflow model
    The requirement is to establish whether affordability is fully supported by and consistent with evidence. Three material gaps emerge:
    
    1. EMERGENCY FUND DEFINITION AND CUSTODY: The report's cashflow chart [P3] displays a line labeled 'Emergency funds' of £600,000, but no document in the file defines what this sum represents, where it is held, or how it is accessed. The extract notes an 'ambiguity' around expenditure inputs but not emergency fund provenance. The report asserts [P2] that the client 'have liquid cash should you require instance access funds' but the only liquid asset identified is the pension transfer itself (Aviva UFPLS). Standard UK financial advice would hold emergency reserves outside the pension wrapper to avoid tax, withdrawal penalties, and the 10-15 day access lag. The £600,000 may represent:
    (a) Existing savings not yet documented in the fact find;
    (b) The combined value of pension funds themselves (which contradicts the assertion of separate 'liquid cash');
    (c) An error or graphical illustration rather than a firm commitment.
    Without evidence, the claim that emergency funds are 'retained' and 'sufficient' is unsupported.
    
    2. CASHFLOW INPUTS AND EXPENDITURE BASIS: The fact find [P5] records £2,986 household expenditure with £600 partner contribution, implying £2,386 client share. The extraction's own metadata notes that modelling used £2,237, a £149 (6.3%) reduction unexplained. The report provides no reconciliation. If £2,237 is a forecast adjustment (e.g., for partner's increased contribution post-retirement, reduced outgoings, or inflation assumption), the basis is not disclosed. This matters because affordability conclusions rest on expenditure: if modelled expenditure is materially lower than current fact find baseline, the analysis understates commitment intensity. The guard permits differences where they are explained (e.g., 'one is gross household, one is client-only net expenditure'); here, the report does not explain the basis.
    
    3. LIQUIDITY AND ACCESS TIMEFRAME: The report asserts [P2] comfort with '10-15 working days' for pension access, framed as acceptable because 'you have liquid cash'. This phrasing implies liquid capital outside the pension. But the only asset identified for drawdown is the Aviva UFPLS itself, which is subject to that 10-15 day lag. If the emergency fund IS the pension, then access is not immediate — a material difference for emergency resilience. If it is separate, no document evidences it. The Aviva terms [P16] add that 'if there's insufficient cash in your cash account to pay your income, we will automatically sell enough investments to pay any charges… and will include an additional amount which is the greater of £10 or 10% of the disinvestment', creating a secondary cost if the cash buffer is depleted.
    
    4. INHERITANCE TREATMENT: The report [P5] notes an expected £350,000 inheritance 'currently pending legal proceedings' as part of the basis for concluding funds are sufficient. This is material to affordability but carries execution risk: no completion date, no security of receipt, and no indication whether it is modelled as a discrete lump sum at a specific age or smoothed across retirement. The cashflow chart [P3] does not label where this inflow appears.
    
    5. ONGOING CONTRIBUTIONS: The report [P5] states 'I recommend that you consider commencing regular contributions to the new pension' but does not quantify the amount or make it a firm commitment. No document evidences whether the cashflow model assumes contributions, and if so, at what rate. If contributions are not modelled but affordability depends on them, the analysis is incomplete. If they are modelled but optional, the suitability of the recommendation depends on whether the client can afford them — a loop unresolved in the evidence.
    
    6. VULNERABILITY OVERLAY: The evidence [P5] records that the client is 'recently divorced' and 'recently altered your employment status, going from employed to self-employed with the same company'. Self-employment introduces earnings volatility and loss of employer pension contributions; recent divorce introduces potential financial instability and emotional stress. The risk profile [P1], [P21] is assessed as 7/10 (Growth), described as 'adventurous'. The extraction notes an 'internalInconsistency' that this profile is 'suited to capital accumulation rather than the stated income objectives' of £25k–£30k annual income with 'flexible income over guaranteed income' preference. A recently self-employed, post-divorce individual with moderate capacity for loss but a growth portfolio risks both sequence-of-returns risk in early retirement and income volatility if employment circumstances change. The report's affirmation that funds are 'sufficient even following a fall in the market' does not account for parallel employment income shock. This is a process gap: vulnerability assessment does not inform cashflow stress-testing.
    
    CONCLUSION ON COMPARISON: A comparison between report assertion and file evidence CAN be performed on the factual elements (expenditure figures, fund values, timeline). The comparison reveals: (a) expenditure basis unexplained (£2,986 vs. £2,237); (b) emergency fund value stated (£600,000) but not sourced or evidenced in custody; (c) access to 'liquid cash' asserted but not separately documented, creating ambiguity about whether it exists outside the pension; (d) inheritance treatment not detailed in cashflow; (e) contribution commitment not quantified. These are material inputs to affordability. The report does not disclose its assumption set or show the underlying cashflow model outputs. The assertion that funds are sufficient is therefore not supported by a complete and disclosed analysis. Under the standard 'No Issue: evidence complete, consistent and supports the outcome', this fails. The evidence is incomplete (missing emergency fund custody and quantification), inputs are unexplained (expenditure variance), and material assumptions are unstated (contributions, inheritance phasing).

  [G5.6] Liquidity mismatch with access needs
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) Recommended wrapper is a Personal Pension; (2) Selected retirement age is 60; (3) Planned withdrawals commence via UFPLS on 2026-06-24; (4) Tax treatment includes up to 25% tax-free lump sum (subject to Lump Sum Allowance of £268,275), with remainder taxed at marginal rate; (5) Sustainability of withdrawals is assessed as true. The report states liquidity requirements with a date of 2031-03-24 in the liquidityAndAccessNeeds field. The withdrawal start date of 2026-06-24 is approximately 4 years before the stated retirement age of 60 and approximately 5 years before the stated access-need date.
    File:     The Aviva Pension product literature [P2, P13, P18, P20] confirms that from normal minimum pension age (currently 55, rising to 57 from April 2028), clients can choose to take benefits through flexi-access drawdown with up to 25% as tax-free lump sum and remainder as taxable income. The product permits withdrawals, income drawdown, and phased access. The Aviva documentation [P2] states: 'Once you reach the normal minimum pension age, you can choose to use all or part of your pension pot to provide an income, either through income drawdown or by buying a lifetime annuity. You may also be able to withdraw all, or part, of your pension pot as a cash lump sum.' [P20] confirms: 'You can normally choose via Your Adviser to take retirement Benefits, in the form of a Pension Commencement Lump Sum and/or by using Income Drawdown, from all or part of Your Pension Portfolio at any time from the Minimum Pension Age. This is currently age 55.' The Fact Find [P23] shows current monthly expenditure of £2,836.23 against net monthly income of £800.00, resulting in a disposable income of -£2,036.23 (a deficit). No emergency fund figure is stated in the file. No evidenced cashflow assessment reconciling the current deficit with the ability to sustain contributions or withdrawals is provided in the passages. The file does not contain explicit quantification of emergency reserves, nor does it detail how ongoing contribution commitments will be sustained given the current income-expenditure gap.
    Discrepancies:
      - Retirement age (60) vs withdrawal start date (2026-06-24): Report states retirement age of 60, but planned UFPLS withdrawal commences in 2026, which is approximately 5 years before the stated retirement age. The report does not clarify whether age 60 is when full retirement income begins or whether phased access is intended from 2026.
      - Liquidity access date (2031-03-24) vs withdrawal start date (2026-06-24): The liquidityAndAccessNeeds date of 2031-03-24 post-dates the planned withdrawal start of 2026-06-24 by approximately 4.8 years. This discrepancy is not explained and raises ambiguity about which date represents the actual access need.
      - Tax-free lump sum allowance (£268,275) vs total arrangement values: The report asserts a Lump Sum Allowance of £268,275. The derived figure for total existing arrangement values is £327,945.44. The allowance cited (£268,275) is lower than total pension assets, indicating the allowance limit may apply, but the report does not address the implications or whether this constraint affects suitability.
      - Current income-expenditure position: Fact Find [P23] shows monthly income of £800 against expenditure of £2,836.23, yielding a deficit of -£2,036.23. The report does not explain how this deficit is being funded, whether it will be eliminated before retirement, or how contributions to the recommended pension can be sustained given this deficit. No cashflow model reconciling current position with future affordability is presented.
      - Emergency fund: The file contains no quantified emergency fund or liquid reserves figure. The report asserts that sustainability is assessed but provides no evidence of emergency reserves or liquid assets available to cover the income-expenditure gap or contingencies.
    The comparison can be performed in part, but material gaps prevent full assessment of affordability and liquidity fit. The report asserts the recommended wrapper is a Personal Pension with UFPLS withdrawals. Aviva product literature [P2, P13, P20] confirms that Personal Pensions permit UFPLS access from minimum pension age (currently 55). At the client's apparent age (not explicitly stated but withdrawal start in 2026 and retirement at 60 suggest current age circa 35–40), minimum pension age restrictions are not a barrier; the client will be 55+ by 2026 when withdrawals are to commence. The product wrapper itself does not lock capital beyond stated access dates. However, three material inconsistencies undermine the affordability finding: (1) The retirement age of 60 does not align with the withdrawal start date of 2026. If the client is currently under 50, age 60 is still 10+ years away, but withdrawals are to start in circa 4 years. This suggests either early access before stated retirement age or an error in one of these figures. The report does not explain this discrepancy. (2) The liquidityAndAccessNeeds date of 2031-03-24 is stated but the withdrawal schedule commences 2026-06-24, approximately 5 years earlier. The file does not explain whether 2031 is a secondary access need or whether the 2026 date is provisional. This ambiguity undermines confidence that affordability has been properly assessed against actual needs. (3) The Fact Find shows a severe current income-expenditure deficit (monthly disposable income of -£2,036.23). The report does not evidence how this deficit is currently met, whether it will resolve before contributions are to commence, or how contributions will be affordable. The internal note on ambiguities flags that monthly expenditure figures differ (£2,986 household vs £2,237 personal), but even the lower figure of £2,237 exceeds the net income of £800 monthly, leaving a deficit of circa £1,437. No cashflow or affordability assessment in the file reconciles this gap with the recommendation. (4) No emergency fund is quantified. The requirement to assess financial resilience includes addressing whether liquid reserves sufficient to cover the current deficit and contingencies exist. The file is silent on this. (5) The report states sustainability of withdrawals is assessed (isSustainabilityAssessed = true) but the file contains no explicit cashflow model, stress test, or sustainability narrative supporting this claim. The absence of such evidence, combined with the unexplained income-expenditure deficit and the discrepancies in dates, means the affordability claim is not supported by evidence in the file. This is a Potential Concern under the decision logic: 'Affordability is asserted without figures, cashflow inputs contradict the fact find...' Here, affordability is asserted but the supporting cashflow inputs (demonstrating how current deficit resolves and future contributions are sustainable) are absent from the file, and the access dates within the recommendation do not align with each other or stated retirement age.

    The requirement is to establish whether planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs are fully supported and consistent across the evidence. Comparison is performed: both report and file address retirement income needs, inheritance, state pension, employment transition, and emergency reserves. However, material discrepancies and gaps prevent a complete corroboration. The £350,000 inheritance is asserted with a fixed 2027-03-24 date in the report, but evidence [P2] describes the property as encumbered by 'mother's ex-partner still residing in the property, which has delayed the proceedings' with client estimating 'at least £150,000' with 'timeline remains uncertain due to ongoing legal matters'. This is a significant inconsistency: the report treats a contingent, uncertain, delayed inheritance as if it were certain and imminent. The £45,000 retirement financial impact stated as 'Gross' basis cannot be reconciled to the stated net income target of £25,000–£30,000 without further calculation. No tax modelling is evidenced. The affordability assessment acknowledges an employment transition to self-employment on 2026-03-24 with loss of employer pension contributions (Nest becomes paid up), but reports this as zero financial impact. Evidence [P2] and the report itself acknowledge this loss but do not quantify the income shortfall during the self-employment period 2026–2031. The report states the client will access funds via Flexi-Access Drawdown but does not demonstrate that the Aviva platform chosen permits access at age 60 or before state pension age, nor that liquidity is maintained for contingencies. The client's state pension forecast is referenced in evidence [P5] as something to be provided but is not included in the case file, and without it, the retirement cashflow cannot be fully verified. The pending pension sharing order is mentioned in evidence as 'potentially worth £270,000 or more' but the report does not confirm receipt date or amount, leaving a major element of the recommended solution unquantified. None of these gaps is explained by a guard: the inheritance uncertainty is not a comparison-safety issue but a contingency planning failure; the expenditure ambiguity is real and material; the transition period affordability is not addressed. The file is internally consistent on the facts (inheritance uncertain, mortgage rate rising, employment changing), but the report's assertions do not account for these realities or quantify their impact.

CHK-006 — Recommendation Rationale and Suitable Alternatives
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 9 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G6.1, G6.2, G6.3, G6.4, G6.5, G6.7, G6.8, G6.9). Report claims Quilter switch saves £208 p.a. (0.27% vs 0.19%); extracted charges show Quilter 0.27% and proposed platform 0.19%, but rationale does not specify total wrapper or fund charges for the recommended scheme—only platform component cited. Report states True Potential platform charges are 0.40% vs Aviva 0.21%, saving 0.19% or £88.68; also states ongoing fund charges higher in existing (0.58% vs 0.20%), saving £177.35, excluding advice charges. These are presented as separate justifications but both relate to the same £46,672.80 fund, creating ambiguity about which cost comparison is primary and whether both savings can be realised simultaneously. Report claims Nest switch increases cost by £19.15 p.a. (0.39% vs 0.30%) but characterises this as 'relatively small', yet the analysis reports [P4, P6] show Critical Yield reduction (yield loss) of 0.3%, suggesting real economic cost beyond platform fees. 4 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G6.1] Client-specific rationale for each recommendation, linked to specified goals
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report provides 24 rationale statements across multiple recommendations. The canonical model marks 16 of these as client-specific (isClientSpecific=true) and 8 as generic (isClientSpecific=false). Rationale addresses: cost savings (Quilter switch saves £208 p.a. at 0.27% vs 0.19%; True Potential switch saves £88.68 at 0.40% vs 0.21%; Nest switch increases cost by £19.15 but at 'relatively small' 0.39% vs 0.30%); investment alignment (existing funds not in line with agreed risk level 7; new schemes offer wider fund ranges); active monitoring capability; flexible retirement options; fund diversification and performance; simplified investment approach; and client circumstances (employment status change from employed to self-employed, £45,000 gross income, eligibility for tax relief on contributions). Five rationale statements are linked to specific objectives: OBJ1 (Quilter cost savings), OBJ2 (client preference, active monitoring), OBJ3 and OBJ4 (True Potential and Nest cost/investment alignment). Generic rationales include: 'provides exposure to a range of asset classes', 'provides a blend of active and passive investments', 'you want to keep investment strategy fairly simple', 'charges are competitive', 'funds have performed well', and 'funds are part of our CIP'. Four rationale statements about multi-asset funds and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity are repeated verbatim across recommendations.
    File:     Evidence confirms the client's stated objectives from the risk profile report [P11] and suitability report [P13]: retire or reduce hours at 60 with £25,000-£30,000 p.a. income; risk profile level 7 agreed following discussion; desire to have pension funds perform well; review of dormant True Potential and Nest pensions; receipt of Quilter pension share. Meeting records [P1, P2] document risk profile assessment as level 7, growth-oriented, with investment horizon 5-10 years. The client agreement [P9, P14] confirms advisory services will explain recommendations and suitability. Critical Yield analysis [P4, P6, P8] shows: Nest switch reduces yield by 0.3% (existing scheme £48,651 at 2.94% growth vs Aviva £45,900); True Potential/Quilter combined to Aviva reduces yield by 1.5% at 2.94% growth (existing £48,651 vs Aviva £33,400). No alternatives analysis is documented—no record of comparing Aviva against other providers, no record of evaluating retention of Quilter/True Potential/Nest with rebalancing, no evidence of consideration of alternative fund ranges within existing schemes before recommending switch.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims Quilter switch saves £208 p.a. (0.27% vs 0.19%); extracted charges show Quilter 0.27% and proposed platform 0.19%, but rationale does not specify total wrapper or fund charges for the recommended scheme—only platform component cited.
      - Report states True Potential platform charges are 0.40% vs Aviva 0.21%, saving 0.19% or £88.68; also states ongoing fund charges higher in existing (0.58% vs 0.20%), saving £177.35, excluding advice charges. These are presented as separate justifications but both relate to the same £46,672.80 fund, creating ambiguity about which cost comparison is primary and whether both savings can be realised simultaneously.
      - Report claims Nest switch increases cost by £19.15 p.a. (0.39% vs 0.30%) but characterises this as 'relatively small', yet the analysis reports [P4, P6] show Critical Yield reduction (yield loss) of 0.3%, suggesting real economic cost beyond platform fees.
      - Report states 'The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure' for Quilter recommendation but provides only platform charge comparison (0.19% vs 0.27%), omitting fund management charges and advice charges that should form 'overall' cost structure.
      - Report states recommendations provide 'wider range of investment options' and 'more tailored investment strategy' but does not cite what the existing schemes offered or why rebalancing within existing schemes was unsuitable; the Nest scheme is dismissed as having 'only 6 funds' and 'simple auto-enrolment design' but no evidence provided that these 6 funds could not accommodate the risk profile 7 objective.
      - Rationale 'The current portfolio is not in line with the agreed risk level' is stated for both True Potential and Nest, but no fund-by-fund analysis provided; no evidence what funds were actually held in existing arrangements or how they deviated from risk 7.
      - Report recommends 'Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity - 50% HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic - 50%' but rationale uses generic multi-asset fund language without explaining why Vanguard and HSBC were chosen over alternatives, or whether these specific funds match the stated objectives of £25k-£30k income drawdown with flexible access.
      - Four rationale statements ('provides exposure to range of asset classes', 'blend of active and passive', 'you want to keep investment fairly simple', 'charges are competitive') appear verbatim across multiple recommendations and are marked isClientSpecific=false, yet they are presented without linking to specific objectives or explaining why each recommendation's chosen funds satisfy them.
      - Report states 'funds have performed well in comparison with their peers' but no performance data, benchmark comparisons, or peer rankings provided in evidence; recommendation unsupported by cited research.
      - Report states funds 'are part of our CIP (Centralised Investment Proposition)...regularly reviewed by quarterly investment committee...utilise leading external research team' but no CIP assessment, committee minutes, or research reports provided; firm's internal process cited as rationale rather than client-specific justification.
      - Report recommends 'regular contributions' to new pension given employment status change (employed to self-employed, £45k gross earnings) but does not quantify contribution recommendation, does not state whether this is discretionary or required to meet objectives, and does not link to objective of achieving £25k-£30k retirement income or capacity to contribute while earning £45k.
      - Report claims new scheme 'provides more flexible retirement options, ensuring you can access your pension savings in a way that best suits your needs, including tax-efficient withdrawal strategies' but Aviva T&Cs [P12] define standard pension access terms; no Aviva-specific flexibility feature cited and no comparison with True Potential/Quilter/Nest flexibility offered.
    Comparison performed: both sides contain stated objectives and recommendations. The report provides rationale for recommendations but the evidence base for those rationales is incomplete or absent. Client-specific elements identified: (1) Employment status change (employed→self-employed, £45k gross) cited as context for contribution recommendation [report text: 'You have recently altered your employment status...']. (2) Objective of £25k-£30k retirement income at age 60 stated in report and corroborated by risk profile report [P11]. (3) Risk profile level 7 (Growth) confirmed in meeting records [P1, P2, P11]. (4) Cost savings quantified for three switches: £208 (Quilter), £88.68 (True Potential platform), £177.35 (True Potential fund charges), £19.15 increase (Nest). (5) 'No ties or preference to leave Quilter' cited from client input. Generic/Boilerplate elements identified: (1) Four rationale statements ('range of asset classes', 'active and passive blend', 'keep investment fairly simple', 'charges competitive') repeated verbatim and flagged isClientSpecific=false. These are presented as justifications but do not explain why the specific recommended funds (Vanguard, HSBC) are suitable for this client's £25k-£30k income objective or risk 7 profile. (2) Fund performance claim ('funds have performed well') unsupported by research document; only report itself cites this [passage marked I]. (3) CIP process rationale cites firm's internal governance rather than client need; no evidence of how this governance produces better outcomes for this client. Inconsistencies in cost comparison basis: The report presents multiple cost justifications for the same arrangements without clarifying total cost impact. For True Potential, both platform cost saving (£88.68) and fund charge saving (£177.35) are cited separately, totalling £266.03 p.a. in claimed savings, but both percentages are presented as comparisons to different bases—the platform switch (0.40%→0.21%) and fund manager change (0.58%→0.20%)—creating ambiguity about whether both savings materialise or whether one is subsumed in the other. The Nest switch is presented as cost-increasing (+£19.15) but the Critical Yield analysis [P4] shows a 0.3% yield reduction (far greater than the 0.09% cost increase implied by £19.15/£21,273), indicating the rationale undersells the true economic cost of the switch. Guard application: (1) Identical rationale bullets flagged isClientSpecific=false are boilerplate and should not be counted as supporting evidence of client-specific rationale; they appear without modification across recommendations. (2) Rationales naming client figures (£25k-£30k income, £45k earnings, risk level 7, employment change) satisfy the guard for client-specificity even if sentence structure is templated. These are present for Quilter (employment status), True Potential (cost quantification), and Nest (employment status) recommendations. However, the absence of fund-level justification for why Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity and HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic are suited to this client's specific objectives (flexible income drawdown at £25k-£30k, not capital growth) is a material gap. The risk profile is assessed as 7/Growth but the income objective is modest; the report does not reconcile the recommendation of high-equity solutions (80% equity in LifeStrategy) against the stated goal of generating £25k-£30k p.a. flexible income. Critical Yield impact: The switching analysis [P4, P6] shows that transfers to Aviva incur significant yield reduction (1.5% for True Potential/Quilter combined; 0.3% for Nest). The rationale does not address whether this yield loss is acceptable given the stated objective of ensuring 'pension funds performing to the best of their ability' and building funds for retirement income of £25k-£30k. The yield reduction is cited in technical analysis but not reconciled with the client-specific rationale that emphasises cost savings and better performance. Alternatives: No evidence that alternatives were considered. The report does not state: (1) Whether retention of Quilter with rebalancing was evaluated. (2) Whether True Potential could be rebalanced to risk 7 without switching. (3) Whether Nest's 6 funds included any compatible with risk 7 (e.g., lower-risk or balanced funds that could be upgraded over time). (4) Whether other platforms or providers were compared to Aviva. The decision logic for CHK-006 requires 'alternatives presented with reasons for rejection, corroborated by research'—this is absent. Vulnerability overlay: The client's circumstances include employment status change (employed→self-employed), income change (from employed benefits to £45k self-employed), and inheritance expectancy (ex-husband's pension share). The meeting records [P1] note 'Clarifying the implications of your risk profile' but no record of how the status change or income volatility affected advice suitability assessment. The report recommends 'regular contributions' but does not quantify or explain affordability given the employment transition. No evidence of reasonable adjustment, communication review, or suitability reassurance for this potentially vulnerable circumstance is present.

  [G6.2] Justification for the particular product, provider, wrapper and investment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts Aviva Platform Personal Pension is recommended based on: (1) Provider strengths: Financial strength (A Stable by S&P, £407bn AUM), choice (6,900+ funds), flexibility (ISA withdrawals/replacements), expertise (award-winning strategies, private markets access), competitive charges with tiered pricing, online tools, strong governance, and innovation. (2) Active management ability: 'By moving to the recommended scheme, I will have the ability to actively monitor and adjust your investments as required, helping to ensure they remain aligned with your changing needs and market conditions' [P8]. (3) Investment strategy rationale: funds reflect risk profile (7/10), provide diversification across asset classes, blend active and passive management, align with client preference for simplicity, have competitive charges, outperformed peer funds, are part of CIP with quarterly oversight. (4) Multi-asset fund justification: 'professionally managed, diversified investment solution that aligns with your financial goals and risk profile...combining growth potential of equities with potentially uncorrelated returns of other asset classes, such as bonds, property and absolute return' linked to objectives OBJ3, OBJ4, OBJ5. (5) Wrapper rationale: Personal Pension is 'most suitable given your circumstances and retirement objectives', offers tax efficiency, variable contributions, flexibility from age 55, allows Flexi Access Drawdown, provides death benefits and IHT treatment clarity.
    File:     The case file contains: (1) Aviva Platform Key Features Document [P9] describing it as a portfolio management service allowing consolidation across tax wrappers, with reduced fund management charges and combined viewing. (2) Aviva Terms & Conditions [P14, P15] confirming adviser-managed investment options, DIM capability, and tax relief on contributions. (3) Fund research showing three funds recommended: HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio, Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity, and Aviva Investors AI Multi-asset Plus IV 2 [P13]. Performance data [P13] shows: Aviva AI Multi-asset Plus IV 2 at 3-month 4.03%, 1-year 14.81%, 3-year 43.61%; HSBC at 3-month 4.73%, 1-year 15.63%, 3-year 46.53%; Vanguard at 3-month 5.18%, 1-year 16.28%, 3-year 48.56%. (4) Existing arrangements comparison [P11]: Quilter platform charges 0.27%; True Potential platform 0.40% + 0.50% advice + 0.58% fund charges = 1.48% total; Nest plan charges 0.30% + 1.8% contribution charge. Report states Aviva platform charges are 0.19% (stated as negotiated savings of £208 on Quilter's £260,000 at 0.27% versus 0.19%) [P8]. (5) The comparison table in the report [P8] names five alternative options considered: Option 1 (leave untouched), Option 2 (switch funds only), Option 3 (switch to another pension), Option 4 (use employer scheme), Option 5 (new pension plan). Recommendations stated: Quilter to switch (lower cost structure, no ties/preference stated), True Potential to switch (detailed review completed), Nest to switch (detailed review completed). (6) Cashflow analysis [P1] projects fund value at age 87 under low/medium/high growth scenarios. (7) Risk profile research [P13] shows fund comparison across multiple performance metrics.
    Discrepancies:
      -  Report claims funds 'have performed well in comparison with their peers' and 'outperformed your existing True Potential funds' [P8], but performance data in [P13] shows: Aviva AI Multi-asset Plus IV 2 (1-year 14.81%, 3-year 43.61%) underperforms both HSBC (15.63%, 46.53%) and Vanguard (16.28%, 48.56%) on both timeframes measured. The three funds are listed but the specific performance claim is unsupported by rank order.
      - Report states Aviva platform will deliver 'ability to actively monitor and adjust your investments' and claims this is a client-specific rationale. However, the report does not establish whether True Potential or Quilter platforms lack equivalent adviser-monitoring capability. The comparison does not explain whether active management is unique to Aviva or why it justifies replacement rather than in-place fund switching.
      - Report asserts Aviva charges are 'transparent, competitive charges with potential tiered pricing' and states platform charges of 0.19%, yet does not provide a direct charge comparison table showing platform charges (0.19% for Aviva vs. 0.27% Quilter vs. 0.40% True Potential). The term 'potential tiered pricing' is aspirational, not demonstrable.
      - Report's justification for multi-asset fund approach emphasizes simplicity and diversification, but does not explain why a multi-asset fund is preferable to the existing three-fund arrangement in True Potential (Goldman Sachs Balanced, Growth Aligned Aggressive, Schroders Balanced, SEI Aggressive, Trinity Bridge Balanced, UBS Aggressive) beyond stating it as 'professionally managed' and subject to quarterly review.
      - Report states CIP 'provides enhanced oversight, risk management, and adaptability' compared to a 'static multi-asset fund' [P10], positioning CIP as a justified alternative to single multi-asset solutions. However, the client is not being offered a choice between single multi-asset and CIP; the recommendation is embedded without presenting this option as an alternative considered and rejected.
    Comparison performed: The report identifies five alternative options and provides rationales for rejection of three existing schemes (Quilter, True Potential, Nest). The structure satisfies the requirement to consider alternatives. However, material gaps exist in depth and client-specificity of the justifications. On provider selection, the report names generic strengths (financial strength, fund choice, governance) and one client-specific element (active management ability), but does not distinguish Aviva's capabilities from competitors on these points. The 'financial strength' claim cites S&P rating and AUM but provides no evidence in the file. The 'active management' justification is asserted without comparison. On investment strategy, the report claims funds 'outperformed your existing True Potential funds', but the evidence shows the recommended Aviva AI Multi-asset Plus IV 2 underperforms both HSBC and Vanguard alternatives on the only comparable performance data provided (1-year and 3-year returns in [P13]). This is a material factual discrepancy: the report's performance assertion contradicts the ranked data. The claim about competitive charges references a £208 annual saving on platform costs (0.19% vs 0.27%) but does not present a full charge comparison including adviser charges, fund management fees, or contribution charges that apply to the existing arrangements. The True Potential arrangement includes 1.48% total charges (0.40% platform + 0.50% ongoing advice + 0.58% fund management), but the report does not state whether the Aviva recommendation includes equivalent ongoing advice charges or how the full-service charging compares. On wrapper selection (Personal Pension), the rationale is generic and could apply to any personal pension. On fund selection, the report links multi-asset approach to objectives OBJ3 (simplicity), OBJ4 (flexibility/income), and OBJ5 (flexible income preference), but does not explain why flexibility is better delivered through a multi-asset fund than through the existing True Potential arrangement which already holds six funds. The CIP governance claim emphasizes quarterly review but does not demonstrate this provides materially better outcomes than the existing schemes' governance. The vulnerability overlay: The extraction notes [P8] an ambiguity about the client's objective preference for 'flexible income over guaranteed income'—whether this is active rejection of annuitisation or simply stated preference. This ambiguity is not clarified in the report's justifications, and the link between multi-asset fund selection and flexible income preference is assumed rather than explained. No vulnerability drivers, support needs, communication requirements or reasonable adjustments are recorded in the extraction or evident in the evidence pack, so no vulnerability overlay applies here.

  [G6.4] Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that alternatives were considered and rejected: (1) Retain Quilter pension — rejected because higher platform charges of 0.27% vs Aviva's 0.19% (£208 annual saving) and client has no ties or preference to Quilter; (2) Leave True Potential in place and switch investment funds within the plan — rejected because Aviva offers wider range of investment options for more tailored strategy; (3) Leave Nest in place and switch to Lower Risk fund within the plan — rejected because Aviva offers wider range of investment options for better tailored strategy and greater choice; (4) Investment Pathway Option 1 — rejected because it is a generic solution requiring more tailored solution, does not reflect individual circumstances/withdrawal requirements/risk profile as accurately, requires ongoing professional advice and regular review as part of ongoing advice service, and has not performed as well as the recommended investment strategy. The research file lists five broad options considered: Option 1 (Leave untouched), Option 2 (In-situ fund switch), Option 3 (Consolidate into existing), Option 4 (Into employer scheme), Option 5 (New plan, recommended). Investment Pathways are rejected with reasons including lack of personalisation, generic nature, need for ongoing oversight, and poor performance.
    File:     The case file contains research documents showing fund performance comparisons (P2, P3, P4: Performance data for HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio, Vanguard LifeStrategy 80%, Nest Higher Risk, True Potential, and UT Mixed Investment comparing performance and volatility as at March 2026). Switching analysis documents (P11-P24) provide projections comparing existing schemes against Aviva Pension Portfolio (Choice) at various growth rates, comparing PCLS values, death benefits, and charges. P6 [I] explicitly lists Options 1-5 with recommendation for Option 5 (new plan on Aviva). Charge data in P12 shows Aviva platform standard charge 0.25% reduced to 0.17% (0.25% - 0.08% = 0.17%, not 0.19% as asserted in rationale). P1 notes that investment choice available with Aviva may differ from current providers and could impact future performance. P5 [I] describes Aviva's features including over 6,900 funds, flexibility, expertise, competitive charges. P7 provides detailed fund research including asset allocations for Aviva AI Multi-asset Plus IV 2, HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio, and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80%. No document provides a comparison showing that Investment Pathway Option 1 'has not performed as well as' the recommended strategy — the only performance data (P2, P3, P4) shows weighted average performance for proposed plan but does not compare against any specific Investment Pathway option.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts Aviva platform charges are 0.19%, but P12 shows standard Aviva Annual Management Charge of 0.25% with a decrease of 0.08%, resulting in 0.17% (0.25% - 0.08% = 0.17%), not 0.19%.
      - The report rejects Investment Pathway Option 1 stating 'It has not performed as well as the recommended investment strategy,' but no evidence in the case file compares the performance of Investment Pathway Option 1 against the recommended Aviva portfolio. The performance data provided (P2, P3, P4) show only the proposed plan's weighted average performance or comparisons between HSBC and Vanguard funds, not against any Investment Pathway option.
      - The report lists rejection reasons for alternatives but does not consistently quantify or evidence the performance claim for Investment Pathway rejection; P4 shows comparative performance rankings but does not identify which is the Investment Pathway option or provide a direct comparison.
    The comparison can be performed. The report presents multiple alternatives with rejection reasons, and these can be cross-checked against the case file. (1) On Quilter charges: The report claims 0.27% vs 0.19%, yielding £208 saving. The extracted charge data confirms Quilter at 0.27% (per extraction summary), but the report's stated Aviva charge of 0.19% does not match P12's stated standard charge of 0.25% less 0.08% reduction = 0.17%. This is a discrepancy in the charge basis used to justify the switch. The £208 annual saving is consistent with 0.27% - 0.17% = 0.10% on a fund value around £208,000 (not £260,000 Quilter value, suggesting a mid-point or averaging assumption that is not transparent). The rejection reason itself is client-specific and objective-linked (OBJ1, cost efficiency). (2) On True Potential and Nest in-situ switches: The rejection reasons cite 'wider range of investment options' but do not quantify or evidence this comparison. P1 notes Aviva offers 'over 6,900 funds' but no document states how many options True Potential or Nest offer, making the comparison qualitative rather than evidenced. The reasons are stated as client-specific and linked to OBJ2 (tailored strategy) but lack concrete support. (3) On Investment Pathway Option 1: The report asserts it 'has not performed as well as the recommended investment strategy,' marked as not client-specific. However, no document in the case file identifies which funds constitute 'Investment Pathway Option 1' or provides a direct performance comparison. P4 shows a performance scatter plot ranking funds by performance and volatility but does not label any as an Investment Pathway option. This rejection reason is unsupported by evidence: the case file does not contain the performance data needed to substantiate this comparison. The other Investment Pathway rejection reasons (lack of tailoring, need for ongoing review) are process-based arguments supported by P5's description of Aviva capabilities, but the performance claim stands alone and unsubstantiated. (4) The report does present alternatives, rejection reasons are largely client-specific and linked to stated objectives, but two material issues emerge: the charge comparison uses a figure (0.19%) not evidenced in the underlying documents, and a key rejection reason for Investment Pathways (superior performance) is not supported by any performance comparison in the case file. The method of comparison is SetCoverage (options listed with rejection reasons), and this is met at a formal level, but the accuracy and substantiation of specific rejection rationales show gaps.

  [G6.5] Every existing arrangement has a stated recommended outcome and a rationale supporting it
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Three existing arrangements identified: Quilter Pension (FullTransfer), True Potential Pension (FullTransfer), Nest (FullTransfer). Each has a stated recommended outcome of FullTransfer. For Quilter: rationale includes lower platform charges (0.19% vs 0.27%, saving £208 pa), client preference to leave, and adviser oversight capability. For True Potential: rationale includes lower platform charges (0.21% vs 0.40%, saving £88.68 pa), lower fund charges (0.20% vs 0.58%, saving £177.35 pa), risk alignment with wider fund options, and adviser oversight. For Nest: rationale includes slight charge increase acknowledged (0.39% vs 0.30%, increase of £19.15 pa) but justified by risk alignment, wider fund options, and adviser oversight. All rationales are marked isClientSpecific=true and linked to stated objectives (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3).
    File:     Pension Switch Report documents (P2-P14) show three arrangements with transfer values: Quilter £260,000.00, True Potential £46,672.80, Nest £21,272.64.
