CHK-008 — Disadvantages, Risks and Customer Understanding
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G8.1, G8.2, G8.4, G8.5, G8.6, G8.7, G8.8). The report does not identify or discuss the risk that existing defined benefit pension rights or guaranteed income protections may be lost through the transfer, despite this being material for pension transfers and appearing prominently in the Aviva product literature [P12: 'you may be losing valuable rights or benefits within the scheme such as a guaranteed income based on your final salary']. The report does not identify the risk that income drawdown may deplete the pension fund if investment growth does not keep pace with withdrawals, despite this being material to the client's stated objective of flexible income and appearing in product literature [P2: 'Your investments need to grow to compensate for the income you withdraw. If that doesn't happen, the income you take will deplete your pension fund']. The report does not disclose the risk that means-tested benefits may be affected by pension income withdrawals, despite this being material to retirement planning and appearing in product literature [P2: 'The income you take may have an impact on any means-tested benefits you're receiving']. 6 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G8.1] Material disadvantages, risk factors and limitations of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report states the following material disadvantages and limitations: (1) Access delay: "it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete" [I, p.12, 14]. (2) Lower absolute performance: "The recommended funds, being more balanced and in line with your risk tolerance, has understandably lagged behind the higher-risk funds but has demonstrated strong risk-adjusted performance within its category" [I, p.20]. (3) Market timing risk: "if the market rises in value between the date of realising your existing funds and the purchase of your new funds, you could be financially disadvantaged" [I, p.12]. (4) No guarantee of outperformance: "There is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement for these funds" [I, p.12]. (5) Higher charges: "The annual charges for the recommend plan are higher at 0.39% compared to 0.30%, representing an increase of £19.15 per annum, based on the current values" [I, p.12]. (6) Projection limitations: "even a small variance between the assumptions used and actual experience can result in a marked difference in the outcome when compounded over a long period of time" [I, p.22]. (7) Long-term care not considered: "the chart does not consider any potential long-term care costs which could occur in later life" [I, p.22]. (8) Cancellation limitation: "any previous provider is not obliged to take the plan back" [I, p.26]. The report also discloses tax treatment is based on current HMRC practice which may change [I, p.20].
    File:     The Aviva Pension Key Features Document [P2, P4, P11, P12] identifies material risks and disadvantages applicable to the recommended product including: (1) Investment and market risks generally: "The value of your pension pot and the retirement benefits it can provide may go down as well as up. You could get back less than has been invested" [P5]. (2) Small company and overseas investment risks: "Small company investments may take longer to sell... The value of any overseas investments will be influenced by the rate of exchange" [P2]. (3) Property investment risks: "If the underlying investments include property, it may take some time before we can sell your investment" [P2]. (4) Market timing risk on transfer: "As a result of trading practices, there's a possibility that the price of investments may fall or rise in the period between us receiving your instructions and the time of the transaction" [P2]. (5) Illiquid assets risk: "It may not be easy to sell investments such as commercial property" [P2]. (6) Fixed-term product restrictions: "If you invest in some products with a fixed term, then you may not be able to access the monies until the end of that term, and even where you can, you may receive back significantly less than you invested" [P2]. (7) Income drawdown specific risks: "Your investments need to grow to compensate for the income you withdraw. If that doesn't happen, the income you take will deplete your pension fund. Especially if you choose to take a high level of income" [P2]. (8) Means-tested benefits impact: "The income you take may have an impact on any means-tested benefits you're receiving" [P2]. (9) Transfer of pension rights: "If you're transferring benefits from an older company pension scheme it could be a Defined Benefit Pension and you may be losing valuable rights or benefits within the scheme such as a guaranteed income based on your final salary" [P12]. (10) Structured products restrictions: "It's not possible to transfer structured products either into or out of your Pension Portfolio" [P11]. (11) Delays in investment execution: "For Direct Debit and Immediate electronic Payments funds will clear within three Business Days of the crediting of monies to Your Cash Account to be invested" [P3]; and separately administration processing and unit conversion delays during transfers [P23]. (12) Tax treatment subject to change: "The current favourable tax treatment of the Pension Portfolio may change if tax rules change" [P4].
    Discrepancies:
      - The report does not identify or discuss the risk that existing defined benefit pension rights or guaranteed income protections may be lost through the transfer, despite this being material for pension transfers and appearing prominently in the Aviva product literature [P12: 'you may be losing valuable rights or benefits within the scheme such as a guaranteed income based on your final salary']
      - The report does not identify the risk that income drawdown may deplete the pension fund if investment growth does not keep pace with withdrawals, despite this being material to the client's stated objective of flexible income and appearing in product literature [P2: 'Your investments need to grow to compensate for the income you withdraw. If that doesn't happen, the income you take will deplete your pension fund']
      - The report does not disclose the risk that means-tested benefits may be affected by pension income withdrawals, despite this being material to retirement planning and appearing in product literature [P2: 'The income you take may have an impact on any means-tested benefits you're receiving']
      - The report does not address small-cap or overseas investment currency risks, despite these being relevant to balanced growth funds and identified in product literature [P2]
      - The report does not identify illiquidity risks associated with property investments, despite these being relevant to the fund composition and appearing in product literature [P2]
      - The higher charges disadvantage stated in the report (0.39% vs 0.30%, £19.15 per annum) is calculated only on Nest's current value (£21,272.64) and appears incomplete; the analysis identifies three separate existing arrangements with differing charge bases, but the comparison does not account for the aggregate position or clarify which arrangement's charges are being compared
      - The report discloses a three-day settlement delay for new investments [implicit in 10-15 working days] but product literature separately identifies a three-day delay for cash clearance [P3] plus the time for trading, unit conversion, and disinvestment timing risks—these are multiple overlapping sources of access delay not fully synthesized in the disadvantages disclosure
      - The access delay disadvantage (10-15 working days) is stated generically without specifying whether this applies uniformly to all three arrangements being switched or whether it represents the worst-case timing
    The report discloses several material disadvantages of the recommendation and contains statements that are broadly consistent with the Aviva product literature. However, there are multiple material risks identified in the product literature and applicable to pension transfers that the report does not address. Most significantly: (1) The defined benefit pension transfer warning (loss of guaranteed income) is a FCA-mandated disclosure for pension transfers and appears prominently in the Aviva product literature [P12]. The extraction metadata indicates that guaranteed annual amounts for existing arrangements are not present in the report ('NotPresentInDocument'), suggesting the existing benefits were not analysed for defined benefit features. This omission is particularly material because the regulatory basis for this check includes COBS 9.4.7R and PRIN 2A.5, which require fair disclosure of what the client is giving up. (2) The income drawdown depletion risk [P2] is material to this client because the stated objectives explicitly include preference for 'flexible income over guaranteed income' and a target retirement income of £25,000–£30,000. The report does not warn that if investment returns do not keep pace with drawdowns, the fund will be depleted—this is the inverse of guaranteed income and directly relevant to the suitability of the recommendation. (3) The means-tested benefits risk [P2] is omitted from the report. This is a financial planning risk that should have been addressed, especially where the stated objectives mention flexibility and income planning. (4) The higher charges disadvantage is stated as 0.39% vs 0.30% on £19.15 per annum. The derivation shows this is calculated as £21,272.64 × 0.09% (Nest's value only), meaning the comparison is against Nest's 0.30% charge rate. However, the three existing arrangements have differing charge bases: Quilter 0.27%, True Potential 0.98%, and Nest 0.30%. The report's statement that charges increase from 0.30% to 0.39% is incomplete and potentially misleading if it implies this applies uniformly across the consolidation, when in fact True Potential's charges are 0.98% and the consolidated rate of 0.39% may represent an improvement for True Potential while a cost for Quilter and Nest. The charge analysis lacks transparency about which existing arrangement's rate is being used as the baseline and why. (5) The access delay (10-15 working days) is stated generically. The Aviva product literature identifies: a three-day clearing period for cash [P3], plus trading settlement periods, plus unit conversion delays during transfers [P23]. These are presented as a single disadvantage without decomposing the components or clarifying whether the 10-15 day estimate includes all of these or applies consistently to ad hoc withdrawals versus new transfers. (6) Specific product risks from the Aviva KFD—small-cap illiquidity, currency risk, property investment illiquidity, fixed-term product restrictions, and structured product transfer restrictions [P2, P11]—are not addressed in the report. While not every risk in product literature need be repeated if it is not material to this client's fund selection, the report provides no evidence that it has considered and concluded these are immaterial. The lack of detail on fund composition makes this assessment impossible. (7) The Tax Treatment section [I, p.20] states 'Levels and basis of tax relief are subject to change' but does not address the Aviva platform-level tax treatment risk that 'The current favourable tax treatment of the Pension Portfolio may change if tax rules change' [P4]. These are overlapping but distinct risks. The report's language is generic; Aviva's is specific to pension tax status. Consistency is achieved but specificity is limited. (8) The Cancellation limitation is stated [I, p.26]. This is consistent with the product literature framework (Aviva T&C addresses cancellation) [P3, P11], though the exact mechanics are not detailed in the passages provided. (9) Projection limitations are stated in generic form [I, p.22] and are consistent with standard industry practice and Aviva KFD disclosure [P2] that projections are not guaranteed. The report's use of cashflow modelling to project retirement sufficiency is sound in principle, but the disclosure of projection uncertainty could be more prominent. Overall, the report presents selected disadvantages but omits material risks that are either (a) FCA-mandated disclosures for pension transfers (defined benefit warning, loss of protections), (b) directly material to this client's stated objectives (income drawdown depletion risk, means-tested benefits impact), or (c) product-specific risks in the recommended fund literature (small-cap, currency, property, fixed-term, and structured product restrictions). The higher charges disadvantage is stated but the basis is incomplete and potentially misleading when viewed against the full suite of existing arrangements. The report's disclosures are not false, but they are incomplete and lack the specificity and synthesis that would constitute fair, clear and prominent explanation of all material disadvantages.

  [G8.2] Product-specific and investment-specific risks explained
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states product-specific and investment risks across multiple sections: (1) Timing Risk: 'whilst the transactions are pending, there could be movements within the stock-market...If the market rises in value between the date of realising your existing funds and the purchase of your new funds, you could be financially disadvantaged' (page 14); (2) No Outperformance Guarantee: 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider' (page 16, repeated on pages 12, 19); (3) General Capital Risk: 'Unit prices and the value of your pension fund can fall as well as rise and there is no guarantee you will receive a return of your original capital, especially in the early years or if you choose to take an income from it' (pages 12, 19); (4) Growth Investor Risk: 'As a Growth investor you do not see yourself as a cautious person and are inclined to view risk as a source of opportunity rather than as a threat...you typically look for higher returns rather than safer investments and understand that you need to take some risk to meet your investment goals' (page 9); (5) Accepted Volatility: 'you accept that values will fluctuate over time' (page 9); (6) Past Performance: 'Past performance is no guarantee of future returns' (page 18).
    File:     Product literature on file discloses multiple specific risks: (1) From Vanguard KIIDS [P1]: Counterparty risk, Liquidity risk, Credit risk, Inflation risk, Use of derivatives with potential for increased exposure and greater fluctuations; (2) From Aviva Pension KFD [P3]: Small company liquidity risks with greater price fluctuations, overseas investment currency exchange exposure, property investment timing and valuation risks, trading timing risks, fixed-term product access restrictions, income drawdown risks (depletion of fund if withdrawals exceed growth, impact on means-tested benefits, tax position uncertainty); (3) From Vanguard LifeStrategy documentation [P6]: Currency exchange rate movements affecting returns, Emerging markets sensitivity to economic/political conditions with greater liquidity risk, restrictions on investment/transfers, failed/delayed delivery risks; (4) From True Potential factsheets [P15-P18, P20]: Capital at risk, investments can fluctuate in value, past performance not a guide to future performance, specific risks including Credit Risk (bond issuer default, interest rate and inflation effects), Exchange Rate Risk (securities not in GBP), Liquidity Risk (difficulty selling quickly at fair price); (5) From Aviva Pension KFD income drawdown section [P9]: Specific drawdown risks and mechanics; (6) From general Aviva Pension KFD [P12]: 'The value of your pension pot and the retirement benefits it can provide may go down as well as up...You could get back less than has been invested' and dependence on investment performance and charges.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report does not disclose specific Credit Risk as documented in True Potential and Vanguard literature: 'the bond issuer may fail to meet its repayments' and impact of interest rates and creditworthiness on bond market value [P20]
      - Report does not disclose specific Exchange Rate Risk as documented in True Potential and Vanguard literature: 'The value of your investments may be affected by changes in currency exchange rates' for GBP-denominated funds investing internationally [P20, P6]
      - Report does not disclose specific Liquidity Risk as documented in Vanguard [P1] and True Potential [P20] literature: that 'Lower liquidity means there are insufficient buyers or sellers...could cause the Fund to incur higher costs when buying or selling investments' or that 'Certain market conditions could make sufficiently liquid assets difficult to sell quickly at a fair price'
      - Report does not disclose specific Emerging Markets Risk as documented in Vanguard [P6]: that 'Emerging markets are generally more sensitive to economic and political conditions than developed markets...greater Liquidity Risk, restrictions on investment or transfers of assets and failed/delayed delivery of securities'
      - Report does not disclose Counterparty Risk as documented in Vanguard [P1]: 'The insolvency of any institutions providing services such as safekeeping of assets or acting as counterparty to derivatives...may expose the Fund to financial loss'
      - Report does not disclose Inflation Risk as documented in Vanguard [P1]: 'The value of your investments may not be worth as much in the future due to changes in purchasing power resulting from inflation'
      - Report does not disclose specific Derivatives Risk as documented in Vanguard [P1]: 'Use of derivatives could increase or reduce exposure to underlying assets and result in greater fluctuations of the Fund's net asset value' and True Potential [P20] derivatives-related risks
      - Report does not disclose specific Income Drawdown Risks as documented in Aviva [P3]: that income drawdown may deplete the fund if withdrawals exceed growth, particularly with high income levels, and that 'Your investments need to grow to compensate for the income you withdraw. If that doesn't happen, the income you take will deplete your pension fund'
      - Report does not disclose that income drawdown may impact means-tested benefits as documented in Aviva [P3]: 'The income you take may have an impact on any means-tested benefits you're receiving'
      - Report does not disclose that high income drawdown reduces future options as documented in Aviva [P3]: 'If you withdraw high levels of income, there'll be less money to provide for dependants or to buy a lifetime annuity in the future'
      - Report does not disclose specific Small Company Investment Risks as documented in Aviva [P3]: 'Small company investments may take longer to sell than those in larger companies, so the price fluctuations may be greater'
      - Report does not disclose specific Property Investment Risks as documented in Aviva [P3]: that 'If the underlying investments include property, it may take some time before we can sell your investment...may be based on the valuer's opinion' and timing risks with sales
    The suitability report asserts general principles of capital risk, growth investing, timing risk, and absence of performance guarantees that are consistent with the supporting evidence. However, the report fails to disclose numerous material product-specific and investment-specific risks that appear in the product literature and factsheets on file. The standards require 'all material disadvantages, risks, limitations, tax consequences, potential loss scenarios' to be explained. The report contains boilerplate general warnings but delegates detailed risk disclosure to product literature without specifically highlighting the material risks identified in that literature. The report does state risks on pages 9, 12, 14, 16, and 18, but examination against product literature shows the report is silent on: Credit Risk (bond issuer default, interest rate sensitivity, creditworthiness effects), Exchange Rate Risk (currency exposure for international holdings), Liquidity Risk (difficulty selling in adverse conditions, higher transaction costs), Counterparty Risk (insolvency of service providers), Inflation Risk (loss of purchasing power), Emerging Markets Risk (enhanced volatility, restrictions, failed delivery), Derivatives Risk (increased exposure, greater fluctuations), Income Drawdown Depletion Risk (fund exhaustion if withdrawals exceed growth), Means-Tested Benefits Impact, Small Company Liquidity Risk (extended sale periods, greater price fluctuations), and Property Investment Risk (extended sales timelines, valuation uncertainty). While the recommendation appears appropriate to the client's risk profile and the general risks disclosed are consistent with the evidence, the absence of these material product-specific risks from the report creates a disclosure gap. The report's reference to 'you understand that some risk needs to be taken' and acceptance of volatility does not substitute for specific disclosure of identified risks. The guard that standard capital-at-risk wording satisfies general warnings but not product-specific risks applies here: the report has generic capital-at-risk statements but lacks the product-specific detail present in fund literature. The guard that deferring risk disclosure to product literature without highlighting material risks within it represents a limitation on the report also applies: risks exist in the file, are material to the recommendation, and are not highlighted in the report.

  [G8.4] Loss scenarios illustrated
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts two loss scenarios: (1) 'should this high level of inflation continue at the assumed rate, your assets could be depleted at age 81' [P7, page 22]; and (2) 'if your plan sustained a loss similar to the crash of 2007/8 your assets would still last your lifetime' [P7, page 22]. The report states these are stress tests illustrating how the recommended investment strategy would perform under adverse conditions. The report also states that 'Based on your circumstances alone, your pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement, even following a fall in the market.' [P7]. The report further states 'Cashflow analysis to assess whether you will have sufficient funds for retirement' was performed. [P7]. The report does not quantify either loss scenario with a loss percentage or loss amount.
    File:     The evidence contains generic product literature risk disclosures in the Aviva Pension KFD [P1, P2, P3] which describe risks such as 'The value of your pension pot and the retirement benefits it can provide may go down as well as up' and 'You could get back less than has been invested' [P3], and that 'Income drawdown risks' include 'If your investments need to grow to compensate for the income you withdraw. If that doesn't happen, the income you take will deplete your pension fund.' [P1]. The illustration documents [P4, P5, P9] state 'The projected amounts shown within this Illustration are indicative and are not guaranteed.' [P9]. The SAP (Suitable Alternative Provider) comparison documents [P19, P20, P21, P24] note 'The fund available at retirement will depend upon the actual growth' and are not guaranteed [P19, P20, P21]. The supporting evidence does not contain a detailed cashflow stress test, sensitivity analysis, or quantified loss scenarios. The KIID document [P11] identifies material risks including counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, and derivatives risk, but does not quantify loss scenarios. No document in the evidence pack contains specific calculations of how long funds would last under a 6% inflation scenario or under a 2007/8-style market crash scenario.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts two specific loss scenarios (6% inflation scenario and 2007/8 market crash scenario) with narrative outcomes, but no supporting document in the case file contains quantified versions of these stress tests or the underlying calculations that produced the conclusions stated.
      - The report states 'Cashflow analysis to assess whether you will have sufficient funds for retirement' was performed, but the specific stress test outputs or cashflow model runs showing the 6% inflation or market crash scenarios are not present in the evidence pack.
      - The report's loss scenarios describe outcomes (depletion at age 81 under inflation; funds lasting lifetime under market crash) but provide no loss percentage or loss amount figures, whereas the requirement [G8.4] expects loss scenarios to be illustrated quantitatively.
      - The report presents the market crash scenario as a positive outcome ('your assets would still last your lifetime') rather than as a loss scenario with quantified impact, which may conflate reassurance with loss illustration.
    The report asserts two loss scenarios, both derived from cashflow analysis, but neither scenario is quantified with a loss percentage or loss amount in the canonical model. The evidence contains generic product risk disclosures and a cashflow illustration, but does not include the detailed stress test calculations or sensitivity analysis outputs that would support the specific narratives stated. The 6% inflation scenario describes a specific outcome (depletion at age 81) which is a loss scenario, and is stated as occurring 'should this high level of inflation continue at the assumed rate'. The 2007/8 market crash scenario is framed as a resilience test (funds lasting lifetime), not as a loss scenario—it illustrates the client's capacity to absorb losses, not the magnitude of loss. Both scenarios are presented in the report but the underlying evidence (stress test outputs, sensitivity tables, or detailed cashflow runs) is absent from the case file. The Aviva Pension KFD [P1, P3] contains generic risk disclosures but not the client-specific stress test results. The SAP comparison documents [P19, P20, P21, P24] note that 'The fund available at retirement will depend upon the actual growth' and projections are not guaranteed, but do not present the specific stress scenarios. The extraction shows no quantified loss figures were present in the report's risk warnings section. The requirement demands that loss scenarios be 'illustrated'—which implies quantification or clear numerical impact—but the report's narratives lack this precision. The report does perform a cashflow analysis and bases its loss scenarios on it, so the comparison can be made: the report makes assertions about stress test outcomes without providing the supporting calculations in the evidence. This is a disclosure shortfall where material stress test analysis is referenced but the results are not documented in the case file.

  [G8.5] Disadvantages and risks are fairly and prominently presented
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that disadvantages and risks have a dedicated section with the heading 'Other Considerations of Switching' appearing on page 14, not only in an appendix. The report states it is designed to 'highlight any potential disadvantages and risks, as well as any other implications in terms of costs, charges and taxation' [P3]. Material disadvantages identified include: (1) switching risk—'if there is a fall in the market between the date of realising your existing funds and the purchase of your new funds, you could be financially disadvantaged' [P6]; (2) access timing—'10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes' [P6]; (3) performance uncertainty—'There is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement' [P6]; (4) capital risk—'unit prices and the value of your pension fund can fall as well as rise and there is no guarantee you will receive a return of your original capital' [P7]; (5) switching performance risk—'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement' [P7]; and (6) delegated risk disclosure—'All other risks associated with this investment are detailed in the product literature provided to you' [P6]. The report presents a multi-asset fund as offering 'growth potential of equities with the potentially uncorrelated returns of other asset classes' [P7] and states the funds are 'regularly reviewed by a quarterly investment committee' [P7]. Death benefit reduction is noted: existing arrangements provide £67,945 while the proposed Aviva solution provides £65,907, a reduction of £2,038, with the note that 'After transfer - the proposed Aviva Pension Portfolio (Choice) will provide 97% of the existing plan's death benefit' [P9]. The report also notes that at proposed retirement age 87, the PCLS under existing schemes is £33,600 but under Aviva is £26,600, a reduction of £7,000 [P13].
    File:     The case file contains supporting illustrations [P9], [P10], [P11], [P13] that show: (1) Death benefit comparison—existing arrangements total £67,945 (True Potential £46,672 + Nest £21,272) vs. proposed Aviva £65,907, a shortfall of £2,038 [P9]; (2) Projected fund value at age 87 under 'no charges' scenario—existing schemes £330,027 (at 5.88% growth) vs. Aviva £370,000, showing a +12.34% advantage to Aviva [P16]; (3) Projected fund value at age 87 under 'all charges' scenario—existing schemes £252,282 (at 5.88% growth) vs. Aviva with lower projected outcome implied by the comparison table structure [P13]; (4) PCLS comparison at age 87—existing schemes £33,600 vs. Aviva £26,600, a reduction of £7,000 [P13]; (5) Reduction in Yield (RiY) disclosure across scenarios: the 'all charges' illustration shows RiY of 1.5-1.6% for moving to Aviva [P1], while the 'no charges' illustration shows RiY of 0.4% and the 'IAC only' shows RiY of 1.0-1.1% [P10], [P11]. The report discloses that the critical yield (rate of return required) is in some scenarios lower for Aviva than the assumed growth rate (e.g., -0.6% to -0.63% required vs. 2.94-5.88% assumed) [P11], indicating a favourable switching outcome on this metric. A death benefit shortfall is explicitly flagged: 'If the death benefit available after switch is lower than that provided by the existing arrangements, consideration should be given to replacing the shortfall' [P9]. The supporting papers note Reduction in Yield assumptions used ('0.3% Fund AMC', '0.4% plan charges') [P10], [P11]. The case file contains an internal note that True Potential charges are listed as 0.40% platform + 0.50% ongoing advice + 0.58% fund management = 1.48%, but the replacement analysis shows only 0.98% current recurring charge for True Potential, a discrepancy flagged in the extraction metadata.
    Discrepancies:
      - PCLS reduction: report states existing schemes £33,600 vs. Aviva £26,600 (reduction of £7,000) [P13] but does not explicitly discuss this material disadvantage in the main narrative body or the 'Other Considerations' section; this is quantified only in the supporting illustration appendix.
      - Death benefit reduction: report discloses £2,038 shortfall (67,945 vs. 65,907) with note that proposed plan provides 97% of existing [P9], but the prominence and prominence of this disadvantage placement is unclear—it appears in the supporting illustration rather than the main 'Other Considerations' section.
      - Reduction in Yield variability: report presents multiple RiY figures (0.4%, 1.0-1.1%, 1.5-1.6%) depending on scenario [P1], [P10], [P11], but the main narrative does not explain why RiY differs across illustrations or what each scenario represents; the relationship between 'no charges', 'IAC only', and 'all charges' illustrations is not clearly explained in the narrative.
      - Fund growth projection inconsistency in supporting illustrations: 'no charges' scenario shows +12.34% advantage at all growth rates [P16], but 'all charges' scenario [P13] does not show equivalent projected figures for Aviva, making direct comparison unclear.
      - Critical yield appears negative in some scenarios (-0.6% to -0.63%) [P11], suggesting Aviva outperforms on return required, but the report narrative does not explain why a negative critical yield is favourable or what assumption this rests on.
    The requirement is to present material disadvantages, risks, tax consequences and loss scenarios fairly, clearly, accurately and prominently, consistent with the evidence. Comparison is performed: the report's assertion that a dedicated section exists on page 14 is confirmed [P3]. The main narrative does disclose several risks: switching risk, access timing, performance uncertainty, capital risk, and provider-switching risk [P6], [P7]. However, the evidence reveals material disadvantages that are quantified only in the supporting illustrations and are not adequately explained in the narrative body or the 'Other Considerations' section. The PCLS reduction of £7,000 at retirement age 87 (from £33,600 to £26,600) [P13] is a material disadvantage to the client's retirement income, yet it is not mentioned or explained in the report narrative passages provided—it appears only in the appendix illustrations. The death benefit shortfall of £2,038 is flagged in the supporting illustration [P9] with a statement that 'consideration should be given to replacing the shortfall', but this recommendation does not appear to be addressed in the main narrative, leaving it unclear whether the adviser has explained to the client how to mitigate this gap. The Reduction in Yield ranges from 0.4% to 1.6% depending on the illustration scenario [P1], [P10], [P11], but the report does not explain in the narrative which scenario is relevant to the client or why three different RiY figures are presented. This creates ambiguity about what cost impact the client should expect. The supporting illustrations show that in the 'no charges' scenario, the projected fund at age 87 is £370,000 (Aviva) vs. £330,027 (existing), a +12.34% advantage [P16], but the critical yield required is negative (-0.6% to -0.63%) [P11], which suggests the switch is favourable on a like-for-like basis—however, this counter-intuitive finding is not explained in the narrative. The narrative states 'all other risks' are 'detailed in the product literature' [P6], which is a delegation of risk disclosure to external documents rather than a prominent summary in the report itself. The extraction metadata notes that the report makes an internal consistency claim ('flexible income over guaranteed income' preference) that is not reconciled with the growth-oriented risk profile (7/10), and the cashflow analysis shows assets exceed retirement needs even under stress, which could support either a lower-risk approach or a deliberate capital accumulation strategy—but this is not explicitly addressed. The guard on narrative ordering applies: disadvantages scattered across the report in discrete passages, some appearing only in appendices, may still constitute 'prominent' presentation if they are clearly flagged and easy to find, but quantitative disadvantages (£7,000 PCLS loss, £2,038 death benefit loss) that appear only in appendix tables while benefits are highlighted in the main narrative suggest a prominence imbalance. Tax consequences are not addressed in the passages provided: no mention of annual allowance impact, lifetime allowance status, or tax-free cash threshold effects. Loss scenarios are not illustrated: there is no worked example showing what would happen if markets fell by a specific percentage, or what the fund would be worth if the client needed to access it in, say, year 3 of the switch. The report states cashflow shows sufficiency 'even following a fall in the market' [P5], but does not quantify such a scenario. The recommendation for a multi-asset fund is justified on grounds of diversification, expert management, and cost-efficiency [P7], but no direct comparison is made between this fund and any alternative (e.g., an investment pathway, a lower-risk option, or an annuity), despite the client's stated preference for 'flexible income' over 'guaranteed income'. The report does not explain why drawdown is suitable for the client or what the mechanics of drawdown entail—risk to capital, sustainability of withdrawals, sequencing risk, etc.—beyond the generic statement that 'The income from a drawdown contract is not guaranteed and will depend on future investment returns and levels of withdrawals' [P15].

  [G8.6] Benefits, features or protections the client is giving up
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states two features or protections being lost: (1) 'Plan is now paid up with no further contributions possible under current scheme structure' regarding one of the existing arrangements (Nest, stated on page 9); (2) 'Ongoing advice charge of 0.50% on True Potential plan no longer payable (replaced by 1% ongoing advice on Aviva platform)' (pages 8, 25). The report also confirms via extracted data that all three existing arrangements 'Do not contain any safeguardedbenefits'. On retirement options, the report records that Quilter and True Potential both offer 'FlexiAccessDrawdown, UFPLS, PartialUFPLS', while Nest offers only 'UFPLS, PartialUFPLS' (no flexi-access drawdown). The report states explicitly regarding Nest that 'Flexi-access drawdown is not available' (page 8).
    File:     The evidence confirms the features and constraints recorded in the report. Nest product literature [P5] confirms that the Nest Guided Retirement Fund (NGRF) 'is available for members to join between age 60 and 70' and describes a structured drawdown approach with Vault, Wallet, Safe and Later Life components, but does not reference flexi-access drawdown as an option. Nest literature [P5] describes the fund as 'designed to be more cautious' and aimed at 'members past State Pension age, who are able to withdraw their Nest pension savings', consistent with UFPLS and partial UFPLS but not flexible drawdown mechanics. True Potential fund literature [P11], [P14], [P15] provide OCF charges (0.54%, 0.63%) but do not detail the ongoing advice charge structure on the platform itself. Aviva Terms and Conditions [P20], [P22] describe investment options, commercial property, and unit pricing but do not explicitly state the 1% ongoing advice charge claimed in the report. The Aviva pension illustration [P21] does not specify ongoing advice charges. Product literature does not contradict the stated loss of the 0.50% True Potential ongoing advice charge or the move to a 1% charge on Aviva, but neither does it corroborate the 1% figure. No document in the file states that paid-up status on Nest prevents future contributions or that moving to Aviva changes this constraint; the paid-up status appears to be a Nest scheme feature, not unique to the current holder.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the ongoing advice charge changes from 0.50% (True Potential) to 1% (Aviva), but no passage in the case file corroborates the 1% Aviva figure.
      - The report states 'Plan is now paid up with no further contributions possible under current scheme structure' as a feature of Nest being lost, but the evidence does not clarify whether this is a Nest-specific constraint or a state already present, nor whether switching to Aviva restores contribution capacity.
      - The retirement options recorded for Nest show 'UFPLS, PartialUFPLS' but product literature on Nest [P5] describes a Guided Retirement Fund structure with Vault, Wallet, Safe and Later Life mechanics, which is distinct from standard UFPLS/partial UFPLS nomenclature and may mischaracterise the retirement access mechanism being given up.
    The report identifies two specific features being lost: (1) the paid-up status and no-further-contributions constraint on Nest, and (2) the difference in ongoing advice charges between True Potential (0.50%) and Aviva (1%). On the first point, the evidence does not establish whether this is a unique feature of Nest being surrendered or a universal scheme constraint. On the second point, the 0.50% charge on True Potential is stated in the extracted data but the 1% Aviva charge is not corroborated by any passage in the file; True Potential fund literature gives OCF figures (0.54%–0.63%) but does not state the platform-level ongoing advice charge, and Aviva literature does not state any ongoing advice charge. The retirement options table shows Nest as offering 'UFPLS, PartialUFPLS' only, excluding flexi-access drawdown, which the report explicitly notes on page 8. However, Nest product literature describes a Guided Retirement Fund with a specific drawdown structure (Vault, Wallet, Safe, Later Life) that is more sophisticated than standard UFPLS and may not be accurately captured by that label alone. The report does not explain what the client gains from moving away from this structured approach or how Aviva's flexible drawdown options compare in substance to the Nest Guided Retirement Fund model. The comparison between 'flexible income over guaranteed income' (stated client objective in the extraction notes) and the structured Nest drawdown approach is not addressed. The report confirms no safeguarded benefits exist in any arrangement, so no protected guarantees are being lost. The key omission is that the report does not explain the material difference between the Nest Guided Retirement Fund structure (which provides a managed drawdown with protective staging) and the proposed Aviva drawdown, or how this difference relates to the client's stated preference for 'flexible income' and their modest income target of £25,000–£30,000 annually. The ongoing advice charge increase (from 0.50% to 1%) is not explicitly stated in the report as a disadvantage or cost; it is mentioned only as a factual change, without prominence or explanation of its impact.

  [G8.7] Justification for increased costs or reduced projected outcomes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts three cost justifications: (1) 'Product charges are competitive' [P23, page 12]; (2) 'Blends active and passive investments to keep costs low while allowing active management' [P23, page 12]; (3) 'Recommendation based on comprehensive analysis of charges, performance, fund range and retirement options' [P23, page 8]. On Quilter switch: 'The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to the Quilter plan. We have negotiated a reduced platform charges of 0.19%, compared to the Quilter charges of 0.27%, representing a savings of £208 per annum' [P23]. On Nest switch: report states the Aviva plan 'is more expensive than your current Nest plan' and 'your proposed plan will need to outperform your current plan by 1.23% per annum to age 89' [P19]. The report discloses a disadvantage: 'The annual charges for the recommend plan are higher at 0.39% compared to 0.30%, representing an increase of £19.15 per annum' [extracted model]. Disadvantages stated include market timing risk, no guarantee of outperformance, and access delays (10-15 working days). No loss scenarios with numerical illustrations are provided. Tax consequences of pension transfer and drawdown are discussed generically (Lifetime Allowance removal, Lump Sum Allowance £268,275) but not applied to this client's specific position or withdrawal strategy.
    File:     Evidence supports charges on existing arrangements: Quilter 0.27% [P23], Nest 0.30% + 1.8% contribution charge [extracted model and P6], True Potential 0.98% [extracted model]. Aviva recommended solution: 0.39% total (0.19% platform + 0.20% fund management) [P17, P19]. Product literature (Vanguard 0.20% [P16], HSBC OCF 0.63% [P4], True Potential Schroders 1.19% [P5]) confirms fund charges but does not state Aviva's platform charge of 0.19%. On outperformance: Critical Yield analysis [P18] shows Aviva requires 0.01% annual outperformance vs True Potential and 1.23% vs Nest at 2.94% growth to match returns. No loss scenarios or stress-tested illustrations are provided in the evidence. SAP illustrations [P1, P2, P3, P6, P8] show projections at 0%, 2.94%, and 5.88% growth but these are generic comparison documents, not client-specific modelled outcomes. Death benefit comparison shows Aviva will provide 97% of existing arrangements' death benefit [P10, P11]. Product KIIDs state standard risk warnings but do not illustrate specific loss scenarios for this client. Tax information [P20] is generic: Lifetime Allowance removed 6 April 2024, Lump Sum Allowance £268,275, but client's personal tax position and impact of 25% PCLS plus income drawdown strategy is not worked through.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims 'Product charges are competitive' but evidence shows Aviva 0.39% is higher than Nest 0.30% (ignoring contribution charge) and comparable to True Potential 0.98% when all components counted. Justification does not acknowledge Nest's lower baseline.
      - Report states charges are 'higher at 0.39% compared to 0.30%' creating £19.15 annual cost increase, but this comparison is unclear: 0.30% applies only to Nest's ongoing charges, excluding its 1.8% contribution charge. The basis of comparison is not transparent to the client.
      - Report justifies switch by stating Aviva platform charge is 0.19% vs Quilter 0.27%, saving £208 per annum. However, the report does not clearly show whether this saving is offset by the fund charges (0.20%) or other costs. The 0.39% total to the client is not reconciled against what Quilter total charge would have been.
      - Report claims Aviva is 'more expensive than your current Nest plan' requiring 1.23% annual outperformance [P19], yet simultaneously claims charges are 'competitive' and the scheme has 'lower overall cost structure' [P23]. These statements are internally contradictory: if Aviva is more expensive and requires significant outperformance to match Nest, the competitive justification is undermined.
      - Report discloses no specific loss scenario. Generic statements about 'no guarantee of outperformance' and 'market movements' are present, but no numerical illustration of downside risk (e.g., 2007/8 crash outcome, client fund value at retirement under stress, sequence of returns risk) is provided, despite SAP illustrations [P1, P2, P3, P6, P8] containing 0% growth scenarios that could illustrate loss potential.
      - Report discusses tax consequences only in generic terms (Lifetime Allowance, Lump Sum Allowance) [P20]. Client objectives state preference for 'flexible income over guaranteed income' and retirement at 60 with £25,000–£30,000 annual income. No tax calculation is provided showing the impact of 25% PCLS withdrawal plus income tax on drawdown at the client's marginal rate, or comparison to annuity tax treatment.
      - Report states 'It provides a blend of active and passive investments, which allows for some active management, whilst keeping costs low' [P23] as a cost justification, but the fund mix (50% HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic 0.19%, 50% Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% 0.20% [P19]) does not corroborate this claim: both funds are actively/passively blended already, and the 0.20% blended charge is stated but no evidence that this is 'low' relative to alternatives is provided beyond the competitive claim.
    1. Cost increase acknowledgment: The report correctly identifies higher charges on Nest (0.39% vs 0.30% base rate = +£19.15 p.a.) as a disadvantage [extracted model]. This acknowledges the cost direction and is disclosed. However, the comparison basis is opaque: Nest's stated 0.30% ongoing charge excludes its 1.8% contribution charge. The report does not clarify whether the 'increase' comparison includes or excludes that contribution charge, making it difficult for the client to assess total cost impact.
    
    2. Quilter savings claim: The report justifies the switch from Quilter on platform charge savings (0.27% to 0.19%, £208 p.a.) [P23]. This is corroborated by evidence [extracted model shows Quilter 0.27%] and the arithmetic is correct. However, the report does not show the client what their *total* charge would be if they stayed with Quilter (i.e., 0.27% platform + fund charges on Quilter funds). Without this, the client cannot assess whether the 0.19% to 0.39% overall (fund management added) represents net value.
    
    3. True Potential/Nest outperformance requirement: Report states Aviva is 'more expensive' and requires 1.23% p.a. outperformance vs Nest [P19]. This is supported by Critical Yield analysis [P18], which shows the same rate of return required. This is a material disadvantage and is disclosed, but buried in the Appendix and not prominently featured in the main advice section where costs are justified.
    
    4. Competitive charges justification: The statement 'Product charges are competitive' [P23] is unsupported by comparative evidence in the file. No market comparisons, peer groups, or industry benchmarks are provided. The charge is stated as an assumed modality, not derived from research. This is weak justification, relying on assertion rather than evidence.
    
    5. Active/passive blend cost rationale: The claim that the solution 'keeps costs low' through active/passive blending [P23] does not connect cost to the specific fund choice. Both selected funds (HSBC and Vanguard) are themselves active/passive blends; this is a feature of those funds, not a feature of blending them together. The report does not explain why these specific funds, at 0.20% average charge, are lower cost than alternatives.
    
    6. Loss scenarios: The requirement asks for 'loss scenarios and potential consumer harms illustrated.' The report provides only generic warnings ('no guarantee of outperformance', 'market movements'). SAP illustrations [P1, P2, P3, P6, P8] include 0% growth scenarios, which effectively show an outcome where the fund matches inflation but does not grow; the report could have translated one of these into a client-specific loss scenario (e.g., 'at 0% growth, your fund at age 87 would be £60,100, compared to £53,497 if you stayed with the existing scheme'). This scenario exists in the evidence [P8] but is not reproduced in the main report or prominently disclosed to show the downside.
    
    7. Tax consequences: The report discusses general tax rules [P20]: Lifetime Allowance removed, Lump Sum Allowance £268,275, income tax on drawdown. However, it does not apply these to the client's stated objective (£25,000–£30,000 annual income, flexible drawdown preferred). For example: if the client draws 25% PCLS (£81,986 tax-free) plus £25,000 income, that income is taxed at marginal rate; if they were instead to use an annuity from the Nest scheme (which they rejected), the tax treatment differs. No calculation or comparison is provided. This is a generic disclosure, not client-specific.
    
    8. Internal contradiction: The report states Aviva has 'lower overall cost structure' than Quilter [P23] and 'competitive' charges in general [P23], but also that Aviva is 'more expensive than your current Nest plan' and requires 1.23% outperformance [P19]. These cannot both be true without clarification: competitive can mean 'lowest' or 'market-rate'. If Aviva is more expensive than Nest, the 'competitive' claim is misleading unless it means competitive with True Potential (which is 0.98% before platform, much higher than Aviva 0.39%). The report does not resolve this ambiguity.
    
    9. Justification structure: The three justifications given (competitive charges, active/passive blend, comprehensive analysis) do not address the specific cost increase. None of them say 'the cost increase of £19.15 p.a. on Nest and the 1.23% outperformance requirement is acceptable because [reason].' The guards note that 'a justification that repeats the benefit without connecting it to the cost is weak'; here, 'competitive' and 'blend' describe features but do not justify the cost relative to Nest. The comprehensive analysis justification is procedural (we did a full review) rather than outcome-based (we found Aviva better value despite higher Nest charges because [reason]).
    
    10. Prominence: Cost justifications appear in the main recommendation section [P23]. Disadvantages (including higher charges on Nest) appear on page 12 of the report [extracted model]. Loss scenarios and stress illustrations are absent from the main report; SAP comparisons are relegated to appendix-style documents [P1–P3]. The guards note: 'risk content appears only in an appendix while benefits are prominent' is a Moderate concern. Here, cost justifications are prominent, but the quantified disadvantage (1.23% outperformance needed vs Nest) is in the Appendix, and loss scenarios are not provided at all.

  [G8.8] Material risks or limitations identified in the evidence but not disclosed in the report
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report discloses material disadvantages, product-specific risks, and limitations. Material disadvantages disclosed: (1) Access delay of 10-15 working days for disinvestment and banking, stated as client-specific on page 14; (2) Lower absolute performance of recommended balanced funds versus higher-risk alternatives, stated on page 20. Product-specific risks disclosed: (1) Timing risk — market movements between realising existing funds and purchasing new funds, with potential for financial disadvantage or advantage, stated on page 14; (2) No outperformance guarantee — a switch does not guarantee greater pension fund or outperformance versus existing provider, stated on page 16. Limitations disclosed: (1) Projection limitations — small variances in assumptions can produce marked differences in outcomes over long periods, stated on page 22; (2) Long-term care costs not considered in projections, stated on page 22; (3) Cancellation limitation — previous provider not obliged to take plan back, stated on page 26.
    File:     The evidence includes product literature, fund research, and pension transfer analysis. From Aviva Pension KFD [P14]: transfers from defined benefit schemes may lose valuable rights or guaranteed income based on final salary; growth is dependent on future investment returns which can go down as well as up; the value of pension can go down as well as up and you may get back less than has been paid in; transferring pensions isn't right for everyone; it is a complex decision requiring consideration of charges, fund ranges, valuable benefits lost, and tax implications. From Aviva Pension T&C [P19]: the Aviva Pension Portfolio is aimed at clients with advisers as it is an advised platform. From Aviva KFD [P20]: disinvestment to pay income includes an additional amount which is the greater of £10 or 10% of disinvestment; commercial property and structured products excluded from disinvestment process; current favourable tax treatment may change if tax rules change; certain circumstances apply. From critical yield analysis [P3, P6]: the reduction in yield from existing scheme to Aviva ranges from 0.3% to 0.5% (depending on charges scenario) at different growth rates, with fund value comparison showing immediate loss on transfer (e.g., -31.35% effect shown in one scenario, -5.6% in another). From fund research [P13]: comparative fund performance data showing existing True Potential funds alongside proposed Aviva funds. From Nest fund documentation [P21, P22, P23, P24]: Nest Higher Risk fund composition and performance. From Morningstar data [P1]: standard risk disclosure that capital is at risk, investments can fluctuate and you may get back less than you invest; past performance is not guide to future performance.
    Discrepancies:
      -  Report does not disclose the immediate reduction in yield / fund value effect upon switching, shown in the critical yield analysis as ranging from -31.35% to -5.6% depending on charges treatment and growth scenarios [P3, P6].
      -  Report does not disclose the specific disinvestment cost structure (£10 or 10% of disinvestment amount, whichever is greater) identified in Aviva KFD [P20], only stating generic access delay.
      -  Report does not disclose the restriction that Aviva Pension Portfolio is aimed at clients with advisers on an advised platform [P19], and the consequences if advice relationship ends.
      -  Report does not prominently address tax consequences: while projection limitations mention assumptions, the Aviva KFD [P20] warns that current favourable tax treatment of pension may change if tax rules change — this specific tax consequence is not disclosed.
      -  Report does not disclose investment choice differences: Aviva KFD [P9] states 'You should be aware the investment choice available with Aviva may differ from your current providers. This could impact on the future performance of your fund' — this material limitation is not addressed in the report.
      -  Report discloses no loss scenario illustrating the financial impact of the switch; critical yield analysis [P3, P6] shows projected fund values under the new scheme are materially lower than existing scheme at all three growth rate scenarios (0%, 2.94%, 5.88%), but the report contains no explanation to the client of what this means for retirement income or outcomes.
      -  Report does not address that the client is transferring from defined benefit-like guaranteed structures (True Potential and Nest); Aviva KFD [P14] identifies loss of guaranteed income and final salary benefits as a material disadvantage of pension transfers, but the report does not address what protections or guarantees the client is giving up in their existing arrangements.
    The report discloses several generic risks (timing risk, no outperformance guarantee, projection limitations, long-term care not considered, cancellation provision) consistent with standard pension transfer warnings. However, material risks and limitations present in the supporting evidence are either absent or under-disclosed in the report. Most critically: (1) The critical yield analysis [P3, P6] demonstrates that under every growth scenario modelled, the proposed Aviva arrangement produces lower fund values than retention of existing arrangements, with a reduction in yield of 0.3% to 0.5% depending on charges treatment. This is a material disadvantage specific to this recommendation — a switch that guarantees a smaller fund — yet is not disclosed or explained to the client in the report. The report states 'no guarantee of outperformance' (generic) but does not disclose that the analysis affirmatively shows underperformance is the baseline projection. (2) The disinvestment cost structure [P20] is material to a client in drawdown: £10 or 10% of each disinvestment is a recurring charge beyond platform charges, yet the report discusses only the 10-15 day delay. (3) The restriction that Aviva is an advised platform [P19] is not disclosed; the client needs to understand they must maintain an advice relationship or face restrictions. (4) Tax rule change risk [P20] is mentioned in product literature but not in the report's tax consequences discussion. (5) Investment choice differences [P9] — Aviva may differ from existing providers — is not disclosed. (6) Most significantly, no loss scenario is provided: the critical yield tables show projected fund values at retirement under both existing and proposed schemes at multiple growth rates, permitting calculation of retirement income shortfall (e.g., at 2.94% growth: existing £48,651 vs proposed £33,400 = £15,251 shortfall at retirement), yet the report provides no such scenario analysis or explanation of what the transfer means in income terms. The extraction metadata notes that guaranteed annual amounts and guaranteed payout periods for existing arrangements are not present in the document, limiting the report's ability to disclose what guarantees are being lost; however, the critical yield analysis itself proves funds are lower, which is the material harm. (7) The report acknowledges the client expressed preference for 'flexible income over guaranteed income' but does not disclose that this preference conflicts with the projection analysis showing materially reduced funds available for such flexibility. These are not gaps in research but disclosure shortfalls in the report itself: material information present in the file — comparative fund projections, detailed charge breakdowns, specific tax and structural restrictions — is not communicated to the client. The guards on generic wording and research order do not apply: the critical yield analysis is not generic market risk language, it is client-specific comparative analysis of the two schemes, and it is in the case file. The reduction in yield / fund value is not a matter of different calculation bases: both existing and proposed schemes are projected at the same growth rates; the difference is the recommendation itself.

CHK-009 — Replacement or Switch Justification
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G9.1, G9.2, G9.3, G9.4, G9.5, G9.6, G9.7, G9.8, G9.9, G9.10). Report assesses True Potential charges as 'Better' when comparing 0.98% to 0.39%, but the comparison shows charges decrease; the logic contradicts the assessment label since lower charges are typically marked 'Better' yet the dimension shows an improvement. True Potential combined projections at all growth rates show -12.42% reduction versus Aviva, yet this is listed as 'Better' assessment; the projected fund values are lower under all scenarios. Nest charges comparison states 0.30% (Plan Charges) + 1.8% (Contribution charge) = total, but replacement analysis only uses 0.30% in impact calculations, excluding the 1.8% contribution charge component. 16 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G9.1] Comparison of existing versus recommended product and investments
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations unverified
    Report:   The report compares three existing arrangements (Quilter £260,000, True Potential £46,672.80, Nest £21,272.64) against a recommended Aviva Pension Portfolio (Choice). For Quilter, it asserts platform charges reduce from 0.27% to 0.19%, a 'Better' outcome. For True Potential, it claims charges worsen from 0.98% to 0.39%, marked 'Better' despite being worse. For Nest, it states charges increase from 0.30% (plus 1.8% contribution charge) to 0.39%, marked 'Worse'. Fund range improves from 6, 35, and 6 funds respectively to over 6,900. Retirement options for Quilter and True Potential remain equivalent; Nest improves from UFPLS-only to include flexi-access drawdown. Risk alignment for Nest improves from Risk Level 8 to 7. Adviser servicing for Quilter and True Potential remains equivalent; Nest improves from 'No servicing adviser allowed' to 'Adviser servicing included'. The report projects funds at age 87 under three growth scenarios (0%, 2.94%, 5.88%). For True Potential with all charges included, existing schemes project to £48,408 / £121,807 / £298,632 versus Aviva at £42,300 / £106,000 / £261,000, showing a reduction of -12.42% at all growth rates. For Nest with all charges included, existing projects to £48,408 / £121,807 / £298,632 (same as TP combined) versus Aviva, with a Reduction in Yield of 1.5%. No alternative options analysis is presented. No safeguarded benefits or guaranteed features of existing arrangements are addressed. No comparison of lost features is disclosed.
    File:     Annes M SAP analysis documents (multiple versions with different charge assumptions: 'All charges inc existing OAC', 'All charges ex existing OAC', 'IAC only ex existing OAC', 'no charges ex existing OAC') provide Aviva projections but reveal inconsistencies in how existing charges are applied. True Potential KFD shows existing fund performance data for seven holdings (ranging from -15.28% to +22.76% in 2023). Aviva KFD confirms flexi-access drawdown, self-select phased income drawdown, and capped drawdown options are available at the receiving scheme. The SAP analyses show at proposed age 87: (All charges inc existing OAC) Existing £48,408 / £121,807 / £298,632 vs Aviva £42,300 / £106,000 / £261,000 with RIY 1.5%; (All charges ex existing OAC) Existing £53,497 / £134,612 / £330,027 vs Aviva £60,100 / £151,000 / £370,000 with RIY 0.4%; (IAC only ex existing OAC) Existing £53,497 / £134,612 / £330,027 vs Aviva £58,200 / £146,000 / £359,000 with RIY 0.5%. These projections contradict each other when the same charge treatment is not applied. Nest pension details are incomplete in the retrieved passages. True Potential Defaqto holdings show past performance but the report does not compare historical fund performance between existing and recommended arrangements to justify switching. No analysis of Nest Higher Risk Fund (Risk Level 8) versus recommended portfolio alignment is provided. No safeguarded benefits analysis is on file.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report assesses True Potential charges as 'Better' when comparing 0.98% to 0.39%, but the comparison shows charges decrease; the logic contradicts the assessment label since lower charges are typically marked 'Better' yet the dimension shows an improvement
      - True Potential combined projections at all growth rates show -12.42% reduction versus Aviva, yet this is listed as 'Better' assessment; the projected fund values are lower under all scenarios
      - Nest charges comparison states 0.30% (Plan Charges) + 1.8% (Contribution charge) = total, but replacement analysis only uses 0.30% in impact calculations, excluding the 1.8% contribution charge component
      - SAP 'All charges inc existing OAC' projects Existing £48,408 / £121,807 / £298,632 with RIY blank, but SAP 'All charges ex existing OAC' projects Existing £53,497 / £134,612 / £330,027, a £5,089 / £12,805 / £31,395 difference at each growth rate depending on whether existing adviser charges are included
      - Quilter comparison states existing 0.27% versus recommended 0.19%, marked 'Better', but the comparison is conditional ('If you were to retain...'); no actual existing arrangement details for Quilter fund range or retirement options appear in the report's comparison section
      - True Potential comparison presents existing charges as 0.40% + 0.50% + 0.58% = 1.48% in the text, but replacement analysis uses 0.98%, a discrepancy of 0.50% (the ongoing advice charge) not explained in the comparison logic
      - Report comparison table shows True Potential Risk Alignment as N/A or unmarked, but no dimension comparison addresses why True Potential's existing funds were suitable at acquisition and are now being switched for risk reasons (only addressed for Nest)
      - Nest existing retirement options stated as 'Full and partial UFPLS only (Flexi-access drawdown not available)' versus recommended 'Flexi-access drawdown, full and partial UFPLS', but Aviva KFD [P10] confirms both options are available at Aviva; the distinction of 'benefit' depends on whether Nest genuinely lacks flexi-access drawdown
      - Report presents multiple SAP scenarios with different charge bases but provides no explanation of which scenario applies to the recommendation or why three different RIY figures (1.5%, 0.5%, 0.4%) represent the same recommendation
    The comparison dimension on Charges for True Potential contains an internal logic error. The report states existing 0.98% versus recommended 0.39%, which is a reduction of 0.59 percentage points and should be marked 'Better' for cost grounds. However, the projected fund values at retirement show a -12.42% reduction across all growth scenarios when moved to Aviva, contradicting the cost advantage. This suggests either (a) the charges comparison is on a different basis than the projections, (b) performance differs between the funds in a way that offsets the cost saving, or (c) the comparison mixes inclusive and exclusive charge bases. The extraction flagged internal inconsistency: True Potential charges in the existing arrangements table are listed as 0.40% + 0.50% + 0.58% = 1.48%, but the replacement analysis uses 0.98%, which equals 0.40% + 0.58% only (excluding the 0.50% ongoing advice charge). This suggests the comparison may be excluding ongoing adviser services, yet the report recommends ongoing servicing with Aviva at 0.39% (platform 0.19% + investment 0.20%). If the 0.39% includes servicing, it is not directly comparable to 0.98% excluding it. No explanation of these bases is provided in the report. For Nest, the comparison lists existing charges as 0.30% + 1.8% but the SAP analysis calculates impact using 0.30% only, excluding the contribution charge. At £21,272.64 fund value, 1.8% annually equals approximately £382 per year, which is material. The report does not disclose whether this charge applies indefinitely, ceases after initial contributions, or applies only to future contributions; without this, the comparison is incomplete. The SAP charge scenario discrepancy is material: 'All charges inc existing OAC' produces a 1.5% RIY and -12.42% fund reduction, while 'All charges ex existing OAC' produces 0.4% RIY and +12.34% improvement. These cannot both describe the same recommendation. The report provides no statement of which scenario was adopted for the advice. This is a fundamental gap: the recommendation cannot be evaluated against the projections if the charge basis is ambiguous. For Nest, the report states existing retirement options as 'Full and partial UFPLS only (Flexi-access drawdown not available)' versus recommended 'Flexi-access drawdown, full and partial UFPLS' marked 'Better'. Aviva's KFD [P10] confirms flexi-access drawdown is available, but there is no documentation confirming that Nest genuinely lacks this option; if Nest also offers it, the 'Better' assessment for retirement options is incorrect. No safeguarded benefits analysis is provided for any of the three existing schemes. Nest is described as an auto-enrolment workplace pension, which typically carries safeguarded benefits not available in personal pensions; none are mentioned. No alternative options are considered or dismissed with reasoning; investment pathways are rejected (per P1) but no other platforms, consolidation without switching, or retention are discussed. The Quilter comparison is presented as conditional ('If you were to retain...') yet marked as though it is an actual existing holding; the extract states 0.27% charges but notes in [I] passage that this is hypothetical wording, creating ambiguity about whether Quilter is a current holding or a comparison baseline. The report does not address why Quilter (if held) is being replaced or what features it offers relative to Aviva. Overall, the comparison framework shows multiple inconsistencies in charge bases, contradictions between cost assessments and fund projections, missing documentation of existing scheme features (safeguards, retirement options confirmation, contribution arrangements), and no documented alternative analysis. These gaps prevent full verification of consistency between the recommendation and supporting evidence.
