  [G9.2] Benefits and costs of switching, including where benefits outweigh higher costs
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report asserts three separate switches: (1) Quilter to Aviva, claiming lower overall cost structure with annual savings of £208 based on 0.27% vs 0.19%, representing a -0.08% differential; (2) True Potential to Aviva, claiming a cost increase of £19.15 annually at +0.09% differential; (3) Nest to Aviva, claiming a cost decrease with -0.59% differential and annual savings of £275.37. Benefits claimed across switches include: lower costs, adviser ability to actively monitor and adjust investments, current fund not in line with agreed risk level of 7, wider range of investment options, greater choice if risk profile changes, more sophisticated adviser-managed approach, online platform access, and more flexible retirement options with tax-efficient withdrawal strategies. Initial advice fee of 3% (£9,838.36) is disclosed. No exit penalties are stated for any plan. The report states death benefits will be 97% of existing under Aviva.
    File:     The case file contains Aviva product documentation (P9-P12) and multiple Statutory Adviser Portal (SAP) reports (P1-P8, P13-P16) with projected fund comparisons and scenarios. The SAP reports show fund projections with charges under different assumptions (all charges ex existing OAC, no charges, IAC only, all charges inc existing OAC), illustrating critical yields and projected fund values at retirement age 87. P14 shows Nest comparison with 0.3% fund AMC and critical yield of +0.09%, which arithmetically matches Nest's stated 0.30% charge. P16 shows True Potential comparison with fund AMC of 0.58% and platform charges of 0.40%, showing critical yield of -0.60% to -0.63%. P13 shows death benefit comparison: existing arrangements total £67,945 (True Potential £46,672 + Nest £21,272) vs Aviva £65,907, representing 97% of existing. No evidence is provided of comparisons of fund ranges, adviser servicing capabilities, online platform features, or flexibility of retirement options between existing and proposed arrangements. No evidence discloses the 0.19% charge figure for Aviva or reconciles the charge differential calculations. The SAP reports provide projected outcomes but do not explicitly compare or justify qualitative benefits claimed.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims Quilter plan has 0.27% charges; file confirms 0.27% conditional phrasing in P7 but no corroborating evidence of proposed Aviva charge at 0.19% — the 0.27% vs 0.19% comparison basis cannot be verified
      - Report claims £208 annual saving for Quilter switch; derived figure of £208 at 0.27% implies £77,037 fund value, which matches no existing arrangement value (Quilter is £260,000) — basis of calculation unexplained and unmatched to actual fund value
      - Report claims True Potential cost differential of +0.09% with £19.15 annual impact; P14 shows critical yield of +0.09% for Nest, not True Potential, and the £19.15 figure matches Nest's value (0.09% on £21,272.64), not True Potential (0.98% shown in replacement analysis)
      - Report states True Potential current annual charge as 0.98% in replacement analysis; existing arrangements table shows 0.40% platform + 0.50% ongoing advice + 0.58% fund charges = 1.48% total; the 0.98% figure excludes the 0.50% ongoing advice charge but lacks explicit justification for this exclusion
      - Report claims Nest cost difference of -0.59%; P14 shows Nest critical yield of +0.09%, not -0.59%, creating fundamental inconsistency in the switching cost analysis
      - Report claims £275.37 annual saving for Nest; derived figure of £275.37 at 0.59% on £46,672 (True Potential value) does not align to Nest's £21,272.64, confusing which plan this figure applies to
      - Report discloses initial advice fee of 3% (£9,838.36) as switching cost; no evidence in file addresses whether this fee is offset by claimed benefits or how payback period compares to switching costs
      - Report claims current fund 'not in line with agreed risk level of 7'; no evidence provided comparing existing fund composition or risk characteristics to proposed Aviva risk profile
      - Report claims 'wider range of investment options' for Aviva; no comparative fund range analysis provided in file
      - Report claims 'more flexible retirement options' and 'tax-efficient withdrawal strategies'; no evidence provided comparing existing vs proposed retirement access features
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Aviva platform charge percentage — reported as 0.19% but not evidenced in file, Reconciliation of True Potential cost differential between 1.48% total stated charges and 0.98% used in replacement analysis, Reconciliation of Nest cost differential: report shows -0.59% but critical yield analysis shows +0.09%, Basis for attribution of £208 saving to Quilter switch — calculation does not reconcile to Quilter's actual £260,000 fund value, Basis for attribution of £275.37 saving — figure appears to relate to True Potential but is referenced under Nest switch, Fund composition and risk profile characteristics of existing arrangements vs Aviva to support claim that current fund not aligned to risk level 7, Comparative analysis of investment fund ranges available under existing arrangements vs Aviva, Comparative analysis of retirement option flexibility between existing and proposed arrangements, Break-even analysis or payback calculation for 3% initial advice fee (£9,838.36) against claimed annual savings, Evidence from Aviva product documentation of online platform features, adviser monitoring capabilities, or withdrawal flexibility claims
    The requirement demands that switching benefits and costs be consistent with and supported by evidence, with particular attention to whether higher costs are justified by benefits. The comparison cannot be performed reliably across all three planned switches due to fundamental arithmetic and methodological problems. For the Quilter switch: the report claims £208 savings at 0.27% existing vs 0.19% proposed; however, P14 confirms Quilter's charge but the 0.19% Aviva figure is not evidenced in the file. The £208 figure, when reverse-calculated, implies a £77,037 fund value, which does not match Quilter's stated £260,000, indicating the calculation is either based on incomplete data or on a different figure than the actual transfer value. For True Potential: the report shows 0.98% in the replacement analysis against 1.48% stated in existing arrangements (0.40% + 0.50% + 0.58%), creating a +0.50% discrepancy. The internal extraction notes suggest the 0.50% ongoing advice charge may have been excluded from the replacement analysis basis, but this methodological choice is not explained in the report and makes the two charge figures non-comparable without clarification. The £19.15 annual cost cited appears to derive from a 0.09% differential on Nest (£21,272.64), not True Potential, compounding the confusion. For Nest: the report claims -0.59% cost reduction and £275.37 savings; however, P14 critical yield analysis shows Nest at +0.09% required return, not -0.59%, and the £275.37 figure when reverse-calculated at 0.59% implies a £46,672.64 fund — True Potential's value, not Nest's. This suggests either data misallocation between plans or calculation error. The 3% initial advice fee of £9,838.36 is disclosed but no payback analysis is provided showing when annual savings would offset this lump cost. Benefits are claimed qualitatively (adviser monitoring, fund range, flexibility, risk alignment) but no supporting evidence is provided: no fund range comparison tables, no retirement option feature matrix, no existing fund composition analysis, no adviser service specification documentation. The death benefit comparison (P13) shows Aviva provides 97% of existing value (£65,907 vs £67,945), a £2,038 shortfall, which is disclosed but not reconciled to recommendations or considered in the switching justification. The report asserts benefits outweigh costs but provides no analytical framework (e.g., Net Present Value, critical yield comparison relative to benefits, or explicit trade-off narrative) to support this claim. The charge comparison tables in the report reference page 12 and 21 'Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment' but these are internal [I] passages and therefore cannot corroborate the assertions made. The vulnerability overlay extract notes client preference for 'flexible income over guaranteed income' but this preference is not mapped to evidence about existing vs proposed flexibility features.

  [G9.3] Details of lost features and guarantees
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report identifies two features or guarantees being lost: (1) 'Plan is now paid up with no further contributions possible under current scheme structure' for the Quilter arrangement; (2) 'Ongoing advice charge of 0.50% on True Potential plan no longer payable (replaced by 1% ongoing advice on Aviva platform)'. The report states that all three existing arrangements 'Do not contain any safeguarded benefits' [P10]. No employer contributions are stated as lost on any of the three arrangements.
    File:     The Aviva pension illustrations [P7, P12, P18, P20, P21, P22, P23] show comparative fund projections, death benefits, and critical yields between existing arrangements and the proposed Aviva platform. The evidence confirms the Nest arrangement contains no safeguarded benefits [P24]. Death benefit analysis [P8] shows existing total of £67,945 (True Potential £46,672 + Nest £21,272) compared to Aviva's £65,907, a reduction of £2,038 or 3%. The Quilter arrangement is described as 'paid up' but no confirmation is provided of what this means in terms of scheme structure or contribution capacity. The True Potential ongoing advice charge of 0.50% is confirmed in the report's charge table, and the Aviva platform charges 1% ongoing advice (stated in narrative and illustrations). No scheme documentation provided confirming absence of guarantees on Quilter or True Potential arrangements.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts all three arrangements contain no safeguarded benefits; no scheme booklets or provider confirmation documents provided to verify this claim, particularly for Quilter and True Potential
      - Death benefit reduction of 3% (£2,038) is identified in illustrations but not addressed in the narrative replacement rationale or comparative analysis
      - Report identifies loss of 0.50% ongoing advice charge on True Potential but replaces it with 1% on Aviva (net cost increase of 0.50% annually), yet does not explain why this cost increase is justified as a benefit of switching
      - Quilter arrangement described as 'paid up with no further contributions possible' but no evidence of what scheme structure permits or prohibits contributions, and no analysis of whether this is a feature being lost or a pre-existing constraint
    The report asserts that all three existing arrangements contain no safeguarded benefits, stated in the canonical model with provenance to page 7-8 of the report. However, this assertion lacks corroboration by scheme documentation. Under the guard that 'an explicit no safeguarded benefits statement in the report must still be corroborated by policy documentation', the report's internal statement is not evidence of the absence of guarantees. No scheme booklets or provider confirmation documents are present in the evidence pack to verify this claim for Quilter or True Potential. The Nest arrangement is confirmed as containing no safeguarded benefits in [P24], satisfying the guard for that plan only. The death benefit reduction of £2,038 (3%) when switching Nest and True Potential to Aviva is documented in [P8] as 'After transfer - the proposed Aviva Pension Portfolio (Choice) will provide 97% of the existing plan's death benefit', but the report does not address this in the replacement narrative or explain whether the loss is acceptable given other benefits. The Quilter 'paid up' status is mentioned but no evidence establishes whether contributions are structurally prohibited or whether this is a scheme-specific rule. The replacement of a 0.50% ongoing advice charge with a 1% charge represents a net cost increase of 0.50% per annum on the True Potential fund value (£46,672.80), which contradicts a narrative framing of cost savings from the switch. The illustrations show this higher charge in Aviva projections but the report does not justify why a higher annual cost supports the recommendation. The comparison cannot be fully performed because the scheme documentation needed to confirm or deny the presence of guarantees on the existing arrangements is absent from the file.

  [G9.4] Alternatives to the switch considered
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states that four broad alternatives were considered: (1) Leave the existing pension scheme untouched (DoNothing); (2) Leave the existing pension scheme in place and switch investment funds (InSituSwitch); (3) Switch the existing pension scheme into another existing pension arrangement (AlternativeProduct); (4) Switch existing schemes into employer's pension arrangement (EmployerScheme). The report also states that Stakeholder Pensions were considered and rejected on three grounds: (a) maximum charges of 1.5% per annum for first 10 years then 1% thereafter, making them more expensive than the recommended plan; (b) typically do not allow flexible access, contrary to client objective OBJ5 for flexible income; (c) have limited fund ranges, contrary to objective OBJ3. No rejection reasons are stated for Options 1–4.
    File:     P2 lists the same four broad options in a table format, presenting them as alternatives compared against the recommendation to place funds on the Aviva platform. The passage states 'I conducted a full review of your existing pension scheme in relation to the charges, investment performance, accessible fund range, and retirement options available' and 'I then compared each pension scheme against the most suitable alternative option from the following' followed by Options 1–5. P2 does not provide explicit rejection reasoning for any of the four broad alternatives (Options 1–4). P2 does not mention Stakeholder Pensions at all. Passages P4, P6, P8 are Suitability Analysis Projections (SAP) documents providing comparative financial projections under different charge scenarios (all charges, IAC only, no charges) but do not articulate why alternatives were rejected. P21 (Aviva KFD) includes generic language that 'Transferring pensions isn't right for everyone' and mentions considering 'charges, fund ranges, any valuable benefits that could be lost' but does not apply this to the specific alternatives the client considered or to the four broad options listed.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts that four broad alternatives (Options 1–4) were considered but provides no rejection reasoning for any of them in the canonical model, whereas the decision logic requires alternatives to be compared and reasons for discounting them to be evident.
      - The report mentions Stakeholder Pensions as a considered alternative with three specific rejection reasons, but this alternative and its rejection reasoning do not appear in the retrieved passages (P2 shows Options 1–5 without naming Stakeholder Pensions or elaborating on any option's rejection).
      - Option 5 in P2 is titled 'Set up a new pension plan to move your existing pension schemes into' but the canonical model extracted only four alternatives and does not capture Option 5 or any rejection reasoning for it.
      - The canonical model states that Options 1–4 have empty rejectionReasons arrays, suggesting these alternatives were considered but not analysed; the decision logic requires that material reasons for discounting alternatives be demonstrated, yet none are evident for the four broad options.
    The requirement is that alternatives to switching are considered and supported by evidence. The report asserts that four broad alternatives were considered (leave untouched, switch funds in situ, switch to another existing arrangement, switch to employer scheme), and that Stakeholder Pensions were considered and rejected. However, a critical distinction must be drawn between presence and justification. P2 (the report itself, category I) lists the four options in a table but does not articulate why each was rejected or what analysis led to the recommendation to switch to Aviva instead. The fact that options are enumerated does not constitute evidence that they were meaningfully compared or that the comparison was documented. The Suitability Analysis Projections (P4, P6, P8 — category G) provide financial modelling under different charge scenarios, which could support a cost-based comparison, but they do not explicitly state which alternative each scenario represents or why alternatives were preferred or rejected. P21 (Aviva KFD, category H) provides generic risk warnings about transfers but does not apply them to the specific alternatives the client faced. The Stakeholder Pension rejection reasoning (charges, lack of flexibility, limited funds) is stated in the report (P2 includes language about Stakeholder pensions having maximum charges of 1.5% for 10 years then 1%, and typically not allowing flexible access), corroborating that this alternative was considered and reasons were articulated. However, the four broad alternatives show no such articulation in any document. The guard 'Options assessed once for the whole case may legitimately cover several plans' applies here: if the report assessed that none of the four broad alternatives suited the client (e.g., because transferring to existing pensions offered no advantage, employer scheme was no longer available post self-employment, and in-situ fund switching was insufficient), a single analytical statement could cover all plans. Yet no such statement appears. The SAP documents show projections for the three existing arrangements under different charge assumptions but do not state explicitly which option each represents or why switching to Aviva outperformed the status quo for each plan. The client's position — recently self-employed, no longer entitled to Nest contributions, divorced, expecting inheritance — is described but not linked to specific reasons for rejecting alternatives. The decision logic requires that 'For every ceding plan the report compares existing against recommended, discloses the costs and benefits of switching, addresses safeguarded benefits and penalties, and considers alternatives — all corroborated by categories E and G.' The comparison on costs is partially present (SAP projections compare fund values), but the articulation of why alternatives were rejected is absent or generic, not plan-specific where plans differ materially.

  [G9.5] Switching costs exceed the benefits
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report recommends switching three existing pension arrangements (Quilter £260,000, True Potential £46,672.80, Nest £21,272.64, total £327,945.44) to Aviva Pension Portfolio (Choice). For the Quilter arrangement, it claims annual savings of £208 based on platform charges differential of -0.08% (0.27% existing vs 0.19% proposed). For Nest, it asserts a 'relatively small increase of £19.15 per annum' based on +0.09% charge differential. For True Potential, it states -0.59% differential representing annual savings of £275.37. The report discloses an initial advice fee of £9,838.36 (3% of transfer values). It asserts no exit penalties (£0). It states the proposed plan can underperform by 0.01% per annum to age 89 (Nest) and will need to outperform by 1.23% per annum to age 89 (one scenario). The report claims benefits include lower overall cost structure, adviser active management, risk alignment with level 7 profile, wider fund range, flexibility, tax-efficient retirement options, and better servicing via online platform. It addresses market timing risk ('could be advantaged or disadvantaged depending on market direction') but concludes benefits justify the switch.
    File:     The evidence pack contains three Aviva Suitability Analysis documents with different charge scenarios (no charges, IAC only, all charges ex existing OAC). These show Reduction in Yield (RIY) calculations comparing True Potential and Nest to Aviva. For True Potential under 'no charges' scenario: RIY is 0.4% across all growth assumptions (0%, 2.94%, 5.88%), with critical yield rates of -0.6%, 2.33%, 5.25%. For Nest, the documents do not provide separate RIY comparisons. The 'all charges' scenario shows at age 87 existing schemes project to £33,600 PCLS vs Aviva's £26,600 — a £7,000 reduction in tax-free cash. Under 'IAC only' scenario, Aviva projects £36,600 PCLS vs existing £33,600. The Aviva platform charges are stated as 0.25% pa with a decrease of 0.08% (net 0.17% for standard platform). Fund AMC is 0.58%. Initial fund allocation shows Weighted Average Fund Cost of 0.2154%. No explicit switching cost disclosure, fund value loss analysis, or charge comparison table for all three arrangements appears in the evidence. The evidence does not independently corroborate the £208 annual saving or the -0.08% differential for Quilter. The evidence does not address safeguarded benefits, guarantees, or employer contributions for any arrangement. No alternative options analysis is present. No explicit mention of the 3% fee or £9,838.36 appears in the evidence documents.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Quilter platform charges 0.27% vs Aviva 0.19%, claiming -0.08% differential and £208 annual savings; evidence shows Aviva platform charges as 0.25% pa with 0.08% decrease (0.17% net), not 0.19% — basis for -0.08% calculation is unclear from evidence.
      - Report claims £208 annual savings on Quilter based on charge differential; evidence does not provide an explicit switching cost or benefit analysis table for Quilter or show this £208 figure.
      - Report states True Potential annual charge difference of +0.09% (representing £19.15 increase); evidence RIY for True Potential is 0.4% across scenarios, not +0.09%.
      - Report states True Potential switching creates -0.59% charge differential with £275.37 annual savings; this contradicts the +0.09% increase stated elsewhere for True Potential and is not evidenced in the pack.
      - Report discloses 3% initial advice fee of £9,838.36; this figure does not appear in the evidence pack for independent verification.
      - Report states Nest charge differential +0.09% and £19.15 increase; evidence does not contain a separate Nest switching cost analysis table.
      - Report projects PCLS at retirement: under 'all charges' scenario, existing schemes £33,600 vs Aviva £26,600 (£7,000 loss); under 'IAC only' scenario £33,600 vs £36,600 (£3,000 gain). Report does not disclose or address this significant variation in projected PCLS depending on charge scenario applied.
      - Report addresses market timing risk as potentially advantageous or disadvantageous but does not quantify impact; evidence does not provide out-of-market exposure analysis.
      - Report does not disclose safeguarded benefits, guarantees, or valuable features that may be lost in any arrangement; extraction metadata indicates these fields are NotPresentInDocument.
      - Report does not explicitly consider or disclose alternatives to switching.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Independent verification of the 3% initial advice fee (£9,838.36) — not present in evidence pack., Explicit switching cost breakdown by arrangement (Quilter, True Potential, Nest separately) — the evidence pack contains only high-level RIY comparisons for True Potential and Nest, not detailed cost-benefit analysis by arrangement., Basis for Quilter charge comparison (0.27% to 0.19%) — Quilter is not present in the evidence documents; only Aviva charge structure is disclosed., Quantified analysis of safeguarded benefits, guarantees, or valuable features potentially lost in each existing arrangement — explicitly absent per extraction metadata., Guaranteed annual amounts and guaranteed payout periods for existing arrangements — explicitly marked NotPresentInDocument., Alternative options considered and comparison analysis — no evidence of alternatives assessment., Out-of-market exposure quantification and switching timeline — only generic narrative provided., Consistent charge scenarios applied: evidence shows multiple scenarios (no charges, IAC only, all charges ex OAC) with different outcomes; report does not specify which scenario basis supports its recommendations.
    The comparison cannot be reliably performed because the evidence pack does not support the report's stated charge differentials or corroborate the switching cost calculations. Three material gaps prevent assessment: (1) The report's charge comparison for Quilter (0.27% to 0.19%, yielding -0.08% and £208 savings) has no basis in the evidence; Quilter does not appear in the evidence documents, and Aviva's platform charge structure (0.25% with 0.08% decrease available) differs from the 0.19% figure stated. (2) For True Potential, the report asserts both +0.09% increase (£19.15) and -0.59% decrease (£275.37) in different sections, which contradict each other; the evidence shows RIY of 0.4% across growth assumptions but does not isolate the charge differential component or validate either figure. (3) For Nest, the report claims +0.09% and £19.15 increase; the evidence documents do not contain a separate Nest analysis table, preventing independent verification. The 3% initial advice fee (£9,838.36) is disclosed in the report but does not appear in the evidence pack. The evidence pack contains three parallel scenarios (no charges, IAC only, all charges ex OAC) producing different outcomes — for example, PCLS projections vary from £26,600 (all charges scenario) to £36,600 (IAC only scenario) at proposed retirement age 87, a £10,000 swing — yet the report does not disclose which scenario underpins its recommendations or acknowledge this material variance. The report does not address safeguarded benefits, guarantees, or valuable features for any arrangement (extraction metadata confirms these fields NotPresentInDocument). No alternative to switching is considered. The report addresses market timing risk only generically ('could be advantaged or disadvantaged'), with no quantification. The extraction metadata identifies internal inconsistencies in charge calculations (Quilter 0.27% stated as unqualified but conditionally formulated; Nest contribution charges 1.8% noted but not integrated into switching analysis; True Potential recurring charge shown as 0.98% vs 1.48% component total). These internal inconsistencies undermine confidence that the switching cost analysis is correctly formulated. The requirement calls for evidence supporting stated advantages and disadvantages, charges and switching costs, claims that higher costs are justified by benefits, and treatment of safeguarded benefits — none of these are adequately evidenced. The report makes cost-benefit claims but the evidence does not provide the detailed comparison tables, cost breakdowns, or benefit quantification necessary to corroborate them.

  [G9.6] Lost guarantees or benefits disadvantage the client
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report states that all three existing arrangements (Quilter, True Potential, and Nest) have 'No charges for transfer and/or switching'. The report recommends transferring all three arrangements to Aviva Pension Portfolio (Choice). The report does not explicitly address what safeguarded benefits, guarantees, or other valuable features might be lost as a result of the transfer. The extraction model shows that /existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp is absent from the report.
    File:     The case file contains multiple analysis documents (passages P1, P2, P3, P4, P5, P6, P7, P9, P13) that provide death benefit comparisons. These show: existing arrangements total death benefit of £67,945 (True Potential £46,672 + Nest £21,272) versus Aviva Pension Portfolio (Choice) death benefit of £65,907, representing 97% of the existing plans' death benefit. The documents note: 'If the death benefit available after switch is lower than that provided by the existing arrangements, consideration should be given to replacing the shortfall.' The file also contains passages [P1, P2, P7] addressing protected pension rights: 'Some individuals may have a higher allowance if they have registered for Protection (This is only relevant if you have a Protected Pension Commencement Lump Sum in respect of the benefits accrued by the scheme before 6th April 2006).' However, the file does not provide evidence that the existing arrangements contain guaranteed income provisions, guaranteed minimum income levels, or other crystallised safeguards beyond death benefits that would be lost on transfer.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report does not disclose or analyse what safeguarded benefits or guarantees are present in the existing arrangements
      - The report does not explicitly address whether any valuable features (guaranteed income, annuity guarantees, protected rights, income guarantees) would be lost on transfer
      - The death benefit reduction from £67,945 to £65,907 (3% shortfall) is identified in the evidence as requiring consideration but is not discussed in the suitability report narrative
      - The report asserts transfer with no penalties but does not address whether guarantees or protected benefits represent a form of valuable 'cost' of switching that should be weighed
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Whether the existing arrangements contain guaranteed minimum income provisions, defined benefit elements, or other safeguarded benefits beyond death benefits, Whether any of the three existing arrangements (Quilter, True Potential, Nest) have Protected Pension Commencement Lump Sum status or other A-Day protections that would be lost, Explicit statement from the report or file confirming the nature and value of any safeguarded benefits being given up, Report's own analysis of whether the 3% reduction in death benefit (£2,038) represents a material loss requiring replacement or justification
    The trigger for this requirement is satisfied: the suitability report recommends switching existing arrangements (Quilter £260,000, True Potential £46,672.80, Nest £21,272.64) to a new provider. The comparison method is NarrativeAlignment with a guard: the analysis only applies where G9.3 identified a benefit actually being given up. The file evidence shows one quantified benefit loss: death benefits reduce from £67,945 to £65,907, a £2,038 shortfall (3%). This is presented in the evidence [P1, P2, P7] with explicit guidance: 'If the death benefit available after switch is lower than that provided by the existing arrangements, consideration should be given to replacing the shortfall.' However, this analysis and the required consideration do not appear in the suitability report itself. The suitability report is silent on whether guarantees, protected rights, or other valuable features exist in the three existing arrangements. The extraction metadata notes that /existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp is absent (NotPresentInDocument), and /existingArrangements[]/guaranteedAnnualAmount is absent for all three arrangements. The evidence files [P1, P2, P7] reference Protected Pension Commencement Lump Sum status as a potential protection under older schemes (pre-6 April 2006), but do not confirm whether any of these three personal pensions hold such status. Nest is a modern workplace scheme established post-2000 and unlikely to hold A-Day protections. True Potential and Quilter are described as personal pensions with no indication of defined benefit elements or guaranteed income provisions. The file contains no evidence of annuity guarantees, income guarantees, or other crystallised safeguards in these arrangements. The comparison cannot be completed because: (1) the report does not state what safeguarded benefits, if any, exist in the existing arrangements; (2) the evidence does not confirm the existence or absence of safeguarded benefits beyond death benefits; (3) the report does not address whether the 3% death benefit shortfall requires replacement strategy. A comparison requires establishment of what is being given up; that has not been established in either the report or the file beyond the quantified death benefit reduction. The death benefit reduction itself is documented in the evidence but not addressed or justified in the report narrative.

  [G9.7] Other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that five alternatives were considered: (1) Leave existing pension untouched, (2) Leave existing pension in place and switch investment funds, (3) Switch existing pension into another existing arrangement, (4) Switch into employer's pension arrangement, and (5) Set up a new pension plan (recommended). For alternatives 1-4, no rejection reasons are stated. Stakeholder Pensions are rejected on three grounds: higher maximum charges (1.5% for 10 years then 1% vs recommended 0.39%), lack of flexible access (client objective OBJ5), and limited fund ranges (client objective OBJ3). Investment pathways are rejected because they do not reflect individual circumstances as well as the recommended strategy, require more tailoring, need ongoing professional advice, and have underperformed the recommended strategy. The report recommends moving all three existing schemes (Quilter £260,000, True Potential £46,672.80, Nest £21,272.64) into Aviva Pension Portfolio (Choice) based on lower platform charges (0.19% vs Quilter 0.27%) and ability to actively monitor and adjust investments.
    File:     The evidence includes three Suitability Analysis & Projection (SAP) documents prepared as of 24 March 2026, comparing existing schemes against Aviva Pension Portfolio (Choice) across scenarios with and without charges. The SAP documents show projected fund values at age 87 at three growth rates (0%, 2.94%, 5.88%). Document [P4] shows True Potential and Nest projections at those growth rates, and document [P5] confirms Aviva AMC of 0.25% with a decrease of 0.08% (net 0.17%), fund charges for selected funds (HSBC 0.21%, Vanguard 0.2207%, weighted average 0.2154%), totalling 0.3854% without initial advice charges. Document [P6] compares projected PCLS at retirement: Existing Schemes £30,400 vs Aviva £26,600 (all-charges scenario). Document [P8] in IAC-only scenario shows Existing Schemes £33,600 vs Aviva £36,600. Document [P9] shows no-charges inflation-adjusted projections: Aviva at 0% growth £60,100, 2.94% growth £151,000, 5.88% growth £370,000; Existing Plan at 0% growth £53,497.38, 2.94% growth £134,612.81, 5.88% growth £330,027.33. Research documents [P17] and [P18] provide risk-7 fund research and performance comparisons. Performance data [P19] and [P20] show True Potential existing holdings with 5-year returns and proposed Aviva fund performance. No evidence appears regarding alternatives 1-4 (do nothing, in-situ fund switch, consolidate into existing arrangement, switch to employer scheme): these are listed as considered but no rejection analysis, comparison data, or supporting documentation is provided. No evidence documents why these four alternatives were rejected or shows any analysis comparing them against the recommendation. The SAP documents and research materials support the Aviva recommendation on cost and projection grounds but do not address the four non-Stakeholder alternatives explicitly considered.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states alternatives 1-4 were considered but provides no rejection reasons; file contains no analysis, comparison tables, or supporting evidence for these alternatives.
      - Report asserts Stakeholder Pensions rejected on cost grounds (1.5%/1% vs 0.39%); no passage in the file corroborates this specific charge comparison or confirms that Stakeholder Pensions carry maximum charges of 1.5% and 1%.
      - Report states investment pathways underperformed the recommended strategy; file contains performance data [P22] and [P23] showing proposed plan performance but no explicit comparison chart or table quantifying the performance gap claimed.
      - Report asserts charge savings of £208 per annum by moving from Quilter (0.27%) to Aviva (0.19%); derived figures confirm £208 savings on Quilter's £260,000 value, but evidence documents show Aviva net charge as 0.17% (after 0.08% reduction from 0.25% AMC) or 0.3854% (with fund charges), creating ambiguity about which charge baseline supports the £208 claim.
    The requirement asks whether alternatives provide better outcomes and whether the report's reasoning supports discounting them. The evidence reveals a two-tier problem. First, alternatives 1-4 are listed as considered with empty rejection reason arrays in the canonical model. The report narrative provides no substantive analysis of why these options were rejected — they are asserted as considered but not explained. This is material because, for example, alternative 4 (employer scheme) raises a compliance issue under COBS and the severity guidance flags this: employer contributions lost on a transfer from a live workplace scheme must be addressed. No evidence documents whether the client has an active employer scheme, whether employer contributions exist, or why transferring out would be appropriate. Second, for Stakeholder Pensions, the report states they carry maximum charges of 1.5% for 10 years then 1% thereafter. This is stated as fact but no industry source, FCA guidance, or provider document in the file corroborates it. The charge comparison (1.5%/1% vs 0.39%) is therefore unsupported. Third, for investment pathways, the report asserts underperformance but the file contains no ranked list of pathways evaluated, no selection of specific provider pathways, and no performance table isolating the claimed gap. The performance data in [P22] and [P23] show monthly cumulative returns for the proposed plan (weighted average 15.94% at end of February 2026) but not for a specific pathway alternative. Fourth, the charge arithmetic shows internal inconsistency: the report claims £208 savings by moving from Quilter 0.27% to Aviva 0.19%, consistent with derived figure at Quilter's £260,000 value. However, SAP evidence [P5] states Aviva AMC is 0.25% with a 0.08% reduction (net 0.17%), plus fund charges of 0.2154%, totalling 0.3854%. This does not match the stated 0.19% saving claim. The Guard on consolidation notes that consolidation for its own sake is valid if the client requested it. CHK-002 objectives would clarify whether simplification was a stated goal. The client objectives in the report (retire at 60, income £25k–£30k, flexible income over guaranteed) do not explicitly mention consolidation. No evidence shows the client asked for it. The recommendation to consolidate three schemes into one Aviva plan appears driven by cost savings (£208 per annum on Quilter alone) and adviser ability to manage, not by a demonstrated client preference for simplification. The Guard on rankings states: where research ranks an option above the recommendation, name both and give maturity values; silence about ranking is itself a finding. No evidence provided ranks Aviva above, below, or against any alternative. No research summary, heat map, or adviser rationale document explains why Aviva Pension Portfolio (Choice) was selected over other platforms, other new plans, or over consolidating into an existing scheme the client already holds. The vulnerability overlay notes the extraction found client may have 'flexibility' and 'income' preferences stated but not fully explained. The interaction between the stated preference for flexible income over guaranteed income and the choice of a Growth 7 risk profile (which emphasises capital appreciation) is noted as potentially incompatible in the extraction. For alternatives analysis, this means no evidence considers whether alternatives such as staying with existing schemes and switching funds in place might better serve the flexibility objective without consolidation costs or the retirement income mismatch implied by a high-risk portfolio and modest income target. In summary: (1) Four of five alternatives have no documented rejection analysis. (2) Stakeholder Pension charge comparison unsupported. (3) Investment pathway underperformance claim unsupported by specific comparisons. (4) Charge arithmetic internal inconsistency weakens cost-savings justification. (5) No evidence of client request for consolidation. (6) No ranked comparison of platforms or plans. (7) Vulnerability factors (flexibility objective, income target, risk profile interaction) not explicitly addressed in alternatives analysis.

  [G9.8] Evidence supporting claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report claims higher costs are justified by additional benefits in two contexts: (1) For Quilter, it asserts a cost saving of £208 per annum (0.27% vs 0.19% platform charges) with no higher costs claimed. (2) For Nest, it states annual charges are higher at 0.39% compared to 0.30% (an increase of £19.15 per annum), justified by 'better risk alignment, wider fund range, more flexible retirement options and adviser servicing'. (3) For True Potential, it claims charges are higher at 0.39% vs 0.98%, representing a cost saving of £275.37 per annum (when excluding ongoing advice charges), justified by 'lower platform and fund charges' and adviser benefits. The report's netBenefitConclusion states that although annual charges for the recommended Aviva plan are higher at 0.39% compared to Nest's 0.30%, this 'represents a relatively small increase of £19.15 per annum, justified by better risk alignment, wider fund range, more flexible retirement options and adviser servicing'.
    File:     The case file provides SAP (Suitability Analysis Product) documents [P1, P3, P5, P7] showing projected fund values under different growth scenarios and charge comparisons. These confirm the existence of the existing plans (Quilter, True Potential, Nest) and the recommended Aviva option. The file contains Aviva product literature [P9, P10, P11, P12] describing the Aviva Platform features including fund choice, flexibility, and online tools. The suitability report [P13, P16, P17] includes charge tables and fund recommendations. However, the file does not contain independent evidence (category E or G documents from product providers other than the adviser's own analysis) that corroborates specific claimed benefits such as: (1) whether the 'wider range of investment options' on Aviva is material to the client's needs; (2) whether Nest's current fund is genuinely 'not in line with agreed risk level of 7'; (3) independent confirmation of the flexibility or service quality differences claimed; (4) whether the recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio C and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity) actually deliver 'better risk alignment' than the existing arrangements.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims Quilter platform charges of 0.27% with annual saving of £208; the charge comparison appears internally consistent but the £208 saving calculation implies a fund value of £77,037, which does not match Quilter's actual stated value of £260,000, suggesting the £208 figure may apply only to part of the portfolio or be based on a different valuation.
      - Report states Nest annual charges are 0.39% for recommended plan vs 0.30% for existing, an increase of £19.15 per annum. The extracted ambiguity notes the Nest existing charges are listed as 0.30% plan charges plus 1.8% contribution charge; the switching analysis uses only 0.30%, excluding the 1.8% contribution charge. This omission means the true cost comparison understates the existing plan's ongoing costs if contributions will continue.
      - Report claims True Potential existing charges of 0.98% in replacement analysis section; extracted data notes existing arrangements show True Potential with platform charges 0.40%, ongoing advice charge 0.50%, fund charges 0.58%, totalling 1.48%. The 0.98% figure excludes the ongoing advice charge of 0.50%, yet the report later acknowledges existing advice charges on True Potential are included in Critical Yield analysis [P17], creating inconsistency in which charges are 'recurring' for comparison purposes.
      - Report justifies Nest switch by claiming the recommended plan offers 'better risk alignment' because current fund 'is not in line with the agreed risk level of 7'; however, no evidence is provided showing what fund Nest currently holds or why it fails to align with risk level 7. The file contains no Nest fund breakdown or risk rating for the existing holding.
      - Report claims benefits include 'ability to actively monitor and adjust investments as required' and 'adviser will have the ability to actively monitor' — stated twice as separate benefits — yet this is a service characteristic of the adviser-client relationship rather than a property of the recommended scheme itself, and no evidence distinguishes this from what the adviser could have done within the existing arrangements.
      - Report states 'new scheme offers access to wider range of investment options'; Aviva literature [P15] mentions 'Over 6,900 funds' but does not state how many funds were available within the existing arrangements (Quilter, Nest, True Potential), making the materiality of the 'wider range' claim impossible to assess.
      - Report claims more 'flexible retirement options' on Aviva; the Aviva KFD [P9] and suitability report [P18] both discuss flexi-access drawdown, but the file does not evidence that the existing schemes do not offer flexi-access drawdown or that this represents a material advantage for this client's objectives.
    The requirement tests whether claimed benefits justify higher costs. For the Quilter switch, no higher costs are claimed; the report asserts a cost saving of £208 annually, corroborated by the charge differential stated (0.27% vs 0.19%). This does not trigger the requirement. For the Nest switch, the report claims higher charges (0.39% vs 0.30%, an increase of £19.15 per annum) justified by four benefit categories: risk alignment, fund range, retirement options, and adviser servicing. For the True Potential switch, the report initially presents higher charges (0.39% vs 0.98%) but then reframes this as a cost saving when excluding the existing ongoing advice charge (£275.37 annual saving). The extracted internal inconsistencies note that Nest's existing charges are stated as 0.30% + 1.8% contribution charge in the existing arrangements table but analysed as 0.30% only in the switching assessment, and True Potential's existing charges shift from 1.48% to 0.98% depending on whether the ongoing advice charge is included. These arithmetic inconsistencies undermine the reliability of the cost-benefit comparison. The claimed benefits (better risk alignment, wider funds, flexible retirement, adviser servicing) are stated but not substantiated by the case file. No document provides evidence that Nest's current fund was misaligned with risk level 7, no document quantifies or names the fund count differences, and no document compares the retirement flexibility options available under each scheme. The adviser's ability to monitor investments is presented as a benefit of the new scheme but is a function of the adviser-client engagement, not the scheme itself, and could apply equally within the existing arrangements. The Critical Yield analysis [P17] shows that when adviser fees are included, the Aviva plan is 'more expensive' than True Potential (can underperform by only 0.01% per annum) and versus Nest (must outperform by 1.23% per annum to age 89). This contradicts the presentation of the switch as justified by superior benefits; the new scheme's outperformance is contingent and uncertain, not a demonstrated superiority. The SAP documents [P1, P3, P5, P7] show projected fund values but do not evaluate whether the claimed benefits are real or material to this client's stated objectives (retire at 60 with £25,000–£30,000 annual income, preference for flexible income). A guard applies: 'Where the recommended plan is cheaper, this requirement does not bite.' For Quilter, the plan is cheaper. For True Potential (when excluding ongoing advice charges as the report does), the plan is cheaper. Only for Nest does the recommended plan carry higher charges; and for Nest, the justifying benefits lack supporting evidence from the case file. The claim that the current Nest fund is not aligned with risk level 7 is particularly material: if unsupported, the entire rationale for that switch fails. The report does not cite any assessment of Nest's risk rating or fund composition.

  [G9.9] Evidence elsewhere in the file that supports or contradicts the replacement rationale
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts three replacement recommendations: (1) Quilter to Aviva: lower cost structure (0.19% vs 0.27%, saving £208 pa), client preference to switch, ongoing monitoring benefit; (2) Nest to Aviva: charges justify switch (0.39% vs 0.30%, increase of £19.15 pa offset by better risk alignment, wider fund range, flexible retirement options, adviser servicing); (3) True Potential to Aviva: platform charges 0.21% vs 0.40% (saves £88.68 pa), fund charges 0.20% vs 0.58% (saves £177.35 pa), total can underperform by £275.37 pa, wider fund range, risk alignment.     The requirement tests whether the replacement rationale is supported by evidence and consistent with what the case file holds. The check applies because the trigger is satisfied (hasReplacementOrSwitch = true). The report makes three distinct replacement recommendations, each with stated rationale. A comparison can only be performed partially because critical inputs are absent. The 'expectedButAbsent' list confirms that safeguarded benefits analysis and guaranteed amounts are not present in the report, preventing full assessment of what is lost. The charge discrepancies reveal internal inconsistency in the canonical model: True Potential is stated at 0.98% in the replacement analysis section but existing arrangements shows 0.40% + 0.50% + 0.58% = 1.48% (three components). The report does not clarify whether the 0.50% ongoing advice charge is excluded or included in different sections. For Nest, the report frames a switch to Aviva as justified by 'relatively small' £19.15 increase, but SAP evidence [P13] shows Aviva outperforming the existing plan significantly at 2.94% growth (£151,000 vs £134,612.81), which contradicts the framing of incremental cost increase as the trade-off. The Critical Yield assertion (1.23% outperformance needed) lacks corresponding support in the SAP documents provided; the passages show projections but not the critical yield calculation itself. Meeting summaries [P2], [P4], [P8] do not record client statement of preference to switch from Quilter; the report asserts 'no ties or preference to leave funds with Quilter' but this is not evidenced in the meeting notes. Aviva KFD [P20] warns of loss of valuable benefits in older defined-benefit schemes, but none of the three existing arrangements are identified as defined-benefit, so this guard does not resolve the gap. The file contains charge comparison analyses (SAP documents) but they do not map cleanly to the specific rationales stated in the report because the charge bases differ (some exclude adviser fees, some do not) and the comparative framing varies. Without evidence of what safeguarded benefits, guarantees, or employer contributions each arrangement offers or loses, the completeness of the replacement analysis cannot be confirmed. The report does not document explicit consideration of retaining funds and switching investments within existing providers as an alternative for each plan. The vulnerability overlay notes ambiguity about client preference for 'flexible income over guaranteed income' and does not elaborate whether annuitisation was rejected; the objectives file shows OBJ5 prefers flexible income, but no analysis addresses whether the switch to Aviva versus retention of existing arrangements (some of which may offer annuitisation or guarantees) was weighed against this preference.

The report's central claims about the switch rationale rest on assertions not corroborated by provider documentation in the evidence pack: (1) Exit penalties and switching costs are stated as zero but no provider confirmations appear. CHK-010 — Vulnerability, Support Needs and Foreseeable Harm Overlay
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 5 requirement(s). 5 raised a concern (G10.1, G10.2, G10.3, G10.4, G10.5). The report states 'you intend to appoint them as your Power of attorney' but the canonical model records isPowerOfAttorneyGranted as false, indicating no actual Power of Attorney is currently in place—the intention is stated but not yet executed. The report asserts support needs related to post-divorce complexity and ongoing monitoring, but the file contains no formal vulnerability assessment, support plan, or documented evidence of how these specific needs were identified, evaluated or tailored to the client beyond the general fact of divorce. The report identifies the client as maintaining informal support from partner Tony with separate financial arrangements, but the file contains no assessment of whether this arrangement provides any meaningful support for vulnerability, decision-making capacity, or comprehension. 4 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G10.1] Details of additional support and needs, including third-party support
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts two support needs: (1) 'Financial advice to navigate complexities of retirement planning post-divorce' (stated as actual, from page 3), and (2) 'Ongoing monitoring to ensure pension funds remain aligned with changing needs and market conditions' (stated as recommended, from page 17). For third-party support, the report states the client 'lives with your partner Tony but maintain separate financial arrangements' (stated as actual, informal relationship). The report also states 'you intend to appoint them [two adult daughters] as your Power of attorney' (page 6). The canonical model records isPowerOfAttorneyGranted as false.
    File:     The case file contains multiple passages confirming the client's post-divorce circumstances and support arrangements. P6 confirms: 'You live with your partner Tony but maintain separate financial arrangements' and 'you intend to appoint them [daughters] as your Power of attorney'. P7 records the client is 'currently in the process of managing your financial affairs following your recent divorce' and notes 'The relationship between you and your ex-husband, Paul Annis, remains amicable'. P17 confirms the client is 'a seasoned professional with a background in accounting and currency trading' and 'recognise[s] the value of professional advice, particularly in managing your retirement funds'. P4 and P10 document the Client Agreement which sets out the adviser's services including 'periodic on-going reviews of your existing and/or any new investments' and provisions allowing 'a third party to provide instructions on your behalf by providing a valid Power of Attorney'. P3 documents consent to processing sensitive personal data. P12 records risk profile discussions. However, no document in the file records a completed Power of Attorney, a formal appointment of the daughters, or specific vulnerability assessments beyond the divorce circumstance itself. The file does not record any health issues, capability concerns, literacy difficulties, communication accessibility needs, or other vulnerability indicators beyond the life event of divorce.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states 'you intend to appoint them as your Power of attorney' but the canonical model records isPowerOfAttorneyGranted as false, indicating no actual Power of Attorney is currently in place—the intention is stated but not yet executed
      - The report asserts support needs related to post-divorce complexity and ongoing monitoring, but the file contains no formal vulnerability assessment, support plan, or documented evidence of how these specific needs were identified, evaluated or tailored to the client beyond the general fact of divorce
      - The report identifies the client as maintaining informal support from partner Tony with separate financial arrangements, but the file contains no assessment of whether this arrangement provides any meaningful support for vulnerability, decision-making capacity, or comprehension
      - No passage in the file records any assessment of the client's capacity to understand financial matters beyond the general statement in P1 and P17 that she has 'accounting and currency trading' background and 'fair degree of knowledge', which is presented as a strength, not a vulnerability requiring support
    The report correctly records the factual circumstance that the client lives with partner Tony but maintains separate financial arrangements—this is corroborated by P6. The reference to intending to appoint daughters as Power of Attorney is also corroborated by P6, but the canonical model's recording of isPowerOfAttorneyGranted=false is accurate: no actual Power of Attorney is currently in place, only an intention. This distinction matters under the guard: 'A recommendation to put a power of attorney in place is future planning, not evidence of current third-party support.' The report mentions support needs for navigating post-divorce complexity and ongoing monitoring. These are stated in the report but the file provides no independent assessment document that identifies these as specific vulnerability-driven support needs. The client's background in accounting and currency trading (P1, P17) and self-described 'fair degree of knowledge' are presented as personal strengths that support her capacity to understand financial matters. No document records any communication accessibility adjustments, format preferences, language support, or literacy concerns. No passage identifies health, mental capacity, resilience, life event impact, or other vulnerability drivers beyond the divorce itself. The meeting summaries (P7, P17) note the client's proactive engagement and willingness to take professional advice, which is documented but does not constitute a recorded support need or vulnerability assessment. The file does not record whether the support needs stated in the report were derived from a formal assessment, client request, or adviser determination. The ongoing monitoring recommendation appears to be standard adviser practice (documented in P4 as 'periodic on-going reviews') rather than a client-specific support need. The comparison can be performed because both sides address the same domain (support needs, third-party involvement, Power of Attorney status), but the file lacks the supporting evidence—a formal vulnerability or needs assessment—that would justify the report's framing of these as specific support arrangements rather than standard advice practice.

  [G10.2] Communication preferences and formats
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report contains no explicit statement of communication preferences or formats. The canonical model path /vulnerability/perClient[]/communicationPreferences is absent, indicating the report does not extract or assert specific communication preferences for this client.
    File:     The case file contains multiple references to communication arrangements. [P7] states: 'We will liaise with you in a variety of ways, including remote video or telephone meetings, letters, emails, through online communication portals and newsletters. You may at any time choose to change your preferences as to how we communicate with you.' This is a general statement in the Client Agreement of available communication methods. [P7] also states: 'Should you require us to make a reasonable adjustment in providing the advisory services to you, please speak to your financial adviser. Examples of reasonable adjustments may be the provision of a large print format pdf or documents provided in braille.' [P1] and [P16] record that meetings were conducted by telephone and via Teams respectively. [P19] confirms 'The meeting will be conducted via Microsoft Teams to facilitate document sharing.' [P23] records client contact details as email (mel.anness@hotmail.com) and phone (07866312561). No specific communication preferences, accessibility requirements, language needs, or literacy considerations are documented in the file for this client.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report makes no assertion about communication preferences, so no direct contradiction exists. However, the case file contains general communication methodology statements ([P7]) but no record of this specific client's stated preferences, adjustments requested, or accessibility needs beyond the contact details recorded.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Specific communication preferences recorded for this client, Any accessibility requirements or reasonable adjustments requested by the client, Any language or literacy considerations discussed with the client, Any third-party communication arrangements or trusted contact preferences
    The comparison cannot be completed because the report does not assert communication preferences and the case file does not record client-specific preferences or adjustments. [P7] describes the firm's available communication methods and the client's right to choose, but no evidence exists that Melanie Anness was asked to state her preferences, or that any specific preferences were recorded and documented. The meetings were conducted by telephone and Teams ([P1], [P16], [P19]), but this reflects the adviser's choice of method, not the client's stated preference. Contact details are recorded ([P23]), but contact details alone are not a communication preference. The Client Agreement ([P7]) makes clear that clients may request reasonable adjustments, but no record exists that this client was asked about such needs or that any were identified. Under FG21/1 and PRIN 2A.2.1R, the adviser should have obtained and recorded the client's communication preferences and any support needs. The absence of both a report assertion and case file documentation means the requirement is not met, but this is a process gap rather than an inconsistency between two documented positions.

  [G10.3] Adjustments made to support client comprehension
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts three adjustments made to support client comprehension: (1) 'Recommended ongoing advice service at 1% per annum to provide regular reviews and adjustments' [I, p.17]; (2) 'Emphasis on the importance of seeking advice before drawing benefits to ensure tax-efficient withdrawal' [I, p.12]; (3) 'Recommended Power of Attorney registration to ensure support for future incapacity' [I, p.18]. The report also records four vulnerability characteristics: recent divorce with Pension Sharing Order [I, pp.2–3], recent employment status change from employed to self-employed [I, p.3], loss of employee benefits and pension contributions [I, p.3], and pending inheritance involving legal proceedings [I, p.3].
    File:     The case file contains extensive evidence of the client's circumstances and meetings with advisers. Meeting notes confirm recent divorce (Certified Final Order dated 12 November 2025) [P17], the Pension Sharing Order process [P20, P21], recent employment transition from employed to self-employed [P8], and pending inheritance of approximately £150,000 from mother's estate complicated by legal challenges [P7]. The file documents discussions about tax efficiency and personal allowance utilisation [P7], inheritance and legal challenges [P7], and future employment plans [P7]. However, the case file contains no evidence of: (1) a recommendation or discussion of ongoing advice service at 1% per annum specifically as an adjustment to support client comprehension; (2) any formal Power of Attorney assessment or recommendation; (3) any documented discussion of comprehension support measures, communication adjustments, or accessibility accommodations. Meeting notes record discussions of pension options, tax efficiency, and financial planning, but do not explicitly frame these as adjustments made to support the client's understanding or comprehension of complex matters.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report recommends 'ongoing advice service at 1% per annum to provide regular reviews and adjustments' as an adjustment to support client comprehension, but the case file contains no evidence that this recommendation was framed as, or discussed as, a comprehension support measure. The file does document that ongoing advice is mentioned in the report context [P3 references advice service discussions], but not as a specific adjustment tailored to support the client's understanding.
      - The report recommends 'Power of Attorney registration to ensure support for future incapacity' but no evidence appears in the case file of a Power of Attorney assessment, discussion, or recommendation. While P22 mentions that protection strategies and safeguarding assets are important considerations post-divorce, there is no specific documented discussion or recommendation regarding Power of Attorney.
      - The report emphasises 'seeking advice before drawing benefits to ensure tax-efficient withdrawal' but while the case file extensively documents tax efficiency discussions [P7], these discussions are framed as financial strategy and tax planning, not explicitly as adjustments made to support client comprehension of pension withdrawal mechanics.
    The canonical model extracts three adjustments claimed by the report to support client comprehension. The first, ongoing advice service at 1% per annum, is mentioned in the report itself [I, p.17] but the case file does not establish that this was positioned or designed as a comprehension support measure. The report's own phrasing ('Recommended ongoing advice service... to provide regular reviews and adjustments') could support ongoing portfolio management rather than support for understanding. The second adjustment, Power of Attorney recommendation, appears only in the report [I, p.18: 'It would also be prudent to set up and register Power of Attorney...'] and is absent from all case file meetings and correspondence. The third, emphasis on seeking advice for tax efficiency, is amply documented in the file [P7: 'Morgan and Melanie discussed strategies for tax efficiency'] but is framed as tax planning strategy, not as a comprehension support measure. The vulnerability characteristics are well supported: divorce is documented [P17, P20, P21], employment transition is confirmed [P8, P6, P7], loss of benefits is noted [P8], and inheritance challenges are extensively detailed [P7]. The client's background in accounting and currency trading is also documented [P8, P9]. However, the requirement [G10.3] specifically asks whether adjustments claimed by the report 'to support client comprehension' are supported by evidence. The evidence shows financial planning and advice delivery, but does not establish that these were designed as or presented to the client as comprehension support adjustments. The guard on cancellation rights does not apply here: these are claimed as client-specific adjustments, not standard regulatory features. The comparison can be performed because both the assertion and the evidence are available; they simply do not align on the framing or existence of comprehension support measures.

  [G10.4] Vulnerability has a material impact on the recommendation (overlay)
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that vulnerability and support needs have been identified and incorporated into recommendations. Specifically: (1) The client intends to appoint adult daughters as Power of Attorney as part of estate planning, linked to objective OBJ8. (2) Detailed Inheritance Tax planning advice has been provided given upcoming inheritance will trigger IHT liability, linked to objectives OBJ6 and OBJ7. (3) Client's capacity for loss is assessed as 'moderate' based on financial circumstances. (4) Client has liquid cash holdings sufficient for immediate access needs. (5) Client is comfortable with 10-15 working day fund access timelines. (6) Stress testing at 6% inflation and 2007/8-style market crash scenarios show assets could last to age 81 and demonstrate resilience respectively. (7) Risk profile agreed as 7/10 (Growth) following discussion of natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss, and required return. (8) Client background in accounting and currency trading noted as equipping solid understanding of financial matters.
    File:     The case file contains multiple vulnerability-relevant assessments: (1) Client is aged 55, recently divorced, recently changed employment status from employed to self-employed, expecting £45,000 gross annual earnings, will lose employee pension contributions [P6]. (2) Client lives with partner Tony but maintains separate financial arrangements; two adult daughters identified as integral part of estate planning [P6]. (3) Risk profile assessment documented in Risk profile report dated 16 March 2026 confirming risk level 7 following interactive discussion of natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return [P1, P9]. (4) Meeting summaries from 16/01/2026, 20/02/2026, and 18/03/2026 document multiple fact-find and advisory meetings; risk profile discussed and agreed as level 7 [P12, P21, P24]. (5) Client has background in accounting and currency trading; characterized as seasoned professional with solid understanding of financial matters [P21]. (6) Client's objectives include consolidating pension pots and exploring tax-efficient strategies; interested in flexi-access drawdown and annuities [P22]. (7) IHT summary in report notes current assets below £500,000 allowance (no IHT liability currently), but inheritance will take client over limit; strategies listed include trusts, gifts & lifetime transfers, whole of life plans [P5]. (8) Client confirmed comfort with risk level associated with investment strategy and comfort with 10-15 working day access timeline [P17, P24]. (9) No formal vulnerability assessment document, no recorded assessment of support needs, communication adjustments, or specific vulnerability indicators (health, mental capacity, cognitive impairment, dependency, isolation) appears in the file.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts vulnerability indicators exist (checkTriggers.hasVulnerabilityIndicators = true) but no formal vulnerability assessment document is present in the case file
      - Report states 'support needs' and 'adjustments' are reflected in recommendations but the specific support needs and adjustments are not detailed or evidenced
      - Report identifies 'adult daughters as Power of Attorney' as a recommendation but the file shows this is stated as something 'you intend to appoint them as' rather than a recommendation made by the adviser [P6]
      - Report lists multiple 'foreseeable harms' with mitigations but none of these harms are identified or assessed using formal vulnerability or harm assessment methodology in the supporting documents
      - Report's framing of capacity for loss as 'moderate' and conclusions about resilience are based on cashflow analysis and financial position rather than on any formal assessment of vulnerability, capability, or support needs in the sense required by PRIN 2A.2.1R and FG21/1
    The check applies because hasVulnerabilityIndicators = true in the canonical model. However, the evidence reveals a material gap between what the report asserts and what the file contains. The report's canonical model lists two 'impactOnRecommendation' entries relating to estate protection (Power of Attorney) and tax planning (IHT), and six 'foreseeableHarms' entries. These are presented as findings from a vulnerability analysis, but no formal vulnerability assessment appears anywhere in the case file. What the file does contain is: (1) standard risk profiling and capacity for loss assessment [P1, P9, P16]; (2) fact-find meetings documenting client circumstances [P12, P21]; (3) IHT planning discussion [P5, P6]; (4) confirmation of client comfort with risk and access timelines [P17, P24]. The risk profile assessment confirms the client's adventurous attitude, moderate capacity for loss, and background in accounting and currency trading — all of which point to a client with reasonable financial understanding and resilience. The IHT planning is triggered by the anticipated inheritance, not by a vulnerability indicator. The Power of Attorney point is framed in the report as something the client 'intends to appoint' [P6] — it is estate planning intent, not a vulnerability-driven support need identified by the adviser. No assessment under PRIN 2A.5 (firm's duty regarding vulnerable customers) or FG21/1 (vulnerability assessment, communication, adjustments) has been documented. The firm has not recorded what specific vulnerability characteristics, if any, have been identified; what support needs exist; what adjustments to communication or process have been made; or why the client is flagged as carrying vulnerability indicators when the evidence suggests a financially literate, resilient client managing a standard divorce and pension consolidation matter. The report's 'foreseeableHarms' appear to be standard pension switching risks, not harms specific to vulnerability. The absence of any formal vulnerability assessment in a case flagged as hasVulnerabilityIndicators = true is the core finding: either the flag is incorrect, or a required assessment has not been performed or recorded. Per the decision logic, where vulnerability is flagged but no assessment appears anywhere in the case, that is a material concern.
