  [G10.5] Vulnerabilities recorded in the file and in the report
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts hasVulnerability = 'Possible' with categories 'LifeEvents' and 'Capability'. Four characteristics are recorded: (1) Recently divorced, resulting in Pension Sharing Order, stated in report pages 2-3, dated 2026-03-24; (2) Recently changed employment status from employed to self-employed with same company, stated in report page 3; (3) Loss of employee benefits and pension contributions as result of self-employment transition, stated in report page 3; (4) Pending inheritance involving legal proceedings (£350,000 from sale of mother's property), stated in report page 3. Assessment date: 2026-03-24.
    File:     The case file contains multiple meeting summaries and fact find documents corroborating key vulnerabilities. [P7] Meeting Summary with Jack Davidson (16/01/2026) states 'you are currently in the process of managing your financial affairs following your recent divorce' and references 'Pension Sharing Order'. [P2] Meeting Summary with Jack (20/02/2026) confirms recent divorce context and ongoing financial situation post-divorce. [P9] Meeting Summary (21/07/2026) discusses Melanie 'recently departure from employment' and references her 'annual income was approximately £45,000', transitioning from employed status. [P10] references pending inheritance: 'Melanie is anticipating an inheritance of approximately £150,000 from her mother's estate, which is currently complicated by legal challenges involving her mother's ex-partner.' [P13] confirms liabilities and financial management post-divorce. [P2] describes Melanie as 'a seasoned professional with a background in accounting and currency trading' indicating capability and financial acumen. No formal vulnerability assessment document appears in the file; vulnerability characteristics are scattered across meeting notes rather than consolidated in a dedicated assessment.
    Discrepancies:
      - Inheritance value discrepancy: Report states £350,000; file evidence [P10] states approximately £150,000. These differ materially by £200,000.
      - Inheritance status: Report describes as 'pending legal proceedings'; file [P10] describes as 'complicated by legal challenges involving her mother's ex-partner' with siblings potentially contributing £20,000 each to resolve, suggesting a dispute rather than simple legal conveyance.
      - Employment transition timing: Report states 'recently changed' but [P9] indicates meeting on 21/07/2026 discusses 'recent departure from employment', suggesting the transition may have occurred around or before mid-2026, while other meetings earlier in the year (16/01/2026, 20/02/2026) frame the primary concern as divorce-related pension transfer rather than employment change as a current event.
      - Formal vulnerability assessment: No standalone vulnerability assessment document dated 2026-03-24 or any date appears in the retrieved passages. The characteristics are evident in meeting summaries but are not consolidated in a formal assessment tool, risk questionnaire, or documented vulnerability screening process.
      - Client capability characterization: Report categorizes vulnerability as including 'Capability' but [P2] describes client as having 'solid understanding of financial matters', 'background in accounting and currency trading', and explicitly 'seasoned professional'. This does not align with a capability vulnerability indicator.
    The report asserts four vulnerability characteristics, three of which are clearly supported by file evidence: recent divorce with Pension Sharing Order ([P7], [P2]), employment transition from employed to self-employed ([P9]), and loss of employee benefits ([P9] context). However, three material issues arise. First, the inheritance value is stated as £350,000 in the report but [P10] records approximately £150,000—a £200,000 discrepancy that cannot be reconciled by rounding or calculation error and materially affects the asset and cash flow assessment. Second, the report categorizes a 'Capability' vulnerability when the file evidence ([P2]) explicitly describes Melanie as a seasoned professional with accounting and currency trading background and solid financial understanding, suggesting high rather than low capability. This appears inconsistent with FCA FG21/1 capability drivers, which identify low capability as a vulnerability factor. The 'Capability' category appears misapplied unless it refers to a domain-specific or transitional capability gap not explained in the file. Third, no formal vulnerability assessment appears in the case file. The characteristics are evidenced in meeting notes but are scattered across multiple conversations (16/01/2026, 20/02/2026, 21/07/2026) rather than consolidated in a single assessment dated 2026-03-24. The report's assessment date of 2026-03-24 corresponds to the date of recommendation but may not be the date of vulnerability assessment; the earliest documented vulnerability-relevant meeting is 16/01/2026. The employment transition is contextualized in [P9] (21/07/2026) as a very recent departure, suggesting this was not yet a factor in earlier meetings. The guards note that a resolved or historic vulnerability may still be correctly handled and that a resolved vulnerability is recorded but may no longer apply; however, the employment transition appears to be ongoing at the time of the 21/07/2026 meeting and is treated in the report as a current characteristic. The report's handling of 'Capability' as a vulnerability category is not supported by the evidence and appears to contradict client capability indicators present in the file.
